**Verifier note.** The verdict \"high\" stands for a Solution-tab explainer, not a Problem-tab one. The lane/deadline/token form is the right way to show the two things prose cannot: the old generation abandoned while the new one runs, and the deadline reset that keeps a healthy ward at generation 1. The README states the four requirements (29-healing-goroutines/README.md:34-48) but not that timeline; the tests only count generations and compare channel identities; the solution doc explains it in prose, which is why a visual companion is worth building for a hard exercise. But the analyst's \"problem-inline\" placement is the wrong host: the browser already has \"Run main\", and main.go prints the flatline. If the owner will not gate the explainer behind the solution reveal, downgrade to \"nice\", because the Problem-tab version mostly restates the README and the run output.

Answer to the originating question: incorporate the visualisation into the existing Solution tab, after the spoiler reveal, not a third tab and not the Problem tab. The interactive explainer is web-only; the clone/GitHub mode gets the static ASCII diagram in the exercise README, which the readme-diagram lint rule already requires and which clears the 29 exception. Feasible with changes: no new npm dependency, SVG + CSS variables + React state; the work is in scenario accuracy, the 288px layout, the lazy registry, and the accessibility/motion path. Effort \"M\" is optimistic if all four scenes and the broken variants are built; budget L.

**Risks.**
- Web only. The site reads README.md and solution docs from git HEAD as opaque markdown, and the clone has no renderer, so an inline component exists only in the browser. To keep the two modes in agreement, add a static ASCII diagram to 29-healing-goroutines/README.md as well - that also clears the known exception `readme-diagram 29-healing-goroutines` at tools/check/lint.go:49, and the lint layout rule forbids putting any visualisation file in the exercise folder.
- Spoiler margin is thin: the README already lists the four required behaviours in bullets, so the visualisation must add the timeline rather than restate them. Keep the healed scene behind a 'heal it' control and use the scaffold flatline as the default frame.
- The default scene is the pass-through, but the visualisation must not draw a fixed select loop, a timeout case, or a second lane before the learner asks for the heal.
- MockWard's wedge is a bare return (mockward.go:99): the goroutine exists, the channel stays open and silent. A real ward would hang. Do not animate a channel close or a clean exit as the wedge - the whole point is that nothing observable happens. Label the difference.
- The solution forwards pulses with a blocking send, so a stalled consumer pauses the deadline. A visualisation that always shows a fast reader hides a real bug class; either expose the slow-reader variant or do not imply detection is instant.
- At the restart instant the old generation and the new one can appear simultaneous. The invariant is close old first, never await it; if the renderer shows the new lane before the close marker, it teaches a race the solution deliberately avoids.
- 320px width: lane labels, tokens, a deadline bar and a Dones readout are tight. Put lane labels above the lane, keep labels to a few words, and never require horizontal scrolling or hover.
- The scenes must be declarative data replayable by step, not timers driven by wall-clock deltas; otherwise step, reduced motion and screenshots diverge from the deterministic contract.
- No npm dependency is needed: SVG or absolutely positioned CSS chips plus React state suffice. Adding an animation library would be hard to justify against the site's four-package baseline.

**Why this tier.** A wedged ward and a steward that restarts it; the old generation keeps pulsing below the new one.


## Tier C — build later, only if the player earns its keep

<a id="00-limit-crawler"></a>
#### 0 — Limit your Crawler
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Shared ticker channel as a system-wide rate-limit gate (one channel receive per interval = one Fetch per interval), with the goroutine fan-out kept intact.

**Core idea.** Throttling does not undo concurrency. Every `go Crawl(...)` goroutine still runs, but each must win one receive from a single shared channel before it may call `fetcher.Fetch`. A tick is a ticket, not a broadcast: the runtime hands each tick to exactly one waiter, so 13 racing goroutines become 13 fetches one second apart.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The learner cannot see the waiter pile-up. In the prose they read "one shared gate" and in the tests they read a 950 ms check, but neither shows that all the crawl goroutines block on the same `<-throttle` receive, that exactly one is unblocked per tick, or that the crawl's total run time jumps from about 5 ms to about 13 s. They also cannot see that an error URL such as `http://golang.org/cmd/` still consumes a slot, because `Fetch` fires the test signal before it looks the URL up.

**Design.** A narrow vertical scene, 320 px wide. On the left, a time axis that can compress: the whole scaffold burst fits in one instant, the fixed run spans 13 seconds. In the middle, a column of goroutine lanes, one per `Crawl` call, labelled with a shortened URL and its depth (`golang.org/ · d4`). To their right, a single shared gate rail, drawn as one vertical channel named `throttle`. A ticker drops a small ticket onto the rail each second. Each lane's bar reaches the rail and turns solid while it waits; when a ticket lands, exactly one bar snaps through, flashes a Fetch marker, and the rest stay solid. Small branches grow from the lane that just fetched to its children lanes. In the scaffold scene, 13 bars reach the rail at the same instant and all pass, and the test lane at the top turns red. In the fixed scene, the same 13 lanes arrive almost together but only one crosses per second, and the clock climbs to 13 s. The scene must never claim a fixed order: the crossing lane is chosen by the script, and a caption says "any one of these; Go does not promise which".

**Scenes.**
1. **Burst (default)** — The scaffold. The fan-out tree grows from `golang.org/` depth 4; 13 lanes reach Fetch at t≈0. A red test lane at the top reports two fetches less than 1 s apart. The clock reads a few milliseconds.
2. **The gate (reveal)** — The same tree, but each lane now waits on one shared `throttle` rail. One ticket per second lets exactly one lane through. The clock climbs to about 13 s. The tree is identical, so the learner sees that concurrency was kept.
3. **Who gets the ticket** — A zoom on the rail. Five lanes wait in a solid block. One ticket arrives; one lane crosses; the other four stay blocked. A caption says the order is unspecified in Go.
4. **The cost of the fix** — The full fixed run with a real-time clock. The rate is correct, but the crawl now takes 13 s instead of 5 ms. A short caption ties this to the 30 s test timeout and to the `time.Tick` ticker that never stops.

**Controls.** Play / pause; Step one event forward (and back); Restart; Speed: 1x, 4x, 16x (compress 13 s into a few seconds); Reveal toggle: 'scaffold' vs 'add the gate' - the default is scaffold; the gate scene is behind this reveal.

**Code anchors.**
- 00-limit-crawler/main.go:38 - `go Crawl(u, depth-1, wg)` is the concurrency the learner must keep
- 00-limit-crawler/main.go:26 - `fetcher.Fetch(url)` is the call the gate must serialize
- 00-limit-crawler/main.go:20 and :34 - `defer wg.Done()` and `wg.Add(len(urls))` frame the lane lifetime
- 00-limit-crawler/mockfetcher.go:22 - `fetchSignalInstance() <- true` fires before the lookup, so a not-found URL still counts
- 00-limit-crawler/check_test.go:34 - the 950 ms check that produces the violation
- solutions/00-limit-crawler.md - approach 1: `var throttle = time.Tick(time.Second)` plus `<-throttle` before Fetch; the gate rail is these two lines

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Timeline is off by one second. A gate solution in a temp copy logs fetch #1 at t=1.00s because time.Tick fires only after one interval. The 13 fetches land at t=1.00..13.00s, not t=0..12s as the data_model says.
- Goroutine count is wrong. The same run makes 29 `go Crawl` calls: 13 fetch, and 16 depth-0 calls return before they reach `<-throttle`. The core_idea sentence '13 racing goroutines' and the 'one lane per spawned Crawl goroutine' model both misstate the actors.
- The fixed scene's premise that 'the same 13 lanes arrive almost together' is false. A child goroutine is spawned only after its parent's Fetch returns, so lanes arrive progressively and the queue grows. Only the scaffold compresses all 13 fetches into one instant; a fixed frame with 13 bars at the rail together teaches a wrong model.
- The lane model is self-contradictory: '13 lanes', 'one lane per spawned Crawl goroutine', 'one lane per Fetch call', 'keyed by url and depth'. Repeats exist: pkg/ at depth 1 is fetched by three goroutines, / at depth 1 by two, and cmd/ is fetched at depths 3, 2 and 1.
- placement_hint problem-inline plus a 'scaffold vs add the gate' toggle puts the code-level fix one click from the problem statement; the control label alone names the fix. The README already shows the abstract gate (README.md:13-23), so the concept is not secret, but the interactive reveal belongs in the Solution tab after the spoiler button.
- The risks section says the scene 'replaces the README's today-vs-goal diagram'. It cannot: that diagram is the only clone-mode artifact, and lint requires it (tools/check/lint.go:257, readme-diagram).
- 'Lazy-loaded with the existing per-exercise JSON' is not implementable as written. web/tools/gen/main.go reads only .go, .md and go.mod from `git archive HEAD` (readHEAD, main.go:459-498), and the lint layout rule forbids new files in the exercise folder (lint.go:154-160). Scene data must load from web/src/, not from public/data/<id>.json.
- Reduced motion is required but not designed. The controls list play/pause and step, but nothing says autoplay is off under prefers-reduced-motion; the stylesheet only neutralizes transitions (styles.css:1067).
- 320px is hand-waved ('scroll or aggregate') while page-level horizontal scroll is forbidden. 13 labelled lanes cannot fit; the design below reduces them to 10 unique (url, depth) lanes plus a counter and fixes row height and type size.
- The 'cost of the fix' scene cites the leaking time.Tick as the cost. The cost the learner needs is 5 ms versus 13 s against the 30 s test timeout; keep the ticker leak as a one-line aside at most.
- Verdict high is overstated. The README already draws today versus goal with a shared gate, the unmodified scaffold fails in 0.00s of test time with the exact symptom, and the solution doc bands the three fix lines. The new insight is the 13 s cost and the not-found URL consuming a tick: useful, but nice, not high. The 'exercise 00 sets expectations' argument is a product reason, not a teaching reason for this exercise.

**Why this tier.** The first exercise a visitor opens; the shared throttle gate and the 5 ms to 13 s cost are visible, but the README already draws the shape.

<a id="01-producer-consumer"></a>
#### 1 — Producer-Consumer
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Producer-consumer pipeline over one channel: the producer returns a receive-only `<-chan *Tweet` and closes it; the consumer ranges over it. The overlap turns additive stage costs into the slower stage's cadence.

**Core idea.** Two stages that do not depend on each other should run at the same time. Total time becomes roughly the maximum of the two stage costs plus one item of pipeline fill, not the sum. On an unbuffered channel the producer also blocks on `out <- tweet` until the consumer receives.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The scaffold prints correct output, so nothing in the output shows that the work is serial; only the ~3.58s total says so, and the test only gates on a 2.5s threshold. The learner cannot see where each goroutine spends its time blocked, how far the producer runs ahead, or that a buffered channel lets the producer finish reading early without changing throughput when the consumer is the slower stage. The `Stream` "not threadsafe" rule (only one goroutine may call `Next()`) is also invisible: the naive code has one goroutine and no race, so nothing teaches why the producer must never be fanned out.

**Design.** A shared time axis runs left to right. The top lane is the producer, the bottom lane is the consumer, and between them sits a channel drawn as C slots. In the default scene only the producer lane is active: five solid 320 ms read blocks plus a short EOF read, while the consumer lane is a flat empty band, then five 330 ms classify blocks begin only after 1.92 s. A finish marker lands at 3.58 s with the wasted consumer band highlighted. Flip to the concurrent run and the second producer block turns amber and reads "send blocks" until the consumer's first classify is ready to receive; the classify blocks slide left to start at 320 ms, and the finish marker slides in to 1.97 s. The learner sees the sum become the max. Turning the buffer control up moves sent tweets into the slot row and lets the producer lane empty out early, but the consumer's finish marker barely moves, which is the counter-intuitive part.

**Scenes.**
1. **Serial scaffold (default)** — One active lane. producer reads 5 tweets plus an EOF check at 320 ms each (~1.92 s); the consumer lane stays empty until then; then 5 classifications at 330 ms each (~1.65 s). A highlighted idle band and a 3.58 s finish marker make the additive cost obvious. No channel is drawn yet.
2. **Handoff on an unbuffered channel** — Producer and consumer overlap. The producer's send turns amber ('blocked: no receiver') until the consumer receives the previous item; classify blocks begin at 320 ms; the producer runs exactly one item ahead; the finish marker sits at ~1.97 s.
3. **Read-ahead with a buffer** — The buffer control (0, 1, 2, 5) fills the slot row between the lanes. A larger buffer lets the producer finish reading sooner and go idle, but the consumer finish marker stays at ~1.97 s because 330 ms per item is the bottleneck. Teaches that buffering changes when a send blocks, not throughput when the consumer is slow.
4. **Three consumers (solution Approach 2)** — Three consumer lanes pull from the same channel. Total barely improves (~1.94 s) because the producer's 320 ms cadence is the limit, and printed lines can land out of source order - which the test's ordered assertion forbids, so this scene must say it is a lesson about bottlenecks, not a recommended answer here.

**Controls.** Play / pause (Space); Step one event (arrow keys), shows the next send/recv/block; Restart (R); Scrub the timeline; Buffer size: 0 / 1 / 2 / 5; Consumer count: 1 / 3; Stage speed: default, or make the consumer fast (e.g. 100 ms) to show when buffering and fan-out do pay off; Speed: 1x / 2x.

**Code anchors.**
- Scaffold main.go: producer(stream Stream) (tweets []*Tweet) and the adjacent producer(stream) then consumer(tweets) calls in main - the serial lane
- Scaffold main.go: tweets = append(tweets, tweet) - the slice that forces the producer to finish before the consumer starts
- Scaffold main.go: consumer(tweets []*Tweet) ranging over the slice
- Solution: func producer(stream Stream) <-chan *Tweet; go func() { defer close(out); ... out <- tweet }(); return out
- Solution: consumer's for t := range tweets - blocks on empty, exits on close
- mockstream.go: comment 'Stream is a mock stream for demonstration purposes, not threadsafe' - why the producer is never fanned out
- mockstream.go: time.Sleep(320 * time.Millisecond) in Next and time.Sleep(330 * time.Millisecond) in IsTalkingAboutGo - the two stage costs the timeline draws
- check_test.go: TestMainRunsProducerAndConsumerConcurrently's 2500ms threshold and TestMainProducesCorrectOutput's ordered-line assertion

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Verdict overstates. The committed README already draws the serial-vs-pipelined Gantt with the exact 3.58s and 1.98s figures (README.md ASCII block), and the solution doc already argues that buffering does not help ("Would a buffered channel help here? No..."). Exercise 01 is rated warm-up (README.md:18). The only net-new content is an interactive buffer/count control and blocking-state labels. That is a secondary point, not a high-value gap. Corrected tier: nice.
- Timing error: the proposal calls the EOF read "short". mockstream.go line 35 sleeps 320ms before the pos check, so the 6th read is a full 320ms. Serial = 6x320 + 5x330 = 3570ms. Draw it as a sixth full block, not a short one.
- Scene 2 says "the producer runs exactly one item ahead". On an unbuffered channel the send is a rendezvous and nothing is buffered. The real overlap is read of item N+1 against classify of item N. As written it teaches a false buffering model.
- Buffered scene says the finish marker "barely moves". In the model it does not move at all: total is 1970ms for buffer 0, 1, 2 and 5. Say "does not move".
- Scene 4 (three consumers) conflicts with TestMainProducesCorrectOutput, which asserts source order (check_test.go:20,35). Animating fan-out as an option pushes learners at a solution the test can fume out of order. Drop the scene or reduce it to one caveat line; do not present it as an equally good answer.
- The claimed blind spot "Stream is not threadsafe, never fan out the producer" is not made visible by any scene. It is only annotation text. Either drop it from the claimed teaching value or add a scene that shows two producers racing s.pos (and note the browser has no race detector).
- spoiler_safe is only honest for the default scene. Scenes 2-4 reveal the pipelined fix and Approach 2. DocPane's revealed flag is local state (DocPane.tsx:17). Without a lifted shared gate or a second explicit gate, the tab leaks the solution.
- Placement reasoning is a false dichotomy. A tab is not needed because the README has no inline Markdown hook: DocPane can render a component after <Markdown> in the Problem tab (DocPane.tsx:54). For one exercise a third tab forces touches to router.ts:6,12,17, DocPane.tsx:11, ExerciseView.tsx:6 and App.tsx (tab=route.tab), plus a hide-empty-tab rule across 40+ exercises, for content that belongs beside the diagram it explains.
- 320px: two horizontal Gantt lanes plus a slot row plus inline labels will not fit. The proposal's own fix (legend below) is a layout change, not a risk note. Feasible only with an SVG viewBox and labels moved out of the lanes.
- Drift risk is unguarded. A hand-copied 320/330 table in web/src can diverge from mockstream.go. The exercise JSON already carries mockstream.go content in exercise.files, so read the sleeps from it (regex with a fallback) or add a check, instead of a second hard-coded table. The proposal's "one table" does not actually stay in step by itself.

**Why this tier.** Producer and consumer overlap on an unbuffered channel; useful, but the 320 ms EOF read and the buffering lesson need care.

<a id="02-race-in-cache"></a>
#### 2 — Race in an LRU Cache
`Tier C` · **nice** · cache grid · family `grid` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Mutual exclusion over a compound structure: a mutex (or RWMutex) around an LRU cache whose hit path writes, plus per-key single-flight load deduplication.

**Core idea.** Get is not a read. A cache hit calls MoveToFront, which rewrites the shared list's prev/next pointers, so the hit path needs the same exclusion as the miss path. And for one key the miss-check, the load and the insert must be one atomic step; if the lock is narrowed to the bookkeeping, concurrent misses each load from the DB and each PushFront a node, so the list grows while the map holds one entry.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** Two failures are invisible from the prose and the test output. First, "hit" sounds read-only, so an RWMutex with RLock on the hit path looks correct, yet two RLock holders both run MoveToFront on the same node (exactly what check_test.go:105 TestConcurrentHits is built to expose). Second, the narrowed-lock fix is race-free but silently wrong: three goroutines can all miss on one key, each call the 20 ms DB load, and each PushFront a node, leaving `pages` longer than `cache`; the test only says "Incorrect pages size 1490" and "Too much db uses 1500". The web runner has no race detector and GOMAXPROCS=1, so the browser learner may never see either failure.

**Design.** One frame in the doc pane, three stacked bands that fit 320 px. Top: two to four goroutine lanes, each a pill with a colour, the call it makes (`Get("x")`), and its state (running, waiting for the lock, loading, done). Middle: the cache — a short recency strip of four key/value cards, front (most recent) on the left, back (least recent) on the right, with small prev/next arrows between neighbours, and beneath it the map as key chips, each with an arrow to the node it points at. Bottom: a lock chip with its holder and a waiter queue, and a database block whose 20 ms load is drawn as a bar on the calling lane's timeline. A caption line states the outcome in the tests' own language (`db.Calls = 3`, `cache 1 != pages 3`, `WARNING: DATA RACE`). Colour comes only from the CSS variables: accent for the actor executing now, a warning tone for any write to a node or the map, muted for idle. Every scene runs on the same skeleton; only the scripted event list changes.

**Scenes.**
1. **Two hits, one node** — Default scene, the scaffold. A and B both call Get("x") at the same instant with no lock. Both read the same map entry, both reach MoveToFront on the same node, and their prev/next rewrites interleave: B reads a neighbour, A replaces it, B writes back the stale value. A warning badge marks the node "write by A / write by B" and the caption reads WARNING: DATA RACE. Takeaway: a hit is a write.
2. **Miss storm** — Three goroutines miss on "y" before any of them inserts. Three 20 ms load bars overlap; each returns and PushFronts, so the strip ends with three "y" nodes while the map holds one chip. Counters read db.Calls = 3 and cache 1 != pages 3, the two numbers the tests print. With lock scope "bookkeeping only" this ordering is deterministic — the quiet failure; with "none" the same calls also race on the map.
3. **One lock, held across the load** — The same three misses inside a single mutex. A takes the lock and holds it through its 20 ms load; B, C, and even a hit on another key sit in the waiter queue. The lane timeline stretches to distinct keys x 20 ms and db.Calls rises by exactly one per key. Caption: correct, and serialized — 100 distinct keys is about 2 s.
4. **Wait on done, not on the lock** — A misses on "y", records loading["y"] = c, unlocks, and loads. B misses on the same key, finds c, leaves the lock and waits on c.done; C hits "x" and never waits. Loads on different keys run in parallel, and close(c.done) releases B at the instant A's bar ends. Caption: one DB call per key, throughput scales with distinct keys.

**Controls.** Play, pause, step, restart, speed — step is the reduced-motion mode, and every frame is reachable without motion.; Goroutines on the same key: 1 / 2 / 4 — the collision appears only at 2 or more.; Lock scope: none / bookkeeping only / whole Get — toggles the scaffold, the race-free-but-double-loading near-miss, and Approach 1.; DB latency: 20 ms / 200 ms — stretches scene 3 until the serialization cost is obvious.; Scene picker; scenes 3 and 4 sit behind a "show the fix" reveal, the way the Solution tab sits behind its spoiler gate..

**Code anchors.**
- 02-race-in-cache/main.go:32-36 — the struct: cache map[string]*list.Element, pages list.List, load func; the three fields a lock must cover.
- 02-race-in-cache/main.go:48-51 — the hit path; line 49 k.pages.MoveToFront(e) is the invisible write.
- 02-race-in-cache/main.go:53 — p := page{key, k.load(key)}: the 20 ms miss cost that decides where the critical section must end.
- 02-race-in-cache/main.go:55-63 — eviction and insert: delete, Remove, PushFront, k.cache[key] = k.pages.Front().
- 02-race-in-cache/check_test.go:105-122 — TestConcurrentHits (warmKeys=4, 500 goroutines) targets RLock-on-hit.
- 02-race-in-cache/check_test.go:60-62 — db.Calls > callsPerCycle is the duplicate-load assertion.
- 02-race-in-cache/mockserver.go:16-28 — 15 cycles x 100 keys; the access pattern the timeline mirrors.
- 02-race-in-cache/mockdb.go:21-23 — the 20 ms sleep and db.Calls increment behind the latency control.
- solutions/02-race-in-cache.md Approach 1 — banded lines 22,38,39: sync.Mutex, Lock, defer Unlock held across the load.
- solutions/02-race-in-cache.md Approach 2 — banded lines 34,55,59,63,64,68,82: the call struct, the loading map, unlock before load, wait on c.done.

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- The central claim is empirically false: the browser runner does surface the duplicate-load failure. `VERBOSE=1 node scripts/smoke-test.ts --only 02` printed `check_test.go:55: Incorrect cache size 99`, `check_test.go:58: Incorrect pages size 864`, `check_test.go:61: Too much db uses 1500`, and TestMain failed. Only TestLRU and TestConcurrentHits passed. So only the hit-path race and the RWMutex trap are invisible in the browser; the drift/duplicate-load failure is loudly visible.
- The design does not show its own headline blind spot. The RWMutex-with-RLock-on-hit near-miss (check_test.go:95-104) has no scene, and the lock-scope control has no RWMutex option. Scene 2 instead repeats numbers the browser already prints.
- spoiler_safe is not honest: the default-visible control `Lock scope: none / bookkeeping only / whole Get` names both candidate fixes. `whole Get` telegraphs Approach 1 and `bookkeeping only` telegraphs the near-miss.
- 320 px is not the real floor. `.workspace` is `grid-template-columns: minmax(260px, var(--doc-frac,40%)) ...` (styles.css:506) and `.doc-body` has 22 px side padding (styles.css:573-575), so usable content width can be about 216 px. Four cards plus prev/next arrows plus a map with connector arrows cannot fit.
- A standalone third tab is the wrong placement. router.ts holds a two-value tab union (lines 4-7, 12, 17) and DocPane hard-codes two tab anchors (lines 26-32); the tab would also read empty on 42 of 43 exercises.
- The per-exercise flag cannot live in exercise data. `tools/check/lint.go:154-156` forbids non-.go files in an exercise folder, and `web/tools/gen/main.go:484-487` reads only .go, .md and go.mod from `git archive HEAD`. A web/src registry is required, with no generator change.
- The event matrix (scene x lockScope x concurrency x latency) multiplies authored scripts and creates nonsensical states, for example scene 4 (`wait on done`) with lock scope `none`. Lock scope belongs to the scene, not to a separate control.
- Scene 2's determinism belongs only to the narrow-lock variant; the scaffold ordering is not deterministic. Every frame needs the label `one possible interleaving`, and 1490 must not be quoted as the test's number: the browser run printed 864.
- The caption `WARNING: DATA RACE` can imply the browser produced it, while web/README.md:42 states the race detector is unavailable there. Say `the race detector would report ...` and mark the panel as scripted.
- Reduced motion needs auto-pause, not just a step button. styles.css already has the `prefers-reduced-motion` block at line 1067.
- Minor line drift: TestConcurrentHits is check_test.go:105-131, and mockserver's constants are lines 15-18 with the loop at 25-44, not 16-28.
- Verdict `high` overstates the value. README.md:27-32 and 49-53 already state both failures in bold, the ASCII diagram at README.md:13-17 shows the collision, and solutions/02-race-in-cache.md:14-80 gives verified race output for both the RWMutex variant and the narrow lock.

**Why this tier.** The hit path writes under a read lock; a recency strip shows the lost update, but the browser already prints loud numbers.

<a id="07-or-done-channel"></a>
#### 7 — Or-Done Channel: Stopping a Monitoring Feed Cleanly
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** or-done channel wrapper (cancellable forwarding goroutine with select on every blocking channel operation)

**Core idea.** A wrapper that copies values from a channel you do not own must race done against BOTH operations that can block: the receive from c and the send on out. Guarding only one still wedges the goroutine. `defer close(out)` is what lets the consumer's `range` end.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The forwarder has two distinct parked states, and the learner can see only one. When done closes, the goroutine may be idle in the outer select waiting for a value from c, or it may already hold a value and be parked in the inner select sending on out, because the consumer stopped reading. The mid-forward case is invisible in prose: a solution whose receive is guarded and whose send is bare passes every test that never sends on c, yet drops a stale value after done. The learner also cannot see that with the passthrough, the leaked goroutine is not a forwarder at all: it is StartMetricStream's own producer, parked forever on `out <- counter` because `out` is literally `c`.

**Design.** Show three lanes stacked vertically in a narrow column. Top lane: the producer goroutine from StartMetricStream, which sleeps 20ms and then offers the next number on c. Middle lane: the orDone forwarder, which does not exist in the scaffold scene. Bottom lane: main, which ranges over out and closes done after five values. Between the lanes sit two channel slots, c and out, drawn as short labelled pipes. In the scaffold scene, c and out are drawn as ONE pipe with a dashed alias line and the caption "out IS c". The player steps through: values cross c into main, main closes done at value 5, and the done signal travels to a wall - there is no goroutine holding a select on it. The producer wakes, offers 6, and main's 200ms probe receives 6 instead of a closed channel. In the fixed scenes, the middle lane appears with a goroutine icon parked on two select cases, draw both as "sleeping" sockets. Closing done lights the done socket, the forwarder exits, a `defer close(out)` marker fires, and the consumer's next receive returns ok=false.

**Scenes.**
1. **Aliased: done hits a wall (default)** — Scaffold. c and out are one pipe. main reads 1..5, closes done at frame 5, and the done arrow stops at a wall labelled 'no goroutine selects on done'. The producer sleeps, offers 6, and main's timed probe prints 'unexpected: got a value after done closed: 6'. The producer lane then parks on `out <- counter` forever.
2. **Parked on the receive** — Fixed forwarder (behind the spoiler gate). The forwarder holds an outer select on c and done, with nothing arriving on c. done closes; the done case wins; `return` fires; `defer close(out)` closes out; the consumer's probe reads ok=false within one step.
3. **Value in hand: the send also blocks** — The consumer pulls one value, then stops reading. The forwarder has already pulled the next value and is parked at the inner select `out <- v` / `<-done`. done closes, the done case wins, the held value is dropped, out closes. A ghost lane beside it shows the broken variant with a bare `out <- v`: it stays parked forever, and a later reader gets a stale post-done value.
4. **What the fix does not stop** — After the forwarder exits, the producer lane keeps sleeping and then parks forever on `out <- counter`, because StartMetricStream never watches done. The scene states the scope: the exercise stops the forwarding goroutine, not the feed goroutine.

**Controls.** Restart; Play / Pause; Step forward; Step back; Scene selector (4 named scenes); Close done at this step (trigger, instead of a fixed frame); Consumer stops reading (toggle; needed to reach the mid-forward scene); Show the broken one-select variant (toggle, ghost lane); Speed: slow / normal (no autoplay when prefers-reduced-motion).

**Code anchors.**
- 07-or-done-channel/main.go:47-49 - `orDone` returns c unchanged; the scene draws one pipe and the alias caption
- 07-or-done-channel/main.go:56-63 - the range over out and `close(done)` at count == 5
- 07-or-done-channel/main.go:72-79 - the probe select with `time.After(200 * time.Millisecond)` and the two printed outcomes
- 07-or-done-channel/mockmonitor.go:19-25 - the producer loop `time.Sleep(MetricInterval); counter++; out <- counter`; the parked-send frame
- 07-or-done-channel/check_test.go:55 (orDoneStopsPromptly) - c never written to; the 'parked on receive' scene
- 07-or-done-channel/check_test.go:120 (orDoneStopsPromptlyMidForward) - drain one value, then close done; the 'value in hand' scene
- solutions/07-or-done-channel.md:70-82 - outer select on done/c, inner select on out<-v/<-done
- solutions/07-or-done-channel.md:67 - `defer close(out)`, the annotation that makes the consumer's range terminate

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Verdict overstatement: the scaffold header already gives the full fix. main.go:18-26 says the goroutine must forward values, and 'each of them must be a select that also watches done'. README.md:20-27 draws both selects and says done unblocks 'whichever select is currently parked'. The visualisation repeats a stated spec; it does not reveal a blind spot. Tier 'high' is too strong.
- Scene 1 duplicates a live demo on the same page. CodePane.tsx:449 has a 'Run main' button that runs 'go run ./07-or-done-channel' in the browser, and main.go:75 prints 'unexpected: got a value after done closed: 6'. The default scene adds nothing the learner cannot already run.
- The third tab overflows the tab row. styles.css:540 '.doc-tabs' is a non-wrapping flex row. styles.css:504 lets the doc pane shrink to 260px, and styles.css:572 gives only 216px of content after 22px side padding. Problem + Solution + Visualise + the difficulty badge + the passed badge (DocPane.tsx:34-39) needs about 370px at the 14px base font. A learner who passes the exercise sees the overflow at 320px.
- The reason for a separate tab is false. The analyst says a 320px doc pane cannot hold an animation inside a scrolling markdown body. But styles.css:572-575 makes '.doc-body' scroll vertically, and height is not scarce. A vertical diagram fits 216-288px of width. A third tab also forces router.ts:4-18, DocPane.tsx:11 and 26-32, and ExerciseView.tsx:10 and 41 changes for no teaching gain.
- The orientation fights the pane. Horizontal swimlanes put time on the x-axis, the short axis (216-288px). Rotate to a vertical sequence diagram: time top-to-bottom, three columns (producer, forwarder, consumer), and c, out and done as arrows. The doc body gives unlimited height.
- The control model contradicts itself. The proposal calls the timeline 'scripted' and 'deterministic', but also offers 'Close done at this step' and a live 'Consumer stops reading' toggle. An arbitrary trigger needs a branching simulator, not a fixed scene script, and it makes Step and Pause ambiguous.
- The ghost-lane claim is inaccurate. With a bare 'out <- v', the forwarder parks only while nobody reads. A later reader receives the stale value, then the loop re-enters the select and exits on done. check_test.go:145-149 takes exactly that stale value. The value is delivered late, not dropped. core_idea's 'drops a stale value' and scene 3's 'stays parked forever, and a later reader gets a stale value' cannot both hold in one run; the ghost must branch or state both.
- The 'done case wins' caption teaches a false rule. Go selects uniformly at random between ready cases. done wins in these scenes only because the other case is not ready. The caption must say 'the other case is not ready', or the learner learns that done has priority.
- The event model splits an unbuffered handoff into send-offer, send-commit and recv. An unbuffered send and receive are one atomic rendezvous. Draw one handoff event per value, or learners will infer a buffer.
- The name 'out' is overloaded. mockmonitor.go:17 and 24 name the producer's channel 'out'; orDone and main call that same channel 'c' (main.go:53-54). Scene 4's 'producer parked on out <- counter' will be read as the forwarder's output. Use 'c' for the producer's channel in the diagram.
- Twelve annotations will not fit 216px. Floating labels collide or shrink below legibility. Put the current-step narration in a caption or log strip below the diagram, and keep at most two or three labels on the diagram.
- Accessibility is underspecified. The lesson is the text state, so each step needs an aria-live='polite' region. The scene selector needs a labelled radio group or a select. Annotations must be static text, not tooltips, because the rules forbid hover. prefers-reduced-motion must disable autoplay and CSS transitions, with rules beside styles.css:1067.
- The spoiler gate leaks. The broken one-select toggle and the fixed-forwarder scenes reveal the fix. The proposal lists them as controls but does not say they are unreachable before 'revealed' (DocPane.tsx:17 and 57). 'spoiler_safe: true' holds for scene 1 only.
- Effort M is low for the first visualisation in the app. The per-exercise story is small, but the shared player shell (step model, controls, live region, theme tokens, reduced motion, narrow layout) is L. Later stories are M.
- The registry can drift. A hand-kept 'id to dynamic import' map will show empty tabs or miss a story. Use import.meta.glob('./visuals/*.tsx') so the key set is the file set, and import only when the Solution tab is revealed.
- Clone and web modes will diverge. Web-only is correct, but scene 4's fact ('the fix stops the forwarder, not the feed') is not stated as a post-fix fact in the committed README. README.md:47-50 speaks only about the scaffold. Add one sentence to 07-or-done-channel/README.md, which is a permitted file, so clone-mode learners get the same model.

**Why this tier.** The done channel stops the feed; a lane shows the leak, but the README diagram carries the shape.

<a id="10-semaphore"></a>
#### 10 — Semaphore: Bounding Parallelism Against a Rate-Limited API
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Counting semaphore from a buffered channel of struct{}: send acquires a permit (blocks when full), receive releases it, bounding how many goroutines are inside api.Call at once.

**Core idea.** A buffered channel of capacity N is a complete counting semaphore. A send acquires one of N permits and blocks when all are held; a receive releases one. The semaphore bounds how many goroutines are inside the critical section at any instant, not how many goroutines exist.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** Twelve goroutines are spawned, so the program looks massively concurrent, yet the only number that matters is how many are between sem acquire and release when the API counts them. The scaffold's failure is also not a fixed number: the API rejects instantly rather than queuing, so the high-water mark depends on scheduling (4 with 9 errors in the deterministic GOMAXPROCS=1 interleaving, potentially higher on multicore). Nothing in the prose shows the send queue of parked goroutines, the release that wakes the oldest waiter, or that the one-at-a-time "fix" passes the limit test and fails the concurrency test.

**Design.** Draw a left-to-right timeline. Twelve thin horizontal lanes, R0–R11, are the twelve request goroutines. Each lane's bar moves from spawn, through a gate, into api.Call, then to a result. The gate near the top holds N permit cells — the buffered channel sem — and a lane whose send finds every cell taken stops at the gate with a "blocked on sem <- struct{}{}" tag, queued left to right in FIFO order. Behind the gate, FlakyAPI is a box with a live in-flight meter (0..12), a red threshold line at 3, and a high-water badge. A rejected call bounces off the API instantly: the lane turns red and writes "ERROR: too many concurrent requests" without ever waiting. A bottom strip holds the twelve result cells and an elapsed clock. Default scene: no gate, all twelve lanes enter at once, the meter crosses the red line, calls bounce back red, and the solution doc's FAIL output appears beside the figure. Second scene: two permit cells; lanes queue, the meter never passes 2, every result turns green, and the clock shows 600ms against 1200ms sequential.

**Scenes.**
1. **Scaffold: fire all twelve** — All 12 lanes spawn at t=0 and enter api.Call together. The meter climbs to 4 — three accepted calls sleep for 100ms, the fourth increments before the check and is rejected — then every later lane bounces red instantly and decrements the meter. High-water badge reads 4, nine result cells turn red with the API's error, three turn green, elapsed 100ms. A caption says this is one valid interleaving: the invariant is that the mark is unbounded and above 2, the exact value depends on the scheduler. The doc's 'high-water mark = 4' FAIL block is quoted.
2. **Two permits: the cap** — The gate has two cells. R0 and R1 acquire and call; R2–R11 park at the gate with 'blocked on sem <- struct{}{}', drawn in a FIFO queue. At t=100ms each call returns, <-sem releases a cell, the oldest waiter becomes runnable and acquires. Six 100ms batches later, elapsed is 600ms, the meter never exceeds 2, every result is green, and the high-water badge reads 2.
3. **One permit: the anti-fix** — A single permit serializes the whole batch. No errors and high-water 1, so the limit test passes, but the lanes run edge to edge for 1200ms. A check panel shows TestFetchAllStillConcurrent's 600ms budget failing (10 requests at 100ms each is 1000ms with one permit), teaching that the fix must bound concurrency, not eliminate it.
4. **Forgotten release: deadlock** — The release on one path is skipped. The buffer fills and stays full; the lanes behind it block on send forever and the elapsed clock stops. The figure reports 'no goroutine runnable — deadlock', showing why defer func() { <-sem }() sits next to the acquire rather than at the end of the happy path.

**Controls.** Play / pause, step forward, step back, restart — real buttons, keyboard reachable, no hover-only affordance; Permits: none (scaffold, default) | 1 | 2 | 3. Option 3 clears the API's own limit of 3 but fails the test's wantMaxHighWaterMark=2; each value shows both check outcomes side by side; Interleaving seed, naive scene only: deterministically permutes the order the 12 spawns reach the API and shows the high-water mark change, teaching that the bug is unbounded concurrency rather than one unlucky number; Skip one release: turns the cap into a deadlock (scene 4), teaching why the release sits beside the acquire; Reveal the fixed mechanics: hidden until the Solution tab's existing spoiler button is pressed (or an in-figure reveal with the same wording); never a default.

I ran HEAD's scaffold three times in a temp module with Go 1.25.6: Fetched 12 requests in ~100ms (API high-water mark: 4); request-0, request-1 and request-11 succeeded, request-2..request-10 errored. The proposal's data_model says G0..G2 accepted and G3..G11 rejected. Script the real order or stop naming which lanes succeed.
- Scene 4 is mechanically wrong. 'Skip one release' leaks one permit; the semaphore degrades to effective capacity 1 and the batch still completes. Verified: Fetched 12 requests in 1.109695334s (API high-water mark: 2), no deadlock. Removing the release on every path does deadlock: 'fatal error: all goroutines are asleep - deadlock!'. The scene must say 'release missing on every path', or model a leak on an error path that actually runs (permits above the API limit), and must not claim a single skipped release deadlocks.
- Request-count conflation. The demo's 12 requests at 2 permits take ~600ms; the 600ms test budget belongs to TestFetchAllStillConcurrent's 10 requests, which take ~500ms at 2 permits and ~1000ms at 1 permit. A 12-request two-permit run is exactly on the 600ms line, so overlaying the test budget on it reads as a failure. The 'in-figure reveal' option would also bypass the existing Solution spoiler. 'Problem-inline' therefore means either a fixed slot in DocPane (above the Markdown or under the topics chips) or a new Markdown directive. A README directive would leak into clone mode for no benefit; use a DocPane slot keyed by summary.id.
- The meter threshold is mislabeled. The API rejects when a.current > a.maxConcurrent (mockapi.go:49), so 3 in flight is accepted and 4 is the first rejection; a 'red threshold line at 3' implies 3 is bad. The test's wantMaxHighWaterMark = 2 (check_test.go:65) is a different, stricter bound. Draw and label both: API limit 3 allowed, reject at 4; test bound 2.
- The data model's 'Sem capacity in {0,1,2,3}' is wrong for the scaffold: capacity 0 is an unbuffered channel, which blocks every send until a receive, not the naive unbounded program. Model 'none | 1 | 2 | 3'.

<a id="11-worker-pool"></a>
#### 11 — Worker Pool: Batch Job Processor with Partial Failures
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Fixed-size worker pool: N long-lived workers range over one shared unbuffered jobs channel, each runs RunJob on the job it receives and sends its Result on one shared results channel; a feeder goroutine closes jobCh when the input slice is exhausted, and a coordinator goroutine does wg.Wait() then close(resultCh) so the caller's drain loop terminates.

**Core idea.** Concurrency is capped by the number of workers, not by the number of jobs. N workers stay alive and pull jobs one at a time, so the jobs in flight at any instant is exactly N. Two separate close points make the pipeline terminate: close(jobCh) ends every worker's range loop, and close(resultCh) after wg.Wait() ends the caller's drain loop.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The scaffold is correct, not broken: TestProcessJobsCorrectness and TestProcessJobsRace pass against it. Only the clock and the in-flight count expose the defect. The learner cannot see from the prose that exactly one RunJob is ever live, that a pool holds the high-water mark at N while a per-job fan-out pushes it to the job count, or that putting wg.Wait() on the caller's goroutine freezes the whole pool: workers block sending on resultCh while the only goroutine that would drain resultCh sits in wg.Wait().

**Design.** A panel with a single horizontal time axis. Two thin channel rails, jobs and results, run left to right between the goroutine lanes. Job tokens are small labelled circles that travel along the jobs rail from the feeder lane to a worker lane; result tokens travel back along the results rail, green for nil and red for ErrJobFailed. Above the lanes sit two counters, in flight and high-water mark, and a virtual clock. The default frame plays the scaffold: one lane named main goroutine, twenty 80ms bars end to end, in flight stuck at 1, high-water mark 1, clock near 1.6s. With workers set to 4 the feeder lane and four worker lanes light up, four bars run side by side, the high-water counter pins at 4, and the clock stops near 0.4s. A shutdown phase replays the two closes: the jobs rail dims and shows close(jobCh), each worker's range ends and its lane closes, the coordinator lane runs wg.Wait() until the fourth worker exits, then closes the results rail, and only then does the caller's drain loop end. One toggle puts wg.Wait() on the caller lane: the four worker bars finish, their result tokens pile up at the results rail, the caller lane sits still holding wg.Wait(), the clock freezes, and a caption names the deadlock cycle. Colour comes only from the --pass, --fail, --accent, --fg-muted and --border CSS variables, so both themes work.

**Scenes.**
1. **Today: one job at a time** — The default scene. One main-goroutine lane, twenty 80ms bars back to back, in flight 1, high-water mark 1, clock about 1.6s. Calls out main.go:70-73: the for loop and the single RunJob call that fix in-flight at 1. Caption says the run is correct, only slow.
2. **Pool of four: the cap, not the job count** — Feeder sends jobs one at a time over the jobs rail; four worker lanes each hold one bar; tokens fan in on the results rail. High-water mark pins at 4 and stays there when the job count rises from 20 to 60; the clock drops to about ceil(jobs/4) x 80ms. Shows solutions/11-worker-pool.md:83-97.
3. **Two closes, two unblocks** — Shutdown phase, one event per step. close(jobCh) at solution doc:103 ends every worker's range at doc:92, each worker exits; the coordinator's wg.Wait() at doc:107 returns, close(resultCh) at doc:108 ends the caller's drain at doc:112. Each close is highlighted with the loop it unblocks, so the learner sees two channels, two owners, two reasons.
4. **Inline wg.Wait(): the freeze** — The deadlock variant the solution doc warns about. Workers finish their bars but block sending on resultCh; the caller lane holds wg.Wait() instead of draining; every lane stops and the clock freezes. Step reveals the cycle and then the fix: the coordinator goroutine is what breaks it.

**Controls.** Play / Pause (keyboard: space); Step one event forward, and back (arrow keys); Restart (Home); Workers slider 1 to 8: at 1 the pool reads like the scaffold, at 8 the high-water mark follows, so the learner sees the cap is the worker count; Jobs selector 8 / 20 / 60: total time scales with jobs, the high-water mark does not; Toggle 'Put wg.Wait() on the caller': triggers the deadlock frame; Toggle 'One goroutine per job': the fan-out anti-fix, high-water mark equals job count.

- 11-worker-pool/mockjobs.go:18 — JobLatency = 80ms, the bar length
- 11-worker-pool/mockjobs.go:63-69 — the high-water instrumentation the counter mirrors
- 11-worker-pool/check_test.go:137-145 — the assertion hwm > 1 and hwm <= 20 that the visual makes legible
- solutions/11-worker-pool.md:83-97 — jobCh, resultCh, wg.Add(numWorkers), the worker range over jobCh
- solutions/11-worker-pool.md:99-104 — the feeder goroutine and close(jobCh)
- solutions/11-worker-pool.md:106-114 — the coordinator's wg.Wait() and close(resultCh), and the caller's drain loop
- solutions/11-worker-pool.md:128-137 — the prose that names each channel's close and its reason

<a id="14b-tee-channel-independent-close"></a>
#### 14b — Tee Channel, Independent Closing: Racing a Fast Consumer Past a Silent One
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Tee channel: fan-out duplication of one input onto two outputs, with independent per-output delivery and completion tracking so each output closes on its own.

**Core idea.** Duplicating a value and closing an output are separate concerns. One goroutine that holds each value until both outputs take it duplicates correctly, but it couples each output's close to the slowest consumer. Each output needs its own delivery path and its own completion counter (one sync.WaitGroup or one forwarder queue per output), and each output needs its own closer goroutine.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The learner cannot see the interleaving. The single delivery goroutine parks on `out2 <- 0`. Therefore it never reads value 1, out1 receives only value 0 and then stops, and `defer close(out1)` cannot run. The test reports this only as a timeout on the second receive. The symptom looks like a misplaced close call, while the cause is the delivery shape. The learner also cannot see the cost of the per-value fix: one blocked goroutine accumulates per pending value on each unread output.

**Design.** Lanes run left to right over a step clock: sensor, delivery, out1 consumer, out2 consumer. Draw each channel as a slot between two lanes, and each value as a chip. A send turns green only when the sender and a receiver are ready in the same step; otherwise it turns amber with a "blocked" badge. Scene 1: the sensor emits 0, the delivery lane hands it into the out1 slot, sets its out1 handle to nil, then turns amber on the out2 slot; the sensor then turns amber pushing 1 into `in`, and out1's lane holds one chip with no close marker. Scene 2: press "Start out2"; chips move one value at a time, and the two close markers appear in the same step, after the last value. Scene 3: press "Close done"; both lanes end in one step and the pending chip drops. Scene 4: split the delivery lane in two, each with a counter; out1's counter hits 0 and its marker appears while out2's lane still shows a stack of five amber chips.

**Scenes.**
1. **Scaffold: the silent output freezes everything** — The given Tee runs with out2 never read. Sensor sends 0. The delivery goroutine's inner select accepts the out1 send, sets out1Ch=nil, then parks on `out2 <- 0`. The sensor now parks on `in <- 1`. out1 shows one value chip and no close marker. Blocked badges sit on the delivery send and on the sensor send.
2. **Scaffold: out2 starts late** — Press Start out2. The frozen send completes. Values 1..9 then move one at a time, each gated on out2 accepting it, so out1 gets the remaining values only as out2 catches up. Both close markers appear in the same step, after the loop reads EOF. out1's close waited for the slow consumer.
3. **Scaffold: done fires** — Press Close done while the delivery goroutine is blocked. The `<-done` case wins in the inner select. Both deferred closes run in the same step and the pending value drops. This part is already correct, and this scene separates it from the closing bug.
4. **Fixed: one closer per output (spoiler)** — Behind the solution reveal. The reader spawns one send goroutine per value per output. out1's counter reaches zero after value 4 and out1 closes while out2 holds five parked send goroutines. Start out2; its counter reaches zero and out2 closes. At value count 50, the parked-send stack for out2 grows to 50.

**Controls.** Play, Pause, Step and Restart, all keyboard reachable (Space, ArrowRight or S, R). Step is the default when prefers-reduced-motion is set.; Scene selector: Scaffold (silent out2), Scaffold (out2 late), Scaffold (done fires), Fixed shape (gated behind the solution reveal).; Start out2: releases the silent consumer's backlog in the current scaffold scene.; Close done: fires cancellation at the current step.; Show the fixed shape: visible only after the Solution tab's spoiler reveal; it is the only control that reveals the answer.; Value count 5 or 50 in the fixed scene: shows the blocked-send pile-up that the per-value design allows..

**Code anchors.**
- 14b-tee-channel-independent-close/main.go: the outer `select { case <-done: return; case v, ok = <-in: if !ok { return } }`.
- 14b-tee-channel-independent-close/main.go: `out1Ch, out2Ch := out1, out2` and `for out1Ch != nil || out2Ch != nil` with `case out1Ch <- v: out1Ch = nil`; this is the hold-until-both model.
- 14b-tee-channel-independent-close/main.go: `defer close(out1)` and `defer close(out2)`, which run only after the loop returns, so both outputs close together.
- 14b-tee-channel-independent-close/main.go (main): `time.Sleep(200 * time.Millisecond)` before `for v := range out2`; this is scene 1's symptom.
- 14b-tee-channel-independent-close/mocksensor.go: `SensorInterval = 5 * time.Millisecond`, plus StartSensor's timer select, send select and `defer close(out)`.
- solutions/14b-tee-channel-independent-close.md: `orDone`, `wgs := make([]sync.WaitGroup, 2)`, `wgs[i].Go(func(){ select { case <-done: case out[i] <- value: } })`, and the per-output closer `go func(i int) { wgs[i].Wait(); close(out[i]) }`.
- 14b-tee-channel-independent-close/check_test.go: TestTeeClosesEachOutputAsSoonAsItIsFullyDelivered, `receiveWithTimeout`'s `4 * SensorInterval` budget, and TestTeeDeliversToOneOutputWithoutWaitingOnTheOther, which the scaffold already passes.

<a id="16b-errgroup-withcontext"></a>
#### 16b — errgroup.WithContext: Cancel-on-First-Error
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** errgroup.WithContext: one derived cancelable context, cancelled by the first error and unconditionally by Wait, with cooperative Done-based fan-out.

**Core idea.** Done() is a broadcast channel, and cancel is its only writer. A group must derive one child context and cancel it from exactly two places: inside the same sync.Once that records firstErr, and unconditionally in Wait. Cancellation is cooperative: it wakes only the tasks that select on ctx.Done() and never kills a goroutine.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The sibling select looks correct, but in the scaffold ctx.Done() is nil, so that case is disabled and can never fire; four goroutines sit out their full 1 s after the failure they were written to hear. The 20 ms vs 1000 ms gap is in the output but not in the code shape. The success-path leak has no symptom at all: nothing fails, nothing looks wrong, and the derived context is never released. And "fail fast" reads like preemption, while a task that ignores ctx keeps running even though Done is closed.

**Design.** A five-lane timeline under one context node. Lane 0 fails at 20 ms. Lanes 1-4 each sit in a select whose two cases are "context done" and "1 s timer". In the scaffold the context node has no cancel handle and its Done channel is nil, so the drawn done case is dead and the lanes wait out the full second. Switch to the fixed implementation and the same 20 ms failure enters the once-gate, sets firstErr, calls cancel, closes Done, and one pulse reaches all four waiting lanes at once; they return context.Canceled and Wait returns at about 20 ms. A third scene removes the failure: every lane finishes, and Wait itself closes Done with nothing left to wake. A fourth adds a lane that never selects on ctx, to show that a closed channel does not stop a goroutine.

**Scenes.**
1. **Scaffold: Done never fires (default)** — Lanes 0-4 spawn at 0 virtual ms. Lane 0 returns an error at 20 ms. The once-gate records firstErr, but the context node has no cancel handle, so no pulse leaves it. Lanes 1-4 are blocked in select: their Done case is drawn dim with the label 'nil channel - never ready', and their 1 s timer case is live. At 1000 ms all four return nil and Wait returns the error. The elapsed badge reads about 1 s.
2. **Fixed: the first error broadcasts cancel** — Same launch, same 20 ms failure. Lane 0 enters the once-gate, sets firstErr and calls cancel in the same step. The Done channel closes and one pulse fans out to lanes 1-4. All four wake at 20 ms and return context.Canceled; their errors reach the once-gate and skip it, so Wait still returns task 0's error. wg reaches zero, Wait returns, and its own cancel call is a no-op. The elapsed badge reads under 100 ms.
3. **Fixed, all tasks succeed: Wait releases the context** — Lane 0 returns nil after 20 ms. No lane fails, so the once-gate stays idle and nothing cancels early. Lanes 1-4 finish on their 1 s timers. When wg reaches zero, Wait calls cancel anyway; Done closes and ctx.Err() shows context.Canceled, labelled 'release, not failure'. Nothing wakes, because every lane has exited.
4. **Cancel is cooperative, not preemptive** — A toggle makes one lane ignore ctx and sleep to 1000 ms. The failure at 20 ms cancels the context and wakes the three lanes that select on it, but the ignoring lane keeps running and returns nil at 1000 ms. Wait still blocks until that lane exits. The label reads: 'cancel closes a channel; it does not kill a goroutine'.

**Controls.** Play or pause the scripted timeline (Space).; Step back and step forward by one event, so a paused frame is exact (arrow keys).; Restart to event 0 (Home).; Implementation switch: Scaffold (HEAD) or Fixed. This is the one difference the learner must find.; Scenario switch: first error, all tasks succeed, or one lane ignores ctx.; Scrubber over the event list, not over wall-clock, so the 20 ms and 1000 ms events are both reachable.; Reduced motion: the component opens paused at event 0, never autoplays, and uses the same step controls..

**Code anchors.**

- main.go lines 87-97: Go records firstErr under errOnce and calls nothing else. The once-gate frame has no outgoing pulse.

<a id="24-priority-worker-pool"></a>
#### 24 — Priority Worker Pool: Weighted Scheduling
`Tier C` · **nice** · heap / queue · family `heap` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Priority scheduling over a mutex-guarded heap: one worker parks on sync.Cond and, at each pick, pops the highest-Priority job currently queued, with ties broken by arrival sequence (container/heap + sync.Cond).

**Core idea.** Priority only exists if the waiting set can be reordered. A channel freezes a value's position at send time, so the backlog must live in a structure the scheduler re-sorts on every push (a heap); the worker takes the root at the moment it becomes free, and that take is atomic with a concurrent Submit only because the empty-check and the Pop run under the same mutex.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The prose says "a channel can't reorder", but it never shows when the decision is made. A p10 job submitted during a 20 ms job cannot preempt it; it only becomes the next pick, so the jump happens at the pick, not at the submit. The prose also hides the cost of an empty queue: the worker must park (cond.Wait releases the mutex) and be woken by a Signal that comes after the push, or the wake-up is lost and a queued job never runs. And it hides that the pick is atomic only because len(queue)==0 and heap.Pop share one lock span; submit and pick can interleave around it.

**Design.** One worker box on the left, one waiting-set panel in the middle, and a completion strip along the bottom. The waiting set changes nature with the scenario: in "as shipped" it is a rigid row of channel slots and each job drops into the right end and never moves; in "fixed" it is a small heap tree sitting above its backing array, and every push re-shuffles positions in front of the learner. The worker box is either busy (a 20 ms progress bar on the running job) or parked (dimmed, tagged "blocked in cond.Wait, mutex released"). Each submit plays in two beats: the job enters, then, in fixed mode, it bubbles up to the root. The completion strip fills left to right and is the only thing the tests read, so it stays visible in every scene.

**Scenes.**
1. **Backlog in a channel (default, as shipped)** — Job 1 (p1) is running (20 ms bar). Jobs 2-6 (p1) drop into the channel in send order, then job 7 (p10) drops into the tail with a bright p10 badge. Its badge is visible the whole time and changes nothing. The completion strip fills 1,2,3,4,5,6,7 and a badge reads 'TestSchedulerPrioritizesUrgentJobs: FAIL, job 7 at position 6'. Annotation: 'a send freezes the position of the value in the channel'.
2. **Same jobs, heap root (fixed)** — Identical input and identical clock, but the six waiting jobs sit in a heap. On push, job 7 sifts up to index 0 in one animated beat. The pick only happens when job 1's bar ends; the worker pops the root then. Completion strip: 1,7,2,3,4,5,6, badge 'PASS, job 7 at position 1'. The scene repeats that job 1 was never interrupted.
3. **Empty queue: park and wake (fixed, bug toggle)** — The queue drains; the worker enters cond.Wait and the mutex badge flips to 'released'. Submit locks, pushes, unlocks, then signals; the worker wakes and re-checks len(queue)==0 in the for loop, so a wake with no job just parks again. The scene-local toggle 'signal before push' produces the real stall: the worker wakes, finds the queue empty, parks, and the job sits queued while the strip stays empty until the simulated 2 s timeout fires, mirroring the test's time.After safety net.
4. **Equal priorities: seq decides (fixed, bug toggle)** — Five p5 jobs arrive; each carries its seq badge (0..4). The heap sifts, but on pop the root is always the lowest seq among equals, so the strip fills 1,2,3,4,5. The scene-local toggle 'Less ignores arrival order' makes the heap pop equal jobs in internal array order (for example 1,3,2,5,4) and TestSchedulerTiesBrokenByArrivalOrder fails.

**Controls.** Play / Pause (Space); Step one event (Right arrow); Restart (R); Scenario switch: 'as shipped (FIFO channel)' | 'fixed (heap + cond)'. Default is as shipped.; Submit an urgent job (p10) now: injects the job at the current event, so it lands at the channel tail in one scenario and at the heap root in the other; Scene 3 toggle: signal before push (lost wake-up); Scene 4 toggle: Less ignores arrival order (heap pops ties arbitrarily); Scene picker: Backlog / Heap root / Park and wake / Ties; A visible aria-live status line that states each event in words (e.g. 'Submit job 7, priority 10; sift-up to index 0'), so no state depends on colour or motion.
