<a id="30-livelock-starvation"></a>
#### 30 — Livelock & Starvation: Two Failure Modes Beyond Deadlock
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort L · README diagram: **no — draw it first**

**Pattern.** Livelock through phase-locked retry with an identical fixed backoff, plus starvation through strict priority with no aging.

**Core idea.** Progress is completed work, not activity. Two goroutines can stay 100% busy in perfect lockstep and finish nothing, and a dispatcher can complete one job every cycle while one queue never moves. Lockstep breaks only when the retry delay is not shared; starvation breaks only when a fairness rule is checked before the privileged branch.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The phase relationship. The prose says both workers back off for the same 10ms, but it never shows why that re-aligns them: the two probeWindow sleeps put both announce marks and both read marks at the same instants, so every read really does sample contenders=2, and the equal backoff lands both wake-ups on the same tick again. The learner sees two busy lanes and the test message "0/50 attempts of progress" (check_test.go TestLivelockBothWorkersMakeProgress), but not the interleaving that produced it. In Part 2 the learner sees high=10 low=0 and cannot tell whether the low job was never queued, dropped, or passed over every cycle; only a waiting counter on that one chip shows it was passed over.

**Design.** Two horizontal lanes, A and B, share one narrow column labelled "contenders", which is the atomic counter. Each round draws four ticks per lane: announce, probeWindow sleep, read, probeWindow sleep, withdraw. The lanes are aligned so a reader sees both reads land on the same instant and both sample 2. The progress counters stay at 0 while an activity strip along the bottom stays full, so the pane states the paradox directly: nothing is blocked, nothing advances. Part 2 turns the same lanes into a high queue and a low queue beside a vertical cycle ticker. One low-priority chip sits at the head of its lane with a waiting counter that climbs each cycle, while a "work done this cycle" pulse fires every cycle and the high queue loses one job. No panel shows CPU or throughput graphs; every mark is one line of the scaffold's loop.

**Scenes.**
1. **Lockstep: busy, zero progress** — Default scene, Part 1 scaffold. Both lanes announce, both sleep one probeWindow, both read n=2, both sleep, both withdraw, both sleep the identical 10ms. The four marks sit at the same x position every round. Progress stays 0/5 and 0/5 while the activity strip is full. A caption reads 'nobody is blocked; nobody advances'. Each round flashes the word collision at the same point. No jitter and no fix appear.
2. **Jitter: phases drift apart** — Behind the same reveal as the Solution tab. Same protocol, but A's backoff is 14ms and B's is 11ms. Round 2 announce marks no longer coincide; by round 3 one lane announces alone and reads n=1, wins, and its progress ticks. Both lanes reach 5 and the pane shows the two backoff bars with visibly different lengths, which is the only difference from scene 1.
3. **Strict priority: busy cycles, starved low** — Default scene, Part 2 scaffold. A cycle ticker counts 1 to 10. The high queue starts at 20 and loses one job per cycle; every cycle fires the 'work done' pulse, so the system is plainly productive. One low chip waits at the head of the low lane; its waiting counter climbs 1, 2, ... 10 and turns red at 10. The completion counters read high=10 low=0.
4. **Aging: every 5th cycle is low's turn** — Behind the reveal. The ticker marks cycles 5 and 10 as aging turns. On cycle 5 the low chip moves to the top and completes before the high queue is consulted. Counters keep the 2:1 shape. With the aging period set to 2 the low share rises to half and the majority-share rule fails; at 11 the low chip passes cycle 10 and the bounded-wait rule fails.

**Controls.** Play / pause; Step one event backward or forward; Restart; Part 1 backoff: shared fixed 10ms (scaffold) or per-worker jitter (fix); Part 2 aging period: off / 2 / 5 / 11; High backlog depth: 2 or 100, which shows why the strict policy only starves when the backlog never drains and why the test uses a 100-job backlog.

**Code anchors.**
- 30-livelock-starvation/main.go: RunLivelockDemo steps 1 to 5 (atomic.AddInt32 announce, time.Sleep(probeWindow), atomic.LoadInt32 read, second sleep, withdraw)
- 30-livelock-starvation/main.go: const fixedBackoff = 10 * time.Millisecond and its comment
- 30-livelock-starvation/main.go: retryBudgetMultiplier escape hatch that ends the churn
- 30-livelock-starvation/main.go: Dispatcher.RunDispatchCycles, the 'if len(d.high) > 0' branch that never ages
- 30-livelock-starvation/check_test.go: TestLivelockBothWorkersMakeProgress (floor = attempts/4, minRoundOverhead)
- 30-livelock-starvation/check_test.go: maxLowWaitCycles = 10 in TestDispatcherLowPriorityBoundedWait, and 'highCompleted < 2*lowCompleted' in TestDispatcherHighPriorityKeepsMajorityShare
- solutions/30-livelock-starvation.md: per-worker rand.New(rand.NewSource(1)) and (2), jitterSpan, agingPeriod = 5, and the aging check placed before the high branch

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Fix-scene timings are invented, not the solution's. With the documented seeds (rand.NewSource(1) and (2)), the first backoffs are A=17.77941ms and B=13.358511ms. A synctest trace of the HEAD solution shows: both announce at 0ms, both read n=2 at 1ms, both collide at 2ms; B alone at 15.359ms; B wins at 17.359ms and 19.359ms; A wakes at 19.779ms and collides again; both reach 5/5 at 62.079ms. The proposal says 'A 14ms, B 11ms' and 'by round 3 one lane announces alone'. Neither matches, so a learner comparing with the Solution tab sees different numbers.
- The starvation backlog is wrong. check_test.go:81 sets `const highBacklog = maxLowWaitCycles * 100`, which is 1000, not 100. The control ('High backlog depth: 2 or 100') and the risk text ('the test uses a 100-job backlog') are both off by 10x.
- Scene 1 says 'four ticks per lane' then lists five marks: announce, probeWindow sleep, read, probeWindow sleep, withdraw. main.go:43-49 has exactly those five events.
- The spoiler rationale contradicts the exercise. 30-livelock-starvation/README.md already says 'The fix: make step 5's backoff independently randomized per worker' and 'The fix — make it fair: ... aging ... within 10 cycles ... at least two-thirds'. A third tab that shows jitter or aging therefore hands over nothing the Problem tab does not already state. Worse, the design as written puts 'per-worker jitter (fix)' and 'aging period: off / 2 / 5 / 11' controls on the default scene. spoiler_safe is true only because the README already spoils the concept; the proposed gate adds a reveal mechanism that protects nothing new.
- The tab plumbing is understated. `tab` is a two-value union in router.ts:4-18 (parseRoute, routePath), App.tsx:30, ExerciseView.tsx:11 and DocPane.tsx:11. A `visualise` tab needs a route case, a widened union in all four files, and a decision about whether the tab appears on all 43 exercises. An empty tab everywhere is worse than a registry map keyed by exercise id.
- The argument against an inline block uses a false mechanism. A theme change calls listeners and re-colorizes Markdown code (theme.ts:22-30, Markdown.tsx effect on `html`); it does not remount DocPane, so an inline React widget would keep its state. The real reasons to use a tab are UX and the fact that a widget inside the markdown needs a new directive. Conversely, DocPane renders its body conditionally, so the visualisation unmounts and loses play state on every tab switch unless it is kept mounted and hidden.
- The 320px problem needs a fixed rule, not a warning. With 12px side padding (.doc-tabs, styles.css:540), three labels (Problem ~62px, Solution ~70px, Visualise ~78px), the gaps and the difficulty badge (~62px), the row is about 304px; the passed badge (~62px) pushes it to about 366px. The row overflows only once the status is passed, which is the worst time to break the layout. Hide the difficulty badge below ~420px or wrap the badges.
- The Part 2 counter claim is wrong. Aging period 5 gives 4 high per 1 low in steady state (9/1 over the 10-cycle demo with one low job), and the test is the floor `highCompleted < 2*lowCompleted` (check_test.go:125), not a 2:1 ratio.
- Value is overstated relative to the repo's own remedy. The failing messages already print 'worker A only made 0/50 attempts of progress' and 'low-priority job was starved: 0 completions'. tools/check/lint.go:50 already lists `readme-diagram 30-livelock-starvation: backlog`, so the repo's planned fix is an ASCII timeline in this README. A static two-lane timeline covers the phase insight; Part 2 is a 10-row table. The only capability prose cannot copy is the aging-period sweep against the two test bounds (2 fails the share, 5 passes, 11 misses the bound) plus the backlog-drain toggle. That is a 'nice', not a 'high'.
- 'The activity strip stays full' is not program output. main.go:135 prints only 'a=%d b=%d'; the busy-ness must be labelled as illustration, or a reader will look for it in the console and not find it.

**Why this tier.** Livelock and starvation are schedules over time; L effort, no README diagram, and the prose already states both fixes.

<a id="32-fanout-deadline-race"></a>
#### 32 — Racing a Fan-Out Against a Deadline
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Fan-out to four independent workers writing disjoint fields of one shared struct, waited on by a sync.WaitGroup, raced against a context deadline; early return while workers are still writing (post-return write / "leak" question for the abandoned goroutines and the helper goroutine).

**Core idea.** A fan-out that waits on sync.WaitGroup cannot see a deadline, because wg.Wait() is not a channel and cannot appear in a select. To respect ctx you must turn "all four finished" into a channel event (a helper goroutine closes done, or four sends land on a channel buffered to exactly 4) and select it against ctx.Done(). When the deadline wins, the workers are still writing into result, so the only safe return is nil, ctx.Err() — not a pointer into a struct another goroutine will write 400ms later.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** Three things the prose and tests cannot make visible. (1) The broken scaffold passes the happy-path test (main.go:94 wg.Wait() is fine when every component is 20ms), so the bug exists only as one failing assertion line; the learner never sees the 400ms of wall clock where Construct sat blocked on a timer nobody was waiting for. (2) After an early return, three workers are already done and history is still writing result.History at t=500ms; a wrong fix that returns &result hands the caller a pointer into a struct being written with no happens-before edge. No test catches this: check_test.go's concurrent test discards the result with `_, _ = Construct(...)`, so even -race sees nothing. (3) Bailing out stops waiting, not the workers; the helper goroutine also lives to t=500ms, and in the channel-signal variant an unbuffered done leaves abandoned sends blocked forever. None of these are observable from a test pass/fail line.

**Design.** A horizontal wall clock from 0 to 600ms, with a vertical dashed line at the 100ms deadline. Five horizontal lanes stack vertically — Construct (the caller), basic, shipping, refund, history — and a sixth lane appears in the fixed run for the helper goroutine that calls wg.Wait(). Each worker is a bar that ends in a small write glyph feeding a Result card pinned at the right edge: Basic, Shipping and Refund fill at 20ms; History stays empty until 500ms, long after the dashed line has scrolled past it. In the scaffold, the Construct lane carries one long wg.Wait() block that runs through the deadline and ends at 500ms, so the return arrow leaves at 500ms with a green "no error" flag. In the fixed run, a ctx.Done() pulse and a close(done) pulse race at the right edge of the caller lane: ctx fires at 100ms, the return arrow leaves with a red DeadlineExceeded flag, and History's bar keeps moving and writes at 500ms. An elapsed-time readout under the axis keeps counting after the return, so the background write is visibly after the end of the function.

**Scenes.**
1. **Happy path — why the bug hides** — Deadline 2s, all four components 20ms. The four lanes start together, all finish at 20ms, the Result card fills all four fields, wg.Wait() returns, and Construct returns &result, nil at 20ms. A note: TestConstructReturnsAllFieldsWithAmpleDeadline passes on the broken scaffold, so the large deadline is exactly the case that hides the bug.
2. **Scaffold — the deadline is ignored (default)** — Deadline 100ms, history 500ms. basic, shipping and refund end at 20ms and their fields light up; the dashed 100ms line crosses every lane and nothing reacts; the Construct lane stays blocked inside one wg.Wait() block until 500ms, then returns &result with no error. The elapsed readout shows 500ms against a 100ms budget. This is the symptom, and it is the scene the tab opens on.
3. **Hazard — returning the struct too early** — Clearly labelled 'a wrong bail-out', not the scaffold: Construct leaves at 100ms and hands back &result while history is still mid-write. At 500ms the History field write glyph and the caller's read glyph land in the same column, flagged 'unsynchronized read of result'. A footnote: no test in check_test.go catches this, because the concurrent test throws the pointer away.
4. **Fixed — done vs ctx.Done** — The helper-goroutine lane calls wg.Wait() and closes done at 500ms; ctx.Done() fires at 100ms; the select picks ctx first and Construct returns nil, DeadlineExceeded at 100ms. history keeps running to 500ms and writes into a result nobody returns; the helper goroutine closes done with no receiver and exits. The elapsed readout shows the 100ms return, and a separate 'background' counter keeps running to 500ms. This scene is gated behind the tab's spoiler reveal.

**Controls.** Play / pause / step / restart, all keyboard-operable (Space, arrow keys, Home); a timeline scrubber is the static mode and is mandatory under prefers-reduced-motion.; Deadline slider: 20ms, 100ms, 300ms, 2s — the same code passes or fails depending only on the caller's budget.; History latency slider: 20ms or 500ms — makes the failing branch appear and disappear.; Mode switch: 'Scaffold' (default) vs 'Race the deadline' (fixed run) — the fixed mode is locked behind the Solution spoiler reveal.; Toggle: 'Return &result on bail-out' — turns scene 3 on and shows the post-return write/read collision.; Optional: completion-channel buffer 0 / 4 — in the channel-signal variant, buffer 0 leaves the abandoned sends blocked forever (a leak label), buffer 4 lets every worker exit..

**Code anchors.**
- main.go:76 wg.Add(4) and main.go:77-92 the four go func bodies with result.Basic = basic() ... result.History = history()
- main.go:94 wg.Wait() — the unconditional block that ignores ctx
- main.go:95 return &result, nil — a pointer returned with no deadline check
- main.go:99 context.WithTimeout(..., 100*time.Millisecond) — the deadline
- main.go:112-115 stalled component sleeping 500ms — the overdue branch
- main.go:118 the Construct call and main.go:119-126 the elapsed-time print that shows the symptom
- main.go:130 time.Sleep(500*time.Millisecond) — waiting for the still-running history goroutine
- mockcomponent.go Component(): time.Sleep(m.latency) then m.completed = true — ctx-blind work that always runs to completion
- check_test.go budget = 150*time.Millisecond and history.RanToCompletion() — the two assertions the timeline must satisfy
- check_test.go concurrent test's `_, _ = Construct(...)` — why the wrong bail-out passes every test
- Solution Approach 1: done := make(chan struct{}), go func(){ wg.Wait(); close(done) }(), select { case <-done: ...; case <-ctx.Done(): return nil, ctx.Err() }
- Solution Approach 2: done := make(chan struct{}, 4) and the for i := 0; i < 4; i++ select loop

TestConstructRespectsDeadlineWhenAComponentStalls fails any non-nil Result on the deadline path at check_test.go:82-86 (`if got != nil { t.Errorf(...) }`). Only TestConstructConcurrentSafety:132 discards the pointer (`_, _ = Construct(...)`). The scaffold's main() prints the elapsed time (main.go:119-126, 'Construct succeeded after 500ms'), and the deadline test's Fatalf message includes '(took %s)' (check_test.go:75-78), which is the exact 100ms-vs-500ms gap. What is unobservable is only the post-return write sequence, and that belongs to a hypothetical wrong fix, not the scaffold.

Today two tabs plus the badge already nearly fill the row. A third tab needs a shorter label ('Demo'), a wrapping strip, or the badge moved into the body.

A 20ms bar on a 600ms axis is then 3-5px, and the 100ms deadline line sits ~17-28px from the origin. Drop the pinned card and merge the three identical 20ms lanes; otherwise the scene reads as noise.
- DEADLINE OPTION 20ms IS A TIE: the fast components take 20ms (main.go:104), and the slider offers a 20ms deadline (main.go:99 is the 100ms demo deadline). A select between done and ctx.Done at the same instant is nondeterministic, so a deterministic scripted explainer cannot show one outcome honestly. Use 50ms instead of 20ms.
- SCRIPT DRIFT IS UNGUARDED: the 100ms/20ms/500ms constants must be hand-copied into web/src, because the generator reads only .go, .md and go.mod (web/tools/gen/main.go:485-487) and the layout rule forbids new files in the exercise folder (tools/check/lint.go:154). No check ties the script to main.go:99/104/113, so a scaffold edit silently desynchronises the tab.

The timeline adds the temporal ordering of return-before-write only, and the most vivid scene is a hypothetical wrong fix. 'high' overstates; 'nice' fits.

<a id="33-load-balancer"></a>
#### 33 — Load Balancer: Self-Scheduling Workers That Report Their Own Load
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Self-scheduling worker pool: a min-heap of workers keyed by pending load, with each worker reporting completion back on a shared done channel (Rob Pike's load balancer).

**Core idea.** The pool's ranking is only as true as its updates. A worker that finishes must be re-ranked on the same single dispatch loop that reads the pool (done -> pending-- -> heap.Fix), or the heap keeps advertising a frozen worker as the least loaded and the whole balancer wedges.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** That the failure is silent for the first burst, and that its shape depends on the inbox buffer. Verified against the committed scaffold: with requestBacklogPerWorker=16 and 3 workers, the first 51 requests all dispatch; each worker runs exactly one request, delivers its result, then freezes on done <- w, and every later request sits in a buffered inbox. The symptom is 9 of 12 submitters timing out at check_test.go:109 ("request N never completed") while Balance is healthy at <-work -- not stuck. The README's diagram says w.requests <- req blocks immediately, but that only becomes true at about request 52: I measured 52 dispatched and 68 never dispatched out of 120. So the learner cannot see (a) the pool-size threshold at which it wedges, (b) that the buffer hides phase A, or (c) that there are two distinct wedge shapes (worker blocked on done; then Balance blocked on the inbox send, cascading to work senders).

**Design.** One frame shows the Balancer goroutine, a work intake above it, and three Worker lanes, each with an inbox gauge (capacity 16, filled dots) and a status chip: parked on receive, running request n, result delivered, or blocked on done. Above the lanes sits the pool as a ranked strip ordered by pending, each entry showing the worker, its pending count and its heap index. As the clock advances, a request slides from work into the least-pending worker's inbox and its pending count rises; the worker runs it, its result flies to the submitter, then it tries done <- w -- and nobody is receiving, so its lane locks with a red blocked-send chip. The first three requests still complete, so the frame looks healthy; the learner has to advance to the fourth, fifth and sixth request to see them land in inboxes that will never be read while the pool strip keeps claiming every worker is busy. A burst control pushes past the buffer, at which point the 52nd request finds a full inbox, Balance's own lane freezes on w.requests <- req, the work senders queue behind it, and every lane is blocked -- the exact picture the README draws. A gated final scene adds the done case, decrements pending and animates heap.Fix sifting the worker to its true rank, so the next request goes to whoever is actually free.

**Scenes.**
1. **A burst that fits the pool** — Three requests, three workers. Each worker takes one, delivers its result, then blocks on done <- w with no receiver. Every request completes anyway, so the frame looks healthy -- this is why the bug is invisible under light load and why the sanity test passes.
2. **One request too many** — Request 4 arrives. The pool strip still says every worker has pending 1 because nothing decrements it, so heap.Pop returns a worker already frozen on its done send. The request queues in the inbox buffer and its submitter waits; the 2s timeout fires 'request N never completed' while Balance sits healthy on <-work. Requests 5-12 repeat the pattern, and the heap ordering is shown as a lie.
3. **Fill the inboxes** — Burst raised past 3 x (16+1) = 51. The 52nd dispatch finds a full inbox, so Balance's single lane freezes on w.requests <- req; work senders queue behind it, done reports pile up, and every lane shows blocked. This is the only phase where the README's diagram is literally true.
4. **Drain done (gated reveal)** — The select gains case w := <-b.done: pending decrements, heap.Fix animates the worker to its true rank, its lane unlocks, and the next request goes to it. All requests complete and the pool strip never lies again.

**Controls.** Play / Pause / Step / Restart (Space, arrow keys, R) -- required to inspect a frozen frame.; Burst size: 3 (fits the pool) / 12 (the test's traffic) / 60 (fills the inboxes) -- the one control that teaches, because it shows the buffer hiding phase A and where phase B starts.; Apply the done case (behind a reveal, matching the Solution tab's gate) -- shows pending-- and heap.Fix reordering.; Optional: follow one request toggle, highlighting a single request's path from work through inbox to result, to make the stranded ones obvious..

**Code anchors.**

- 33-load-balancer/main.go:146-151 -- dispatch: heap.Pop at 147, buffered send at 148, pending++ at 149, heap.Push at 150
- 33-load-balancer/main.go:69-72 -- Worker.work: result delivered at 71, done <- w at 72
- 33-load-balancer/main.go:119 -- requestBacklogPerWorker = 16, the buffer that hides phase A
- 33-load-balancer/main.go:81 -- Pool.Less orders by pending, the key that goes stale
- 33-load-balancer/main.go:153-155 -- main(): 3 workers, 12 requests
- 33-load-balancer/check_test.go:97-130 -- TestBalancerSurvivesMoreRequestsThanWorkers; the 'never completed' failure at 109
- solutions/33-load-balancer.md:31-41 -- the select with both cases and heap.Fix

That is false for the test's traffic. I measured 52 work sends accepted and 68 blocked out of 120 with 3 workers and cap 16, across 5 trials. At the test's 12 requests all 12 dispatch: 3 complete, 9 time out at check_test.go:109, and Balance sits idle on `<-work`, not stuck. Correct this README diagram before building a visualisation.
- Citation error. solutions/33-load-balancer.md:31-41 is the failure output and prose, not the fix. The select with `pending--` and `heap.Fix` is at lines 46-55. The test function is check_test.go:72-133, not 97-130; line 109 is correct.

<a id="33b-load-balancer-nonblocking-dispatch"></a>
#### 33b — Non-Blocking Dispatch: A Load Balancer That Can't Depend on a Buffer
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Load balancer with one dispatcher goroutine: a min-heap of workers ordered by pending load, where dispatch must become a conditionally-enabled select case (nil-channel idiom) instead of a blocking send made outside the select.

**Core idea.** A goroutine that both sends to and receives from the others can never afford to block on the send. The instant Balance parks on w.requests &lt;- req it stops evaluating its select, so no Worker can deliver done &lt;- w and the whole pool is frozen by one send. Dispatch has to be a case inside the same select, enabled only while the target Worker is actually parked on a receive.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The learner can see "balancer looks wedged" in test output but not the causal chain inside one goroutine: the send is a statement after the chosen select arm, not a case, so it can block forever while the very receive that would free everyone (b.done) sits unreachable a few lines below. They also cannot see that a different Worker's completion is what would have unblocked the stuck one — the pool fails as a unit, not per worker — and that the tempting fix (`go func() { w.requests &lt;- req }()`) passes every functional test and leaks one goroutine per queued request, visible only as runtime.NumGoroutine.

**Design.** A narrow vertical stack drawn in the doc pane. At the top, one wide lane for Balance with a three-gate strip: work | dispatch | done. Below it, three worker lanes, each a rounded chip showing a state word (waiting / running / reporting). On the left of Balance, a thin backlog strip; beside the lanes, a heap strip listing workers in load order. Requests travel as small tokens along arrows that are labelled with the channel they use (work, w.requests, b.done). In the wedge scene: Balance's three gates all greys out the moment it commits to w.requests &lt;- req, a red blocked marker sits on that arrow, the target worker runs a job, then a second red marker appears on its done &lt;- w arrow pointing at a dispatcher that is not listening; two other workers finish and their done arrows stack behind the first. Meanwhile request tokens queue on the left edge of the work arrow, and a virtual clock climbs to the test timeouts. Nothing ever moves again — the stillness is the lesson. In the fixed scene the same layout gains one idea: the dispatch gate is drawn dimmed and unlabelled ("nil") when the backlog is empty, and lights with an arrow aimed at b.pool[0] exactly when that worker's receive arrow appears; the send and the receive complete in the same frame, the backlog head disappears, pending increments, and the heap strip reorders. Balance's gates never grey out. A code panel under the diagram highlights the one scaffold line or solution line currently executing.

**Scenes.**
1. **Fresh pool: the burst that still passes** — Three workers parked on receive. Three requests arrive one at a time; each pop returns the least-loaded worker, the send finds a parked receiver and completes in the same frame, pending increments, heap.Fix reorders the heap strip, the worker lane goes running then reporting. The learner sees that the broken loop is only broken sometimes, and why TestBalancerCompletesABurstNoBiggerThanThePool passes on the scaffold.
2. **The wedge: one busy worker freezes the pool (DEFAULT)** — Worker 1 is mid-job when the next request lands on it. Balance pops it, commits to w.requests <- req, and its three gates greys out — the select is not being evaluated. Worker 1 finishes and blocks on done <- w. Workers 0 and 2 finish and stack their done sends behind it. New requests block on work. The virtual clock reaches 2s and the test-name callouts light in order (BackToBackRequestsOnOneWorker, MoreRequestsThanWorkers). The frame is frozen and stays frozen.
3. **The fix: a dispatch case that switches itself off (behind a reveal)** — Backlog strip and three-gate strip appear. Step by step: backlog empty, so dispatch is nil and only work and done are live; a request arrives and lands in backlog, nothing is dispatched in the same iteration; the least-loaded worker parks on receive, and only then does the dispatch gate light with an arrow at b.pool[0]; the send and the receive complete in one frame, backlog head drops, pending++, heap.Fix; a worker reports done, pending--, heap.Fix, and the gate re-aims at the new b.pool[0]. The learner sees that the readiness question is answered by select itself, not by a pending threshold.
4. **The near-miss: one parked goroutine per send (gated with scene 3)** — Dispatch style switched to `go func() { w.requests <- req }()`. Balance's gates stay live and every request eventually completes, but the goroutine counter climbs with each queued request and stays high while workers are busy; ghost lanes sprout under each busy worker. Toggling back to the backlog keeps the counter flat. This is the only view that makes TestBalancerQueuesWithoutASeparateGoroutinePerRequest legible.

**Controls.** Play / Pause / Step forward / Step back / Restart, all real buttons with keyboard focus and shortcuts, nothing on hover; Submit one more request: injects a token into work at the current step, so the learner can make the wedge happen on demand; Worker state: make the target worker busy / idle, to show the wedge needs a worker that is not parked at the instant of dispatch; Buffer capacity 0 vs 3, labelled as "what 33's buffer absorbed (counterfactual)"; capacity 0 stays pinned as the tested constraint; Dispatch style (fixed scene only): backlog slice vs goroutine per send, with a live goroutine counter that stays flat or climbs.

**Code anchors.**

- 33b-load-balancer-nonblocking-dispatch/main.go:89-93 — Worker.work; `done <- w` (line 92) is the second blocked send that turns one stuck dispatch into a pool-wide freeze.

- 33b-load-balancer-nonblocking-dispatch/main.go:153, 155-156 — heap.Pop / pending++ / heap.Push, the pair the fix replaces with a peek plus heap.Fix.

- solutions/33b-load-balancer-nonblocking-dispatch.md:10 — `dispatch, head = w.requests, backlog[0]` after peeking b.pool[0].

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- The verdict overstates the blind spot. 33b-load-balancer-nonblocking-dispatch/README.md:32-61 already gives the causal chain in four numbered steps AND draws it as an ASCII diagram (lines 50-61). The solution doc repeats the same chain. Any learner who reads the exercise already sees what the animation claims to reveal; the animation adds retention, not new facts.

The visual adds a live counter, not visibility.
- The scaffold does not have a dispatch case. main.go:151-160 has two select cases (work at 152, done at 158); line 154 'w.requests <- req' is a statement inside the work arm, after the select is already chosen. Scene 2's 'three gates all grey out' invents a dispatch case and misstates the bug. Scaffold mode must draw two gates plus a separate blocking send; only the fixed mode has three gates.
- Scene 3 contradicts the proposal's own risk note. 'The least-loaded worker parks on receive, and only then does the dispatch gate light' depicts probe-then-send. The fix arms the dispatch case as soon as the backlog is non-empty (solution doc lines 59-62) and lets select answer readiness (lines 113-119). Draw the gate armed while no receiver is parked, then firing in the same frame a receiver appears.
- The solution-doc highlight numbers are block-relative, not file lines. The cited 'solutions/33b...md:5,10,17' are the fence specification {5,10,17} of the code block that starts at file line 51; the real file lines are 56, 61 and 68-71. A highlight model keyed to file:line will point at prose.
- The width target is wrong. The doc pane floor is 260px (web/src/styles.css:506, minmax(260px, var(--doc-frac, 40%))), and a 320px phone leaves about 288px of content after .doc-body padding (styles.css:986-1006). The widget must be designed for 260px, not 320px.
- A third tab overflows the tab bar at that width. .doc-tabs (styles.css:540-560) already holds Problem, Solution, a difficulty badge and optionally a passed badge; .spacer is flex:1 (styles.css:134-136) and the anchors cannot shrink below their text. A third ~54px anchor plus gap exceeds 260px. The tab also needs a per-exercise registry and conditional render, so 'one anchor' undercounts the work, and the tab would otherwise appear on all 43 exercises.
- Interactive controls break the scripted-deterministic model. 'Submit one more request' and 'Worker state' turn the timeline into a branchy state machine and double the test surface. Drop them; scene selection plus play/pause/step/restart is enough.
- The buffer 0-vs-3 control teaches the rejected fix. check_test.go:20-29 forbids capacity > 0, and README.md:63-66 says a buffer only pushes the wedge to a larger burst. Showing capacity 3 as relief contradicts the exercise. Drop it, or show the same wedge with a bigger burst.
- The code panel duplicates the docs. The Problem tab already quotes the scaffold loop with a line band, and the Solution tab quotes the fix with bands (solution doc lines 51-79). A third copy in a 260px pane spends the tightest dimension on repetition. Use one caption line per step ('main.go:154 w.requests <- req').
- Heap order as lane order harms identity. If workers reorder whenever pending changes, Worker 0/1/2 jump during the animation and jump abruptly under reduced motion, so the learner loses track of which lane is which. Keep fixed lane positions and show heap order as a small numbered strip or a 'next' marker.
- Accessibility is incomplete. The step note must be visible text and announced (aria-live="polite"), with the diagram aria-hidden. Keyboard buttons are fine, but a screen-reader user needs the note to carry the state; colour-only red/green markers are not enough.
- Spoiler safety is mostly honest but not fully. Default scene 2 (the wedge) is safe because the README already describes it. However the visible buffer counterfactual and any visible scene names leak the fix. Gate scenes 3-4, the buffer control and the scene names behind the same reveal.

**Why this tier.** Two gates and a nil-channel select; subtle, but the scaffold's shape must be scripted exactly.

<a id="35-singleflight"></a>
#### 35 — Your Own singleflight: Deduping In-Flight Duplicate Calls
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** singleflight / request coalescing: a mutex-guarded per-key map of in-flight calls, each call joined by its own sync.WaitGroup, deleted from the map at completion.

**Core idea.** While one call for a key is in flight, every duplicate must ride that one call and share its value and error; the moment it finishes, the key is forgotten, so the next call starts fresh.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The in-flight window is invisible. The leader runs fn with the mutex released (solution lines 66-68), so duplicate callers never queue on the lock; they park on the per-call WaitGroup and wake together at Done. The learner also cannot see the two ordering rules that make the pattern safe: publish the entry with Add(1) before unlock, and delete it after Done. A visualisation that makes those blocked windows and that one delete moment visible teaches what four tests can only assert as counts and booleans.

**Design.** A narrow vertical scene in the doc pane. Each caller gets a horizontal lane; a shared "Group.m map" row and a single "backend Call" lane sit below them. Time runs left to right, with a 150 ms scale marked at both ends. In the default scene three lanes run their own calls in parallel and three arrows land on the backend, which stamps "CallCount = 3"; every lane returns a red shared=false badge. In the fixed scene the first lane takes a brief lock, drops a call box into the map row, releases the lock, and runs fn in a wide shaded span labelled "mutex free - in-flight window"; lanes 2 and 3 lock, find the box, release, and become parked bars on that box's WaitGroup. At 150 ms the bars collapse at once on Done, and the leader removes the box from the map. A fourth lane arriving after removal finds an empty map and starts a new call. The map row is the star: one box exists, then none.

**Scenes.**
1. **Today: three cold calls** — Three caller lanes each invoke fn directly. Three arrows reach the backend and CallCount climbs 1, 2, 3. All three return shared=false. A caption states the symptom: shared is false for the worst reason, because nothing was shared. All three finish at about 150 ms, so the cost is backend work, not wall-clock delay.
2. **One leader, two riders** — Caller 1 locks, misses the map, inserts its call box with the WaitGroup count set, unlocks, and runs fn across the shaded in-flight window. Callers 2 and 3 arrive during that window, hit the box, unlock, and park on wg.Wait(). At 150 ms Done wakes both; they read the same value and the same nil error, then report shared=true. The leader deletes the key and reports shared=false. CallCount = 1.
3. **The instant after Done** — A slow-motion step across the boundary: Done fires, the riders return, the leader locks and deletes the box, then a fourth caller arrives one millisecond later and finds the map empty. It becomes a new leader and CallCount becomes 2. A side note compares exercise 17, whose Future would have answered this caller from a stored result instead.
4. **The error rides along** — The same leader and riders with key err-k3. The backend returns ErrUnavailable. Every parked rider wakes with that same error, not just the leader, and CallCount is still 1. The scene makes clear that v and err are two fields on one box, so there is no separate failure path.

**Controls.** Play, pause, step forward and step back, restart; Space toggles play, arrow keys step, R restarts. Nothing needs hover or a mouse.; Arrival time of the third caller, 0-300 ms. Before 150 ms it joins and returns with shared=true; after the delete it becomes a new leader and CallCount rises. This teaches the in-flight boundary.; Caller count 1, 3 or 20. Twenty lanes collapse into one visible leader plus a stack labelled with the rest; the leader count stays one and CallCount stays one.; Make fn fail, using the err- key. Shows the error on every waiter and CallCount still 1.; Hold the mutex across fn() as an anti-pattern. Riders queue at the lock instead of the WaitGroup, and a second key waits for the whole 150 ms. Teaches that the lock guards only the map.; Reduced-motion mode is a frame stepper with no autoplay; the same frames are reachable by step forward..

**Code anchors.**
- 35-singleflight/main.go:63 type Group struct{} - there is nothing to rendezvous on; the visualisation shows the empty map row.
- 35-singleflight/main.go:67-70 Do calls fn directly and returns false - the scaffold scene.
- 35-singleflight/main.go:60-63 the stated contract: one execution in flight, duplicates wait and share.
- 35-singleflight/mockbackend.go:14 CallLatency = 150ms - the width of the in-flight window.
- 35-singleflight/mockbackend.go:33-38 Call increments CallCount - the metric the scaffold and fixed scenes compare (3 vs 1).
- 35-singleflight/mockbackend.go:19,40 the err- prefix and ErrUnavailable - the error-sharing scene.
- solutions/35-singleflight.md:40-49 the call{wg, v, err} struct and Group{mu, m} - the map row and the call box.
- solutions/35-singleflight.md:57-61 join path: unlock before wg.Wait, return shared=true - the parked riders.
- solutions/35-singleflight.md:63-66 publish path: new call, Add(1), insert, unlock - the ordering the scene must show literally.
- solutions/35-singleflight.md:68-73 fn outside the lock, Done, then lock and delete - the shaded window and the delete moment.
- 35-singleflight/check_test.go:97-103 notShared == 1 and CallCount == 1 - what the success scene proves.
- 35-singleflight/check_test.go:140 error path still CallCount == 1 - the fourth scene.
- 35-singleflight/check_test.go:183 forget path CallCount == 2 - the boundary scene.

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**

- The mechanics are already drawn statically. solutions/35-singleflight.md:80-88 shows insert, fn outside the mutex, the wg.Done broadcast, delete and the join path, and lines 90-104 state each rule in prose. The animation's unique delta is small, so the 'high' verdict overstates the value.
- The default scene repeats README.md:9-19 (three callers, three fn calls, shared=false). On the Problem tab it adds nothing unless it becomes interactive.
- The width target is wrong. The doc column minimum is 260px (styles.css:506), and the .doc-body padding (styles.css:572-574) leaves about 216px of content. On a 320px phone the content is about 288px (styles.css:1037). A 0-300ms axis, three caller lanes, a map row, a backend lane and 13 annotations cannot fit at that width.
- The arrival control is ambiguous. 'Before 150ms it joins; after the delete it becomes a new leader' skips the Done-to-delete window (solutions/35-singleflight.md:69-73). A caller that arrives there finds a box whose WaitGroup already fired and returns shared=true. Use discrete event stops, not continuous milliseconds.
- Global Space, Arrow and R handlers will fight the Monaco editor and the page scroll. Scope keydown to a focusable visualisation container and use real buttons.
- Reduced motion: styles.css:1067 only stops the existing sidebar and bar transitions. The component must read matchMedia itself, start paused, and offer step and restart only.
- Effort M is low. Four scenes plus a slider, an anti-pattern toggle, 20-lane aggregation, keyboard support and reduced motion is an L, and the 13 proposed annotations must be cut to about four.
- spoiler_safe=false is honest for the mechanics scenes, but only if they sit behind the reveal gate. Under the proposed tab, the whole design is one click away without the gate.
- The scaffold and the fixed code both finish at about 150ms. Only the staggered-arrival stop shows a latency change, and only for callers after the delete. The scene captions must not imply a speedup.
- The lazy-load rule is satisfiable, but the site has no React.lazy today. Use a registry with import.meta.glob so exercise 35 gets its own chunk.
- Technical accuracy checks out otherwise: main.go:63 and 67-70 are the empty Group and the direct fn call; mockbackend.go:14, 33-38 and 40 are the latency, counter and err- prefix; check_test.go:97-103, 140 and 183 are the assertions the scenes mirror.

**Why this tier.** singleflight dedupes in-flight calls; the solution doc already gives the full chronology, so the visual delta is small.

<a id="36-batch-collector"></a>
#### 36 — Batch Collector: A Reusable Batcher With a Deadline and a Real Shutdown
`Tier C` · **nice** · capacity gauge · family `gauge` · effort L · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Rolling batcher with three competing fire triggers (count, deadline, explicit Close) converging on one exactly-once gate.

**Core idea.** A batcher is a buffer plus a deadline plus exactly one arbitration point. Many concurrent Add calls must share one round trip to fn; a batch fires when it reaches MaxBatchSize or when its first request has waited MaxWait, whichever is first, and never twice. The only operation that must be truly atomic is the decision "this batch has now fired", because three different goroutines can reach it at once.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** Three goroutines converge on the same decision, and the prose cannot show the order. A learner cannot see that Timer.Stop() returns without stopping an AfterFunc callback that is already running, so the count path and the deadline path can both call fn for one batch; that a stale timer belonging to an old batch arrives after a newer batch opened; or that inflight.Add(1) must sit inside c.mu or Close can observe a zero counter and return nil while a batch is still about to run. The web runner cannot show any of this either: it has GOMAXPROCS=1 and no -race, and the WaitGroup race reproduces roughly once in 100 local runs.

**Design.** Draw the collector as one horizontal buffer gauge. Caller goroutines are lanes on the left; each Add drops a numbered token into the buffer and reserves a matching result slot under it. Two meters fight to end the batch: a fill bar from 0 to MaxBatchSize, and a countdown bar for MaxWait measured from the batch's first token. When either meter reaches its end, a latch snaps shut, the buffer's tokens travel as one block into a single fn box, responses come back in the same order, and each value drops into the slot of the caller that owns that index. The gauge then clears and a fresh one opens. On the scaffold there is no gauge at all: each token goes straight into its own tiny fn box, six callers make six boxes, and a round-trip counter reads 6 where the fixed version reads 1.

**Scenes.**
1. **Scaffold: one round trip per caller** — Six caller lanes. Each Add produces its own single-token fn call, so the round-trip counter climbs 1 to 6. The batch gauge sits greyed out reading 'MaxBatchSize 5, MaxWait 200ms: never read'. Annotation: fn is called inside Add, so every caller blocks for a full API round trip. This is the default scene.
2. **Batching: count wins, then the deadline wins** — The same six callers on the fixed collector. Tokens 0..4 fill the gauge, the fill bar hits MaxBatchSize, the latch snaps, fn runs once with [0 1 2 3 4], and the five responses fan back into the caller slots that own those indices; a fresh empty gauge opens at once. Tokens 5 and 6 land in the new gauge while its countdown drains; the deadline bar reaches zero and fn runs once with [5 6]. Round-trip counter reads 2.
3. **Exactly-once gate: count and deadline arrive together** — Zoom into the latch. The count path and the deadline path reach for the same batch on the same tick. One takes c.mu, reads fired == false, sets it, stops the timer and detaches; the other blocks as a waiter on the mutex, then reads fired == true and does nothing. The fn-call counter stays at 1. Second variant: batch A's stale timer lands while batch C is open; the c.batch == b check turns it into a no-op and C keeps filling.
4. **Close: flush, then a bounded wait** — Close sets closed, and a late Add gets ErrCollectorClosed on its own channel at once instead of a buffer slot. The open partial batch is detached and fired. The inflight counter goes 0 to 1, and two bars race: Close's wait and the ctx deadline. ctx expires first, Close returns DeadlineExceeded, the fn box is still running, and when it finishes the callers still receive their values. Inset: the Add-after-unlock window where Close can see batch == nil and start Wait at zero before the firing goroutine's Add(1) lands.

**Controls.** Scene picker: Scaffold / Batching / Gate / Close, keyed 1-4; Transport: play, pause, step, restart (Space, Left/Right, R); step is also the reduced-motion mode; Trigger race: count only / deadline only / both (Gate scene): picks which deterministic interleaving plays; MaxWait: off (0) / short: shows the count-only path, and that a very short deadline can legitimately split one intended batch into several; Arrivals: burst / drip: one full batch versus several small ones, the same lesson as the test-design note; fn duration: fast / slow: shows Add never waits on fn and that the next batch keeps filling while the previous fn runs; Close: ctx 50ms / ctx 5s: DeadlineExceeded then background completion, versus Close returning nil because the wait finished first.

**Code anchors.**
- 36-batch-collector/main.go scaffold Add: responses, err := c.fn([]int{request}), the one-element batch that scene 1 counts
- 36-batch-collector/main.go scaffold Close: return nil
- 36-batch-collector/main.go Config.MaxBatchSize and Config.MaxWait, accepted and never read by the scaffold
- solutions/36-batch-collector.md: type batch struct { requests, resultChs, timer, fired } and type Collector { mu, batch, closed, inflight }
- solutions/36-batch-collector.md openBatch: time.AfterFunc(c.cfg.MaxWait, func() { c.onDeadline(b) })
- solutions/36-batch-collector.md Add: if len(b.requests) >= c.cfg.MaxBatchSize { fire = c.detach(b) }
- solutions/36-batch-collector.md detach: if b.fired, b.timer.Stop(), if c.batch == b, c.inflight.Add(1)
- solutions/36-batch-collector.md onDeadline, startExecute, and executeBatch's per-index fan-out loop
- solutions/36-batch-collector.md Close: c.closed = true, detach of the open batch, inflight.Wait() raced against ctx.Done()
- 36-batch-collector/check_test.go TestCollectorNeverDoubleFiresWhenCountAndWaitRace (total processed == submitted)
- 36-batch-collector/check_test.go TestCollectorCloseConcurrentWithAdd, TestCollectorCloseRespectsContextDeadline, TestCollectorCloseFlushesPendingPartialBatch

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- The blind spot is overstated. The solution doc already has an ASCII timeline of the stale-timer no-op (solutions/36-batch-collector.md:221-246) and of the WaitGroup Add-after-unlock race (solutions/36-batch-collector.md:327-340). The README has the three-trigger EXACTLY ONE diagram. The proposal says no static frame shows that the loser no-oped. That claim is false. The player adds a modality, not a new fact.
- Scene 3 teaches the wrong guard. In the real detach (solutions/36-batch-collector.md:143-155), a timer for an already-fired batch returns at `if b.fired` (lines 144-146). The `if c.batch == b` check (line 151) is never reached in that case, and it is redundant under the implementation: a batch becomes non-current only after detach sets fired. The proposal says the `c.batch == b` check turns the stale timer into a no-op, and it annotates that line. That is a false causal claim.
- spoiler_safe is not honest as written. Only scene 1 is safe. Scenes 2-4 (Batching, Gate, Close) and the control labels encode detach, the fired latch, inflight, and the ctx race. The proposal's own risk list says the fix becomes reachable; a risk that contradicts a boolean must change the design, not sit beside it.
- A third tab does not fit at 320 CSS px. .doc-tabs (web/src/styles.css:540) has no flex-wrap, 12px side padding, and 4px gaps. It already holds Problem, Solution, the difficulty badge (web/src/styles.css:560), and an optional passed badge. At the 14px base font that row is about 300px; a Visualise tab adds about 70px. Below 900px the doc pane is full width with 16px padding (web/src/styles.css:986-1030), so the content is about 288px. The row will overflow or squeeze the badges.

- The design does not name a lazy-load path. Today only the per-exercise JSON is lazy (web/src/data.ts:43-59). A player imported by DocPane ships in the main chunk for every visitor. The design must use a code-split, for example import.meta.glob('./visualisations/*.tsx', { eager: false }) with React.lazy.
- Scene 2 has a counting error. Scene 1 says six callers (0-5) and a counter of 6. Scene 2 says the same six callers, then fills 0..4 and puts Tokens 5 and 6 in the next batch. That is seven callers. Use seven lanes and a counter of 7, or keep six and put only token 5 in the second batch.
- The Close scene conflates two tests. TestCollectorCloseRespectsContextDeadline (36-batch-collector/check_test.go:359) starts the batch by count and then Closes. TestCollectorCloseFlushesPendingPartialBatch (36-batch-collector/check_test.go:280) closes an open partial batch with a fast fn and expects nil. The proposal has Close detach a partial batch and the ctx expire while it runs. That is legal, but it is not either test. Split it into two frames or label it composite.
- The control matrix is too large for one exercise. Four scenes times trigger, MaxWait, arrivals, fn duration, and close ctx is 20 or more authored timelines. Each one needs verification against the solution. Scope each control to one scene and drop the arrivals control, which repeats the solution's test-design note (solutions/36-batch-collector.md:370).
- The player has no screen-reader output. Keyboard transport alone is not enough. The current tick and annotation must be DOM text with aria-live=polite, not only SVG labels. The controls must be real button elements. The code uses prefers-reduced-motion only for transitions today (web/src/styles.css:1067-1075); the player must read the media query and start paused.
- The data model invents unobservable state. time.Timer exposes no armed/fired/stopped state, and sync.Mutex does not promise FIFO wake order. An ordered waiter-queue drawing teaches a guarantee Go does not make. Show blocked-on-mu as an unordered set, or label the order as illustrative.
- Drift has no guard. No check ties the scenes to the solution's function names. The doc-tests lint rule only checks TestXxx names. If the solution changes, the scenes can lie. Add a small lint rule that every web/src/visualisations/<id>.* has an exercise folder and a solution doc, and name openBatch, detach, onDeadline, executeBatch, and Close in the script comments.
- The phrase a fresh empty gauge opens at once is wrong. detach only sets c.batch = nil (solutions/36-batch-collector.md:151-153). The next Add calls openBatch (lines 118-131) and opens the fresh batch. Show no open batch until the next token arrives.
- Web-only is the right scope, but state it as a limit. The clone mode keeps the README ASCII diagram and the solution doc. The layout rule forbids new files in an exercise folder (tools/check/lint.go:155), and the generator reads only .go, .md, and go.mod from HEAD (web/tools/gen/main.go:484-487). Do not expect the player in the repo checkout. The nested zeetcode repo is not part of web/ and cannot be reused without vendoring.

**Why this tier.** The extreme batcher: the stale-timer guard and the exactly-once latch are worth seeing, but it is L effort.

<a id="37-safe-pool-shutdown"></a>
#### 37 — Safe Pool Shutdown: Closing a Multi-Producer Job Queue Without Panicking
`Tier C` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort L · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Multi-producer worker pool with a safe Close: a sync.RWMutex-guarded closed flag around an unbuffered job channel, plus a sync.WaitGroup that makes Close wait for every accepted job to finish.

**Core idea.** The read lock must span the blocking send. Submit holds RLock from the closed check through p.jobs <- job; Close's Lock can only succeed once no send is in flight, so close(p.jobs) can never meet a sender. Holding the lock only around the check is not enough.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The shape of the window. The README says "the send can race the close", but it cannot show that the channel is unbuffered (main.go:63), so a Submit is parked inside the send at the moment Close runs. It also cannot show the two quieter failures: a naive Close returns while accepted jobs still run, and the recover "fix" leaves wg.Add(1) unpaired so Close parks in wg.Wait forever. The learner reads a panic or a 3 s timeout and never sees which goroutine stood where.

**Design.** Draw a vertical panel 320 px wide. Time runs top to bottom, because the doc pane scrolls and a narrow column stays legible. On the left, narrow actor columns: Submitter A, B, C, then Close, then Worker 1 and Worker 2. On the right, a fixed object column holds three stacked shapes that every scene shares. First, the jobs channel, drawn as a single slot with a small queue of parked senders, because it is unbuffered. Second, the mutex, drawn as a gate with a reader count and a visible waiter queue. Third, the WaitGroup, drawn as a counter. A horizontal dashed rule labelled "Close starts here" can be dragged up and down the timeline. At the tick where the rule crosses a submitter column, if that submitter is parked in `jobs <- job`, the close reaches the channel first: the submitter's column flashes `runtime: send on closed channel`, the channel slot is stamped closed, and the job is marked lost. Drag the rule a little earlier or later and the same pool behaves differently, which shows that the window is a span, not one magic tick. The fixed scene reuses the same geometry: a submitter's column carries a shaded band labelled `RLock held` that extends over the whole parked send, and Close's column shows `Lock` standing in the mutex waiter queue behind that band. The rule cannot cross the channel until the band ends, so the send completes, the counter drains, and only then does `close(p.jobs)` land. The visualisation is a scripted, deterministic model of the states, not a trace of the learner's run.

**Scenes.**
1. **The send that is already parked** — Default scene, scaffold code. Two workers are busy; Submit C is parked in `jobs <- job` because no worker is ready to receive. The Close rule lands on that tick: `close(p.jobs)` completes, the parked send panics with `send on closed channel`, the channel turns closed, and the job is stamped lost. A second submitter, still behind the rule, also panics. The pool never reports accepted=false because Submit has no such path.
2. **Close returns before the work does** — Scaffold again, without a collision. Ten jobs are accepted; Close returns at once because close(p.jobs) does not wait. Only six jobs have run. The test's helper returns and the assertion at check_test.go:84 fails with `only 6/10 pre-Close jobs had run`. This is the second, quieter defect: close is not a drain.
3. **The recover that swallows the proof** — The Pool now has a wg field and Submit wraps the send in recover (solution doc:104-114). The panic is swallowed and Submit returns accepted=false, but wg.Add(1) already ran and no Done follows. The counter stays at 1. Close's column parks in wg.Wait, workers go idle, and the scene ends on `Close did not return within 3s` from closeWithTimeout. Panic gone, property broken.
4. **Read lock spans the send** — Gated behind the same 'Show the fix' reveal as the Solution tab. Submit holds RLock across the whole parked send, shown as a band. Close's Lock waits in the mutex queue until every band ends. The parked send completes, a worker receives and runs, the counter falls to zero, Close sets closed=true, calls close(p.jobs), the workers' range loops exit, and a late Submit reads the flag and returns accepted=false without touching the channel. The invariant ran == accepted (check_test.go:182) comes from this order.

**Controls.** Play, Pause, Step one event, Restart; bind them to Space, Right-arrow and R; the scene must be fully usable with the Tab key and no mouse.; A draggable 'Close starts here' rule on the shared timeline; this is the main control and the day-one lesson.; Hold worker: one worker refuses to receive, so a send stays parked and the collision becomes deterministic.; Submitter count 2 to 6: more producers widen the window and do not add a new bug.; Scene selector with four scenes; the fourth is behind a 'Show the fix' button that mirrors the Solution spoiler gate.; Reduced-motion mode (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce): no play; render the current step plus a numbered step list, and let Step and the rule still work..

**Code anchors.**
- 37-safe-pool-shutdown/main.go:63 `make(chan func())`: no buffer; this is why a Submit is parked in the send when Close runs.
- 37-safe-pool-shutdown/main.go:73 `for job := range p.jobs`: the only receive; a worker exits only when the channel closes, which the fixed Close does last.
- 37-safe-pool-shutdown/main.go:83 `p.jobs <- job`: the send scene 1 parks and scene 4 protects.
- 37-safe-pool-shutdown/main.go:90 `close(p.jobs)`: the unconditional close that lands first in scene 1 and scene 3.
- solutions/37-safe-pool-shutdown.md:46-53: RLock around the check and the send, with `wg.Add(1)` at line 52 before the send at line 53.
- solutions/37-safe-pool-shutdown.md:57-64: Close does Lock (58), closed=true (59), wg.Wait (62), close (63); scene 4 animates exactly this order.
- solutions/37-safe-pool-shutdown.md:104-114 and 117-124: the recover variant and its counter diagram; scene 3 is that diagram made movable.
- 37-safe-pool-shutdown/check_test.go:23-36 `closeWithTimeout`: the 3 s gate that turns scene 3's hang into a failure.
- 37-safe-pool-shutdown/check_test.go:84 and 182: the two assertions scenes 1-2 and scene 4 must make visible (`preCloseRan`, `ran == accepted`).

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Scene 2 is arithmetically impossible. With `NewPool(3)` (check_test.go:43, 72, 138) at most 3 jobs can be unfinished when Close returns, so `preCloseRan` is at least 7 of 10, never 6. The `preCloseRan` check at check_test.go:84 is also timing-dependent, not deterministic. Show a possible range, not a fixed 6/10.
- The 'high' tier overstates the gap. README.md:23-28 already draws the Submit/Close race, solutions/37-safe-pool-shutdown.md:67-75 draws the read-lock band across the send, and lines 117-124 draw the leaked wg counter. The only real gap is the parked send on the unbuffered channel. That is 'nice', not 'high'.
- The width budget is wrong. The doc column shrinks to 260px (web/src/styles.css:506) and .doc-body pads 22px each side (styles.css:574), so content is about 216px, not 320px. Six actor columns plus an object column at 216px is about 30px per column; labels will not fit.
- A third tab overflows. .doc-tabs is a non-wrapping flex row (styles.css:540-559). Problem + Solution + Visualise is about 231px, the difficulty badge pushes it to about 292px, and the passed badge exceeds 320px. The 260px pane minimum breaks earlier.
- A third tab needs routing changes. Route.tab is a two-value union (web/src/router.ts:5-6, 9-13, 15-18), and the prop types at DocPane.tsx:11 and ExerciseView.tsx:10 change too. The proposal understates this surface.
- Scene 3 depends on an unstated Close order. The hang needs `close(p.jobs)` before `wg.Wait()`. The solution's own Close waits first (solutions/37-safe-pool-shutdown.md:57-64), and then no recovered panic can occur while Close waits. Pin the Close body in the scene, or it teaches the wrong order.
- The draggable 'Close starts here' rule teaches timing, not the bug. A Submit after Close panics deterministically (check_test.go:61-70), and a Submit in flight panics when Close lands. No tick is safe. A slider that implies a safe placement is misleading.
- Global Space/Right/R handlers will scroll the doc pane (.doc-body has overflow-y:auto at styles.css:575) and fight Monaco. Use native buttons and an <input type="range">, and scope keys to a focusable figure.
- The claim 'snapshot-tested' needs a runner the repo does not have. web/package.json has no test script and no vitest. A snapshot test means a new dev dependency, which the constraints forbid. Use a plain `node scripts/*.ts` check like scripts/smoke-test.ts.
- spoiler_safe is honest only if scene 4 stays gated. Scenes 1-3 repeat README.md:23-28 and 57-90. Note that `revealed` in DocPane.tsx:17 survives tab switches, because App.tsx:84 keys the view by exercise id. A shared gate would leak the fix into the visualisation tab.
- The `ties_to_code` line numbers will drift when the scaffold changes. Quote the code, or name the symbol, instead of citing main.go:63 and similar.
- The 'Submitter count 2 to 6' control conflicts with capped lanes and the 216px width, and it adds no new failure. Drop it, or make it a queue-depth control.

**Why this tier.** Closing a multi-producer queue safely; the send parked on an unbuffered channel is the fact, but the fix is narrow.


## Tier D — do not build; improve the README instead

<a id="03-limit-service-time"></a>
#### 3 — Limit Service Time for Free-tier Users
`Tier D` · **nice** · lock and waiters · family `lock` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Per-user lifetime quota enforced by racing a timeout against completion, under a per-user mutex held for the whole call

**Core idea.** Go cannot kill a goroutine: "killing" means the select stops waiting and the process keeps running, orphaned. The quota check, the timeout race and the credit all happen while holding a per-user lock, so a concurrent request for the same user waits at `u.mu.Lock()` and is then judged against the first request's real, recorded usage instead of a worst-case guess.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** Three facts are invisible in the prose and the tests. (1) A killed request leaves its process goroutine running: the job that returned ok=false still burns time and finishes later. (2) The per-user mutex is held for the entire process+timeout window, so two "concurrent" requests for one user serialize: the second parks at `u.mu.Lock()` and reads `remaining` only after the first has credited its real usage. (3) The timer is armed with `remaining` (quota minus TimeUsed), never with the process's own duration, and a kill credits exactly that amount. The tests see only bools and elapsed-time bounds, so none of this is observable.

**Design.** A 10-second quota bar sits above a timeline; it fills as the user's lifetime usage grows. Each request is a horizontal lane. A free request first stops at a lock block labelled `u.mu.Lock()`; once it holds the lock, it reads the quota bar, arms a timer for the remaining slice, and spawns a child process lane that sleeps. If the child ends first, the request lane ends with ok=true and the quota bar grows by the time used. If the timer fires first, the request lane ends at that instant with ok=false, the quota bar jumps by exactly the remaining slice, and the process lane keeps sliding past its request's end as a dashed orphan tail. A second request for the same user waits beside the lock block, so its quota read happens only after the holder credits. Premium requests skip the lock, the timer and the child lane: they run inline and never end early.

**Scenes.**
1. **Scaffold: a free request runs to the end** — One free user, one 11s process. The lane runs straight to 11s, the quota bar stays at 0 of 10s, and HandleRequest returns ok=true at 11s. A premium lane beside it looks identical. No timeout, no lock, no quota, which is the scaffold at main.go:21-24.
2. **Kill at the lifetime mark, then walk away** — The user already used 4s, so remaining is 6s. An 11s process starts; the timer is armed for 6s. At t=6s the select takes ctx.Done(), the request returns ok=false, and the credit is exactly 6s, so the quota bar reaches 10 of 10s. The process lane keeps going past the request's end to t=11s, drawn dashed and labelled orphaned: Go cannot kill a goroutine.
3. **Two 6s requests, one 10s quota** — A and B start together. B stops at the lock block with a waiter badge; A runs 6s under a lock-held marker, credits 6s and releases. B wakes, reads remaining=4s, starts its process, and is cut off at 4s. Results are [true, false] and TimeUsed is 10s, so the double-spend trap stays closed. Before/after toggle shows the naive run where both read 10s and both succeed.
4. **Why the lock is held through process()** — A is a 1s request; B is a 2s request that arrives at t=0.2s and queues. At t=1s A returns true with real usage 1s; B then proceeds and succeeds, because it read A's actual usage. A ghost lane overlays the alternative: reserve the worst case up front, release the lock early, and B is rejected even though 1s+2s is well inside 10s. The overlay is labelled a throughput-for-accuracy tradeoff, not a bug.

**Controls.** Play, pause, step, restart, all keyboard focusable; reduced motion starts paused so step and scrub still work; Timeline scrubber over the virtual clock; Toggle premium for the next request: the lane skips the lock, the timer and the child process; Set the next process duration (1s, 6s, 11s): shows that usage, not duration, is credited; Add a concurrent request for the same user: injects the lock waiter; Lock scope: whole call or bookkeeping only: reveals the worst-case reservation tradeoff; Reset.

- 03-limit-service-time/main.go:16: TimeUsed int64 in seconds, which loses partial seconds; the solution changes it to time.Duration

<a id="05-session-cleaner"></a>
#### 5 — Clean Inactive Sessions to Prevent Memory Overflow
`Tier D` · **nice** · cache grid · family `grid` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** TTL session store. One mutex guards a shared map. A background reaper deletes entries older than 5s. The reaper is a second writer, so it takes the same lock. Its goroutine must also end on its own, because testing/synctest panics when a goroutine is still durably blocked at the end of the bubble.

**Core idea.** A stored session lives forever unless a second, synchronized actor deletes it. Reclamation is a writer, so it takes the same lock as CreateSession, GetSessionData and UpdateSessionData. Under synctest the reaper must also die by itself: one-shot per-session time.AfterFunc timers fire and exit, while one forever ticker stays blocked and panics the test.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The learner cannot see four things. (1) A session that nobody reads again still holds memory; an expiry check inside GetSessionData frees nothing. (2) A sweep that reads an entry, releases the lock, then deletes can remove a session that an Update just renewed. (3) Replacing the timer on every update orphans the old one; the guard no-ops the orphan, the new deadline is never scheduled, and every test still passes. (4) The browser cannot run -race, so the unsynchronized map write in CreateSession and UpdateSessionData shows no symptom there.

**Design.** The doc pane shows a session store as a grid of up to 24 slots, under a 0-10 second timeline and beside a horizontal memory bar. A slot appears when CreateSession runs. Its age ring changes colour as it passes 5 exercise-seconds. It disappears only when something deletes it. In the default scene nothing does, so slots only accumulate, the bar climbs to a hard ceiling, and one label says that GetSessionData reads and never removes. Turn the reaper on and the same traffic turns over: stale slots grey out, a removal tick lands between the 5s and 7s marks, and the bar saws instead of climbing. One lock token sits beside the grid. When two callers hold it at once, the two colliding slots flash and a red "data race" annotation appears.

**Scenes.**
1. **Nothing ever deletes (default)** — A 20s script of Create/Update/Get traffic. Every slot appears and stays. Age rings pass 5s and nothing acts on them. Resident count and the memory bar climb to the ceiling. No reaper, no lock and no failure label: the learner sees that the map only ever gains writers, never a deleter.
2. **A read is not a reaper** — Two slots. Slot A is fetched every second; slot B is never fetched again. Both stay for the full run. A counter shows that a lazy expiry check inside GetSessionData would only ever notice A, so B leaks. The bar never falls.
3. **Two writers, no lock** — Three caller lanes plus a sweeper. With the mutex off, two lanes enter CreateSession's map write together; the cell flashes red and an annotation says what go test -race reports. With the mutex on, callers queue at one token. Then an Update at t=4.9s renews a session and the sweep at t=5.0s must spare it: the time.Since guard keeps the slot.
4. **The cleaner has to end** — Solution-side and gated. Two lanes run the same 10s. The ticker lane loops, stays blocked at the end marker, and the bubble-end marker raises the panic 'blocked goroutines remain'. The one-shot timer lane fires once per session, the callback exits, and bubble-end is clean. A stopwatch row marks the removal at 5.0s (timer) against the 5-7s band (2s sweep).

**Controls.** Play and Pause, one button, Space as the shortcut.; Step: advance one event, so an interleaving can be inspected with no motion.; Restart: return to t=0 with the same scene and settings.; Scene selector as a radio group, reachable with the arrow keys.; Touch session at t=4s: inject one UpdateSessionData for a chosen slot, to show the deadline move and the sweep spare it.; Callers 1 or 20: raise the offered load to expose the race and the lock queue.; Reaper: none, lazy-on-read, sweep, one-shot timer. The last two stay disabled until the Solution spoiler is revealed.; Sweep interval 1s, 2s or 5s: show where the interval places the deletion inside the 5-7s band and how much retention latency it adds..

Under one mutex a sweep cannot both delete and 'lose the race with an update' at t=5.0; the time.Since(lastUpdated) < sessionTTL guard belongs to the AfterFunc callback (solutions:135-141), not to the sweep. A 'read, unlock, then delete' sweep is a third, separate wrong-fix pattern.

- The memory bar saturating at 24 slots is a false quantitative claim. A Go map has no cap and OOM is far beyond 24 entries. Show a resident-session count with a 'no cap' label, or shift a window while the count keeps climbing; never equate slots with bytes.

A scripted model in TS is a second source of truth that can drift from the Go solution; it needs a single-constant table tied to solutions/05-session-cleaner.md plus a plain-node assertion script.

**Why this tier.** Every scene but the symptom is a counterfactual; the orphaned timer is solution-side and already bolded.

<a id="09-context-cancellation"></a>
#### 9 — Context Cancellation & Propagation
`Tier D` · **nice** · timeline · family `lanes` · effort S · README diagram: **no — draw it first**

**Pattern.** Context propagation through a sequential call chain (deadline and cancellation must be threaded into each downstream call)

**Core idea.** A context-aware dependency can only cancel the work when it is handed the caller's real ctx; the outcome is decided by where the caller's deadline falls on the wall clock relative to each layer's 300ms of work.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The learner cannot see where the deadline lands. In a correct run the 200ms deadline expires inside layer A's 300ms window, so layer A is cut short and layers B and C never start. In the scaffold every layer receives context.Background(), whose Done() channel never closes, so the existing error checks have nothing to fire on and the caller's deadline shows up only as 900ms of work plus a result that arrives 700ms after the caller gave up. The test output reports elapsed milliseconds only; it never shows the deadline crossing a layer.

**Design.** A horizontal time axis runs 0 to 1000ms. Three stacked lanes share it. The top lane is the caller: a hollow bar from 0 to the deadline (200ms by default), then a hatched "gave up, nobody reads the answer" region to 1000ms. The middle lane is HandleRequest: three labelled segments, A, B and C, each 300ms long, drawn in sequence. The bottom lane is the context the handler hands to each layer: in scaffold mode it is one flat unbroken strip labelled context.Background(), with an icon showing its Done() channel permanently open; in fixed mode it is the caller's ctx, whose strip turns dashed at the deadline. A playhead sweeps the axis. In scaffold mode the playhead reaches 200ms, the caller lane greys out, and the three segments keep filling to 900ms; a result chip labelled "ABC" drops at 900ms into the dead caller lane and fades. In fixed mode the playhead reaches 200ms, segment A is sliced by a vertical deadline line and turns to an error marker reading DeadlineExceeded, segments B and C stay empty outlines, and the function bar ends at 200ms. At 320px, lanes stack vertically with an 18px label gutter and short labels ("caller", "handler", "ctx"); annotations sit as two-line callouts anchored to the deadline line, not floating over the bars.

**Scenes.**
1. **Ample timeout - the trap** — Timeout set to 2s. All three segments fill green and the result ABC lands at 900ms, before the deadline. Identical in scaffold and fixed mode. This is why the happy-path test passes against the broken code.
2. **Tight deadline, scaffold** — Default 200ms deadline with context.Background() handed to each layer. The deadline line falls inside segment A, the caller lane greys out at 200ms, yet A, B and C all run their full 300ms. A result chip arrives at 900ms into the dead caller lane. Callouts: "Done() never closes", "error checks never fire", "caller gave up 700ms ago".
3. **Tight deadline, ctx propagated** — Same 200ms deadline, now passed to the layers. Segment A is cut exactly at the deadline line and becomes a red DeadlineExceeded marker; segments B and C remain empty outlines; the handler bar ends at 200ms. Callouts: "select on ctx.Done() fires", "return \"\", ctx.Err()", "B and C never start".
4. **Cancel mid-chain** — Cancellable ctx, cancel injected at 350ms while segment B is in flight. Scaffold: nothing happens, all three segments still run to 900ms and ABC is returned. Fixed: B is cut at 350ms with context.Canceled (not DeadlineExceeded), C stays empty, handler returns at 350ms. Makes the third test's 350ms choice concrete.

**Controls.** Deadline selector: 200ms (inside layer A), 450ms (kills layer B), 2s (nothing breaks) - shows the boundary shifting; Cancel button / "cancel at 350ms" toggle - lets the learner cancel mid-chain exactly where TestHandleRequestCancelledMidway does; Mode toggle: pass context.Background() (scaffold) vs pass ctx (fixed) - the only behavioural difference to demonstrate; Play / Pause / Step / Restart, all keyboard reachable; step advances one event at a time (call, timer fire, deadline, cancel, return); Speed 1x / 4x for playback; ignored when reduced motion is set, where step-only is the default.

- 09-context-cancellation/mocklayers.go - LayerLatency = 300 * time.Millisecond, and callLayer's select between timer.C and ctx.Done() returning "", ctx.Err()
- 09-context-cancellation/main.go:61 - main() builds context.WithTimeout(..., 200ms) as the demo deadline
