<a id="12-pub-sub"></a>
#### 12 — Pub-Sub: In-Memory Event Bus
`Tier D` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: **no — draw it first**

**Pattern.** Pub-sub broadcast: a mutex-guarded subscriber registry, one channel per subscriber, bounded per-subscriber buffers, and a non-blocking select/default send.

**Core idea.** Never block inside a critical section. The blocking send runs while b.mu is held. So one stalled subscriber freezes every other goroutine that calls Subscribe, Unsubscribe or Publish, not just the current Publish call.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The visible symptom is "Publish hangs". The real damage hides behind it. Publish holds b.mu (main.go:99) across the blocking send (main.go:103). So every other goroutine piles up on b.mu.Lock() and never runs. A subscriber A that reads promptly receives nothing more. An Unsubscribe that is written correctly can never run. The prose names two bugs. It never names this third one, and no test output shows the waiter queue. The learner also cannot see that a full buffer drops the event for one subscriber only.

**Design.** Four horizontal lanes run left to right: a publisher goroutine, reader A, reader B, and a bus column that shows b.mu. The bus column is green when the lock is free. It is red while a goroutine holds it. A queue of small circles builds at the bus column for goroutines parked in b.mu.Lock(). Events are pills that leave the publisher lane and sit in a subscriber's channel slots. In the scaffold scene, reader B is frozen with a grey pause icon. A pill leaves the publisher and stays on B's zero-capacity channel. The bus column stays red. The waiter queue fills with circles labelled Subscribe, Publish and Unsubscribe. Reader A's lane is ready and empty. In the fixed scene, the bus column flashes for a short sliver. B has eight visible slots that fill to 8/8. Each later pill fades and lands in a drop tray beside B with a counter. A receives every pill. A reader can pause on any frame and read one caption that names the exact Go line in play.

**Scenes.**
1. **Scaffold: one stalled reader freezes the bus** — Publisher takes b.mu and sends event 1. Reader A takes it. The next send targets B, whose reader lane is frozen. The pill stays in B's unbuffered channel, the bus column stays red, and the caption names main.go:103 ch <- e. The test's 200 ms timer fires.
2. **The waiters behind the lock** — A Subscribe goroutine, a second publisher and an Unsubscribe(B) goroutine arrive. Each calls b.mu.Lock() and joins the queue at the bus column. Reader A has an empty channel and is ready to read. The caption says Unsubscribe can be implemented and still never run.
3. **Fixed: eight slots and a drop tray** — B has a buffer of 8 and never reads. Slots fill 1 to 8. The ninth event shows select with the default branch taken. It fades into a drop tray labelled dropped: n. A keeps receiving every event. Each Publish returns after a short red sliver of lock time, with no waiters.
4. **Fixed: Unsubscribe returns and the bus forgets** — Unsubscribe(B) takes the lock for a moment, deletes B's entry and returns. The registry count drops by one. The next Publish reaches A only. The caption says the bus does not close the channel, and that close is the wrong move here.

**Controls.** Play, Pause, Step, Restart: keyboard reachable with visible focus, no hover needed.; Implementation: Scaffold | Fixed. Fixed sits behind an explicit Show the fix control, like the Solution tab gate.; Subscriber B: Reading | Stalled (default Stalled in the scaffold scene).; Subscriber A: Fast | Slow reader. Shows that a drop belongs to one subscriber, not to the bus.; Buffer size: 0 | 1 | 8, in Fixed mode only. Buffer 0 shows why the buffer is part of the fix.; Inject Unsubscribe(B) at step 4. Shows the call that cannot run while the lock is held.; Reduced motion renders the same steps as still frames. It does not change the timeline..

**Code anchors.**
- 12-pub-sub/main.go:99: b.mu.Lock() at the top of Publish
- 12-pub-sub/main.go:102-103: the blocking ch <- e runs inside the locked section
- 12-pub-sub/main.go:88: make(chan Event) creates an unbuffered channel
- 12-pub-sub/main.go:94-95: Unsubscribe is an empty method, so the registry never shrinks
- 12-pub-sub/check_test.go: TestPublishNeverBlocksOnStalledSubscriber (200 ms limit) and TestUnsubscribeStopsDelivery
- solutions/12-pub-sub.md, banded Publish block {6}: the doc already tints the same ch <- e line the visualisation highlights
- solutions/12-pub-sub.md, Approach 1 band {38}: make(chan Event, subscriberBuffer)
- solutions/12-pub-sub.md, Approach 1 band {68-69}: case ch <- e: / default:, the per-subscriber drop
- solutions/12-pub-sub.md, Approach 1 Unsubscribe: delete(b.subscribers, sub) under the same lock, and no close(ch)

The 'third bug the prose never names' is in the prose, so the marginal value is a picture of known facts, not new information.

At HEAD the scaffold fails all four tests, not just two: Basic times out at 1.00s (check_test.go:58), Unsubscribe fails at 0.10s (:132), Stalled at 0.20s (:184), Concurrent at 10.00s (:244). The learner sees 'Publish did not return in time' after 1s first, not the 200ms stalled timeout.

Move that scene under Fixed (where Unsubscribe locks), or label the shown method as the one the learner will write.

<a id="14-tee-channel"></a>
#### 14 — Tee Channel: Duplicating a Sensor Stream
`Tier D` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: **no — draw it first**

**Pattern.** Tee channel (fan-out duplication): one goroutine reads the input once and delivers every value to two independent output channels, holding the value until both outputs have received it.

**Core idea.** A send on a channel is received by exactly one goroutine. `return in, in` does not duplicate — it hands the same channel to both consumers, who race for each value, so each value goes to only one of them and both end with half the stream. Duplication needs one reader goroutine and two distinct channels, with each value held until both outputs accept it.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The failure is a receive race on one channel, invisible in the source: `out1` and `out2` look like two values but are one channel, and nothing in a passing build shows who wins each value. Two consequences are also invisible. (1) With unbuffered outputs the correct Tee throttles the fast consumer to the slow one's pace: value v+1 cannot be read from `in` until v has reached both outputs, so the fast consumer gets a gap, not a head start. (2) Closing `done` while the tee goroutine is parked on `out2 <- v` must escape through the inner select, or the goroutine and both outputs hang forever; `done` checked only in the outer receive loop does not help.

**Design.** A horizontal sensor lane emits a numbered reading every tick, and two consumer lanes sit to its right: a fast one that reads at once and a slow one that pauses between reads. In the default (scaffold) scene there is a single channel bar between sensor and consumers; both consumer lanes reach for the same bar, and each reading slides to exactly one of them — counters show one lane filling with even numbers and the other with odd ones, each half empty. Flip to the fixed scene and a tee lane appears in the middle: it reads a reading once, then drops a copy onto each of two distinct output bars, greying out an output bar as soon as that consumer takes the copy. Now both counters fill to the same full sequence, and raising the slow consumer's delay makes the tee lane wait on the slow bar with the current value held — visibly stalling the sensor and leaving a gap in the fast lane. Closing `done` while the tee lane is parked on the slow bar cuts it loose: the inner select takes the cancel branch, the goroutine exits, and both output bars close.

**Scenes.**
1. **One channel, two readers (default)** — The scaffold scene. Sensor lane emits 0..9, 5ms apart, over a single channel bar drawn between it and both consumer lanes. Each value slides to exactly one consumer; counters read fast [0,2,4,6,8] and slow [1,3,5,7,9] with a caption 'one possible interleaving'. Pressing Close done does nothing: the sensor keeps emitting and the run runs to 9, because out1 and out2 are the same channel as in. Caption names `return in, in`.
2. **Duplicate to both outputs** — Fix scene. A tee lane appears; two distinct output bars replace the shared one. The tee reads value v once, sends it to out1, then out2; each bar greys out once its consumer takes the value. Both counters fill to the full [0..9], and both output bars close when in closes. `defer close(out1); defer close(out2)` is labelled.
3. **The slow consumer sets the pace** — Fix scene with the slow-consumer delay raised. The tee holds v after out1 took it and blocks on out2; the sensor cannot emit v+1; the fast lane shows a visible gap until the slow consumer reads v. A held-value badge sits on the tee lane. This proves duplication throttles the fast consumer, which the prose never says.
4. **done escapes a blocked send** — Slow consumer stops reading; the tee is parked on out2 <- v. Close done; the inner select takes <-done, the goroutine returns, both output bars close, both ranges end. A red 'wrong variant' ghost shows done checked only in the outer loop: the tee stays parked forever and the outputs never close — the failure `TestTeeStopsOnDone` catches.

**Controls.** Play / Pause; Step (advance one event, for prefers-reduced-motion); Restart; Toggle: 'Tee = return in, in' ↔ 'Tee = two distinct channels' (the fix toggle reveals the solution shape; gate it like the Solution tab); Slow-consumer delay (raise it to show the throttle in scene 3); Close done (cancellation while a send is blocked); Step one value (advance just the duplication inner loop).

**Code anchors.**
- 14-tee-channel/main.go `func Tee(...) (<-chan int, <-chan int) { return in, in }` — the bug: one channel under two names.
- solutions/14-tee-channel.md `Tee`: `out1 := make(chan int)` / `out2 := make(chan int)` — two distinct channels.
- solutions/14-tee-channel.md outer `select { case <-done: / case v, ok = <-in: }` — the single reader.
- solutions/14-tee-channel.md banded lines 38,42,44: `out1Ch, out2Ch := out1, out2`, `out1Ch = nil`, `out2Ch = nil` — the inner select and the nil-out trick.
- solutions/14-tee-channel.md `case <-done: return` inside the inner loop — why shutdown escapes a blocked send.
- solutions/14-tee-channel.md `defer close(out1); defer close(out2)` — both outputs close on every return path.
- mocksensor.go `StartSensor` and `SensorInterval = 5 * time.Millisecond` — the emission cadence and close.
- check_test.go `TestTeeDuplicatesToBothConsumers`, `teeStopsOnDoneOnce` and `assertClosesPromptly` — the split and the prompt close that the scenes visualise.

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Verdict overstated: the proposal calls this "high" because the race is "invisible in the source", but 14-tee-channel/README.md says it verbatim: "out1 and out2 are actually the SAME channel: whichever consumer happens to read a given value first gets it, and the other consumer never sees that value at all. Values get split between the two consumers instead of duplicated to both." The scaffold header in main.go (lines 12-31) also states the full fix algorithm, ending in the inner-select and close requirements. There is no hidden mechanism left for a "high" visualisation to reveal. Downgrade to nice.
- Missed sequel 14b: 14b-tee-channel-independent-close exists and is the exercise whose whole premise is scene 3. Its README says "a single goroutine holds value N until both out1 and out2 have received it before it will even look at value N+1", and it already has an ASCII today-vs-goal diagram. The proposal's claim that scene 3 ("the prose never says") proves the throttle is false for the curriculum, and the proposal never mentions 14b.
- Scene 1 hard-codes an alternating split: fast [0,2,4,6,8], slow [1,3,5,7,9]. The verified failure output in solutions/14-tee-channel.md is the reverse (fast [1,3,5,7,9], slow [0,2,4,6,8]), and that was one run. "Both end with half the stream" is not an invariant: the real invariant is a disjoint partition of 0..n-1 summing to n, and with GOMAXPROCS=1 the fast consumer may take almost all values. This contradicts the proposal's own risk #1 and would teach a false deterministic pattern.
- Placement "problem-inline" is not implementable as written. DocPane renders the README only through <Markdown>, which sets dangerouslySetInnerHTML (web/src/components/Markdown.tsx:66). web/src/markdown.ts has no component directive or placeholder hook. Putting a figure beside a sentence needs a new marker plus a portal/replace step, or the figure must be a separate block in DocPane. The proposal treats inline as free.
- Width math is worse than stated. The doc pane minimum is 260px (web/src/styles.css:506) and .doc-body padding is 16px 22px (styles.css:73-77), so the tightest content width is about 216px, not 320px. At <=900px the doc body is 320-32 = 288px. Four horizontal lanes plus two channel bars plus counters plus controls cannot fit at 216px. The proposal lists this as a risk but keeps the goroutine-lanes layout.
- Unbuffered rendezvous is easy to draw wrong and the data model invites it. A send on an unbuffered channel and its matching receive are one instant; there is no value "in the channel". The proposed send/recv events plus "each reading slides to exactly one of them" can imply a buffer. Also the risk text "a committed send to out2 must not be drawn as lost" is confused: on an unbuffered channel a send either completed (the receiver got it) or is blocked; there is no committed in-flight value to protect.
- The proposal's own scene 4 adds a second implementation ("red wrong variant ghost") beside the correct one. At 216-288px this doubles the diagram and makes it unreadable. The failure is already caught by TestTeeStopsOnDone (check_test.go), so the ghost belongs behind an optional toggle or in a caption.
- Spoiler handling can be simpler and stronger. Gating the fix toggle inside a Problem-tab figure creates a second spoiler gate. The existing gate in DocPane.tsx:57-68 already hides the Solution tab until reveal. Put the fix scenes in the Solution tab after that gate and keep only the scaffold symptom in the Problem tab. No router change, no second gate.
- Web-only is the right answer, but then the clone-mode artifact must be built. readme-diagram 14-tee-channel is still an exception at tools/check/lint.go:44 ("backlog"), and the README has no ASCII diagram. If the plan is web-only, it must add the today-vs-goal diagram to 14-tee-channel/README.md and delete that exception. Otherwise clone-mode learners get no explainer at all.
- The generator reads only .go, .md and go.mod from git archive HEAD (web/tools/gen/main.go:485), so visualisation code under web/src is invisible to it. That is fine, but the id-keyed registry must check its key against exerciseById (web/src/data.ts:41) so a renamed folder fails loudly instead of silently losing the figure.

**Why this tier.** The README states the alias bug verbatim; 14b covers the only depth worth animating.

<a id="15-or-channel-combinator"></a>
#### 15 — Or-Channel Combinator: Combining Shutdown Triggers
`Tier D` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** or-channel combinator: variadic fan-in of close-only signal channels with select and (optionally) recursion

**Core idea.** A close-only channel is a broadcast signal, and a combinator must watch every one of them: the combined channel closes the instant any input closes, and every watcher that did not win must be released rather than left parked on a channel that may never close.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** Blocking is invisible in prose and tests: in the scaffold one goroutine parks on channels[0] forever while the caller parks on the combined channel, so closing channels[3] produces no observable event except time passing. Likewise invisible are (a) the 1-channel relay goroutine that stays parked when its channel never closes, and (b) in the recursive design, the tail watcher orphaned whenever the current level's select wins on channels[0] or channels[1] instead of the recursive branch.

**Design.** A doc-pane scene shows three horizontal trigger lanes (healthCheckFailed, adminShutdown, deadlineExceeded), each a close-only channel drawn as a switch on a track, and below them a combined-output rail with a caller goroutine parked on it. Inside <code>or</code>, a watcher goroutine is drawn as a small token sitting on exactly one lane at the receive it is blocked on. Closing a lane flips its switch and flashes a "closed" pulse; a watcher sitting there wakes and closes the output rail, which wakes the caller. In the scaffold only channels[0] has a watcher, so firing any other lane flashes but changes nothing: the caller token stays parked, an elapsed-time counter climbs, and the output rail stays open. Scene two adds the edge cases (zero lanes: nothing to watch, never closes; one lane: passthrough versus a relay token that stays parked forever). Scene three gives every lane its own watcher and a shared close, showing any lane winning in one hop and all other tokens exiting; scene four shows the recursive chain, where a tail win climbs level by level and a top-level win leaves the tail watcher orphaned.

**Scenes.**
1. **Watcher parked on channels[0]** — Default scene. Three trigger lanes, one watcher token on lane 0, caller parked on the combined rail. Fire lane 0: watcher wakes, close(combined), caller unblocks. Fire lane 1 or 2: the lane's switch flips, the watcher never sees it, the caller stays parked, and the elapsed clock climbs with the rail still open. Annotations name both blocked receives.
2. **Zero and one channel** — Zero lanes: no watcher exists, the combined rail never closes, and the caller can never be released (correct, not a bug). One lane: two side-by-side runs. The passthrough returns the input channel and creates no goroutine; the relay spawns a token that stays parked on the never-closed channel while the combined rail closes on another path, leaving the token behind.
3. **Any lane can win** — N lanes, one watcher per lane, one shared output. Whichever lane closes first makes its watcher close the shared rail in a single hop; every other watcher wakes on the same close and exits, so the live-goroutine count returns to the caller alone. The trigger picker lets the learner fire the first, middle or last lane, and the channel count goes 1 / 3 / 20.
4. **The orphaned tail** — The recursive design drawn as stacked levels, each selecting on channels[0], channels[1] and the recursive tail. Fire the tail: the close climbs one level per wake-up. Fire a top level first: its select wins, but the tail watcher is still parked on channels further down that never close, so an orphan token remains after the output closes. A toggle threads this level's orDone into the tail and the orphan disappears.

**Controls.** Play / Pause; Step; Restart; Which trigger fires: first lane / middle lane / last lane / none; Channel count: 1 / 3 / 20; Show the corrected watcher design (off by default; off keeps the scaffold symptom); Thread orDone into the recursive tail (on/off, scene 4 only).

**Code anchors.**
- 15-or-channel-combinator/main.go:58 func or(channels ...<-chan struct{}) <-chan struct{}
- 15-or-channel-combinator/main.go:59-61 orDone := make(chan struct{}); if len(channels) == 0 { return orDone }
- 15-or-channel-combinator/main.go:64-67 go func() { defer close(orDone); <-channels[0] }()
- 15-or-channel-combinator/main.go:72-88 the demo's three named triggers and <-combined in main
- solutions/15-or-channel-combinator.md:55-66 Approach 1: one goroutine per channel selecting on orDone vs channels[i], with sync.Once closing the shared output
- solutions/15-or-channel-combinator.md:120-127 Approach 2: case 1 returns channels[0] (no relay goroutine)
- solutions/15-or-channel-combinator.md:191-223 Approach 3: recursive select with the tail, and the orphan fix at line 256 (append(channels[2:], orDone))

The bug is already named three ways before any picture: the ASCII diagram at 15-or-channel-combinator/README.md:10-16 says "closing 1 or 2 does nothing", the prose at README.md:18-21 says "blocked forever", and the test failure at 15-or-channel-combinator/check_test.go:69 prints "only channels[0]?". The visualisation adds the passing of time, not the fact.
- Scene 2 is self-contradictory and fights the solution doc. With exactly one input channel there is no "another path" that closes the combined rail, so the relay token cannot be left behind after a close that never happens. solutions/15-or-channel-combinator.md:94 calls Approach 1's single goroutine "correct, not a leak", so a "leak" label contradicts the recommended solution.

<a id="16-errgroup-failfast"></a>
#### 16 — Your Own errgroup: Concurrent Tasks with First-Error Capture
`Tier D` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: **no — draw it first**

**Pattern.** errgroup base Group: spawn-per-task with sync.WaitGroup, plus first-wins error capture (sync.Once or mutex)

**Core idea.** Go must hand the task to a new goroutine and return at once; Wait is the only blocking point, and it blocks on the WaitGroup counter reaching zero, not on a timer. "First error" means first to arrive at the capture point, not first registered, so the capture is a race that exactly one goroutine may win.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The scaffold is sequential, so it never races. The instant the learner makes Go concurrent, up to 50 tasks reach firstErr at the same instant, and the browser cannot show that race at all: no race detector, and GOMAXPROCS=1 (web/README.md, "Differences from running locally"). The learner also cannot see that wg.Add must run on the caller before `go`, and that the winning error is decided by scheduling, not by slice index. All of it is invisible in prose and in the tests, which report only elapsed time and one non-nil error.

**Design.** A narrow left-to-right timeline. The top lane is the caller goroutine (main). Below it, up to five task lanes appear one by one as Go is called. A thin rail along the bottom of each lane shows its state: running, sleeping/blocked, or exited. In scaffold mode the caller lane is filled by each task in turn — the Go call visibly sits on the caller for 100ms, then returns — and a small clock reads 500ms at the end while the Wait label on the caller lane does nothing. Switching to the "Go returns" mode, all five task lanes spring out at t=0 and their sleep segments overlap; the WaitGroup counter beside the lanes climbs 0→5 as Go is called and ticks 5→0 as each lane exits, and Wait's bar on the caller lane stays blocked until the counter hits 0. A separate small scene zooms on the Once gate: several error tokens arrive at nearly the same instant, one passes the gate and writes firstErr, the rest are dropped, and a red race band flags the unsynchronised write when the gate is removed.

**Scenes.**
1. **Scaffold: one task at a time** — Five 100ms tasks. Each Go call paints its work directly on the caller lane: start at t, blocked until t+100, return. The next call cannot begin before that. End clock 500ms; Wait is a no-op because nothing is left to wait for; Wait returns task 4's error because it was the second failure, not the fourth call.
2. **The race the scaffold hides** — A hypothetical run where Go returns and the tasks overlap. Tasks 2 and 4 both fail near t=100 and both reach the plain firstErr field. The renderer drops the Once gate so both writes land; a red race band marks the unsynchronised window, and a scheduler badge shows that the winner changes between runs. This is the state the learner's fix creates, and the browser test cannot catch it.
3. **WaitGroup: counter is the clock** — Fixed mode. Go calls return immediately (caller lane free from t=0). The counter climbs to 5, five sleep segments overlap, Done ticks the counter down, and Wait's bar unblocks exactly when the counter reaches 0 at t=100. Stepping one task's sleep to 300ms shows Wait following the slowest lane, not a fixed timer.
4. **First error wins** — Several tasks fail at once and queue at the Once gate. Exactly one token passes and writes firstErr; the others bounce off. A side panel shows Wait returning that same error twice, because it reads the field after wg.Wait rather than consuming a channel.

**Controls.** Play / pause / step (one event at a time) / restart; Mode switch: 'Go blocks the caller' (scaffold) vs 'Go returns' (fixed); Task count 5 → 50, to make the first-error race crowded; Fail together: make every task fail at the same instant instead of only 2 and 4; Wait early: call Wait while lanes are still running, to watch the counter gate it; Slow one lane to 300ms, so Wait visibly follows the slowest task; Speed / reduced-motion toggle that swaps to discrete step frames.

**Code anchors.**
- 16-errgroup-failfast/main.go:50-52 — Group's single unprotected firstErr field
- 16-errgroup-failfast/main.go:58-62 — Go calls f() inline: the blocking the first scene shows
- 16-errgroup-failfast/main.go:68-70 — Wait merely returns the field; it does not wait
- 16-errgroup-failfast/main.go:87-93 — the five 100ms tasks in main
- 16-errgroup-failfast/check_test.go:96-103 — the 300ms budget that turns blocking into a test failure
- 16-errgroup-failfast/check_test.go:107-120 — TestGroupFirstErrorRaceSafe: 50 tasks failing at once
- solutions/16-errgroup-failfast.md Approach 1 — wg.Add(1) before go, defer wg.Done(), once.Do writing firstErr, wg.Wait() then return firstErr
- solutions/16-errgroup-failfast.md Approach 2 — buffered chan error of capacity 1 with select/default; single-use Wait, so the tab must not claim Once is the only correct answer

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Scene 1 is factually wrong about the scaffold. It says Wait returns task 4's error. The scaffold runs the loop in slice order (main.go:85-94) and records the first non-nil error (main.go:59), so it returns "task 2: task 2 failed". Task 2 is the third call, not the fourth. The sentence "task 4's error because it was the second failure" is wrong on both the error and the call index.
- The timing in scene 1 does not match the failing test. The animation ends at 500ms (5 tasks x 100ms, main.go:75-81), but TestGroupRunsConcurrently runs 10 tasks and reports 1s against a 300ms budget (check_test.go:79-103). A learner who runs go test sees 1s while the animation says 500ms. Use 10 tasks in the animation, or label the count.
- spoiler_safe is not honest as designed. The proposal places the visualisation in a third tab (placement_hint: tab) with a default scaffold scene, but scenes 3 and 4 render wg.Add, defer wg.Done(), once.Do and wg.Wait. A tab is one click and no gate. The Solution tab is gated at DocPane.tsx:55-69; a Visualise tab becomes a backdoor to the same answer. Only a visualisation behind the existing spoiler gate is spoiler-safe.
- Scene 2 misattributes the race to the scaffold. check_test.go:107-114 says plainly that the naive version is never concurrent and will not trip the race detector; the scaffold is race-free by construction. Showing "the race the scaffold hides" as a hypothetical run of the scaffold teaches that the scaffold is racy. The race exists only after the learner makes Go concurrent and leaves the capture unguarded. Label it as the partial-fix state.
- The GOMAXPROCS=1 claim overstates the limit. One OS thread does not prevent a data race. Goroutines are preempted and can interleave between the `g.firstErr == nil` check and the write at main.go:59-60, so the buggy outcome can happen in the browser. What the browser cannot do is detect the race (no -race). Lanes drawn as parallel CPU work would be misleading; draw interleaving at scheduling points and say detection is unavailable.
- The 320px layout cannot hold the proposed picture. The doc pane can go to 260px (styles.css:506, minmax(260px, ...)). Caller lane + five task lanes + three registers does not fit at that width. The proposal's own mitigation (three lanes) contradicts its concept of five lanes appearing one by one and its 50-task control. A horizontal scroller is not acceptable for keyboard-only use.
- Effort is understated. Four scenes, seven controls, tab/route/prop changes (router.ts:9-18, DocPane.tsx:11 and 26-32, ExerciseView.tsx:10), light and dark, reduced motion, keyboard and ARIA is L, not M.
- routePath is cited as web/src/router.ts:18-21. It is lines 15-18; parseRoute is 9-13. Small, but the analyst's evidence should match the file.
- The design forks the teaching surface instead of using it. This exercise is already on the repo's visual backlog as an ASCII diagram: tools/check/lint.go:45 lists readme-diagram 16-errgroup-failfast as "backlog", with the instruction "Draw one, then delete the line." A README diagram works in both modes at near-zero cost; a JS-only animation works in one mode and leaves the clone reader with prose.
- The fixed-mode scene says Wait returns the same error twice. That is true of Approach 1 only. Approach 2's Wait drains a channel and is single-use (solutions/16-errgroup-failfast.md:149-153). Showing idempotent Wait as the fixed behaviour implies Once is the required answer, which the doc explicitly denies.
- Lazy loading is stated but not designed. A static map of scene modules pulls 43 entries into the main bundle. Use import.meta.glob with lazy imports, or put the scene spec in the per-exercise JSON that data.ts already fetches lazily.
- The generator drift risk is real but avoidable. web/tools/gen/main.go:484-486 reads only .go, .md and go.mod from HEAD, so line-number and symbol references in a web/src scene file would rot silently. A scene spec embedded in solutions/16-errgroup-failfast.md (as an HTML comment the Markdown renderer hides) is read by the generator, can be validated, and is spoiler-gated with the doc.

**Why this tier.** The README and test name hwm=1; the race scene is hypothetical and misattributed.

<a id="17-future-promise"></a>
#### 17 — Future/Promise Pattern: Async, Memoized Computation
`Tier D` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: **no — draw it first**

**Pattern.** Future/Promise: async, keyed, memoized computation (one shared computation per key, one private channel per caller, built from a mutex-guarded map plus sync.OnceValue)

**Core idea.** A future separates the work from the wait. Future must return at once, ComputeExpensive must run exactly once per key, and every caller must get its own channel to wait on.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** Returning a channel does not make anything async: in the scaffold the full 150 ms is paid inside Future, on the caller's goroutine, before the channel exists. Deduping the work is not the same as sharing the channel: one shared channel makes the first receiver win and leaves the rest blocked forever, or reading a zero value after close. And the map check-then-create window is a race in time, not a data race: -race reports nothing while two callers both start ComputeExpensive for the same key. None of these are visible from the prose or from a passing test run.

**Design.** A horizontal time axis 0–320 sim-ms holds one lane per goroutine and one strip for the shared cache. At t=0 three caller goroutines (C1, C2, C3) call Future("report-42"). In the scaffold scene, each lane turns the fail colour and stays solid for the full 150 ms: the label reads "inside Future, running ComputeExpensive" and a small marker shows that no channel exists yet. Three separate compute bars stack in the compute region, and a meter ticks to 3. In the correct scene, each lane returns from Future in about 0 ms (a short chip labelled "returns ch_i"), then all three lanes park on the same dashed "receive blocks" line; one compute lane fills for 150 ms; at t=150 the value fans out to all three receives at once, the lanes turn the pass colour, and the meter stays at 1. A cache strip above the lanes shows the key cell change from absent to "in flight" to "cached". A scene at t=250 adds a fourth call that returns in about 0 ms from the cached cell. A trap scene shows all three callers sharing one channel: the first receive takes the value, the other two stay parked past the end of the axis. Every scene is a scripted event list, so nothing depends on real scheduler timing; reduced motion renders one static frame per step. Web only: the cloned repo has no host for this, and its static counterpart is an ASCII diagram in the README.

**Scenes.**
1. **Scaffold: the caller pays** — Three caller lanes turn red at t=0 and stay solid for 150 ms. The label reads 'inside Future, running ComputeExpensive; no channel exists yet'. Three compute bars overlap. At t=150 each lane prints 'Future returned after 150ms', takes its buffered value at once, and the counter reads 3 of 3 callers.
2. **Correct: one compute, three channels** — Each caller returns from Future at about 0 ms with its own channel chip, then parks on a dashed 'receive blocks' line. One compute lane fills for 150 ms. At t=150 the value fans out to all three receives at the same instant, all lanes turn green, and the counter reads 1 of 3 callers.
3. **Cache hit** — At t=250 a fourth call finds the cache cell already 'cached'. Its channel returns in about 0 ms with the same value, the counter stays at 1, and the cache cell shows the value sitting there for the rest of the run.
4. **Trap: one channel for everyone** — All three callers share one channel. At t=150 only the first receive gets the value; the other two lanes stay parked past the end of the axis, or read 0 with the label 'zero value from a closed channel'. The counter still reads 1, which shows that deduping the work is not enough.

**Controls.** Play / Pause (keyboard: space); Step one event (keyboard: right arrow); Restart (keyboard: R); Scene selector: Scaffold / Correct run / Cache hit / Shared-channel trap; the three non-scaffold scenes sit behind a 'Show the fixed run' reveal; Callers: 1, 3, 20 (shows the counter stay at 1 while the number of parked receivers grows); Keys: one key, or two keys (shows one compute per key, two cells in the cache strip); Add a call after completion (toggles the cache-hit scene).

**Code anchors.**
- 17-future-promise/main.go:48-52: ch := make(chan int, 1); ch <- ComputeExpensive(key); close(ch); return ch
- 17-future-promise/main.go:71-76: the caller takes constructTime right after Future(key) returns, then receives; this is the 150 ms the visualisation puts on the caller lane
- 17-future-promise/mockcompute.go:12: const ComputeLatency = 150 * time.Millisecond
- 17-future-promise/mockcompute.go:26-31: ComputeExpensive sleeps, then increments callCount under callCountMu
- 17-future-promise/check_test.go: TestFutureReturnsImmediately (want under 10 ms), TestFutureDedupesConcurrentCallers (20 callers, CallCount == 1), TestFutureCachesAfterCompletion (second call instant, CallCount == 1)
- solutions/17-future-promise.md, Approach 1: the mutex-guarded cache map of sync.OnceValue closures, and the per-call goroutine that sends on the caller's own channel and closes it
- solutions/17-future-promise.md, Approach 2: entry.done closed after e.result is written, which is the broadcast the fan-out scene draws

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- The verdict is too high. The Problem text states the symptom at 17-future-promise/README.md:14-19 ("calls ComputeExpensive synchronously, on the calling goroutine ... blocks the caller for the full 150ms"), names sync.OnceValue plus a mutex-guarded map as the fix at README.md:52-58, main.go:72 and main.go:82 print the latency and the call count, and TestFutureReturnsImmediately names the symptom in its own failure message. Only the shared-channel trap and the blocked-versus-parked split are unique to the picture.
- The new tab is the wrong home. At the doc pane's CSS floor of 260px (web/src/styles.css:506, `minmax(260px, ...)`), the tab row (styles.css:540-557) has about 236px and does not wrap. Three tabs plus the difficulty badge need about 270px, and the passed badge (DocPane.tsx:35-39) pushes it further. The row overflows.
- A third tab that one of 43 exercises has makes the navigation shape inconsistent. It also adds a second spoiler gate ("Show the fixed run") beside the existing one in DocPane.tsx:60-68, which already holds the reveal state.
- The cache model matches the wrong approach. `entry.state in {absent, in-flight, cached(value)}` is Approach 2's entry/done design (solutions/17-future-promise.md, Approach 2). The recommended Approach 1 keeps a `func() int` in the map and the value inside sync.OnceValue, so it has no explicit in-flight flag and no value cell in the map. Drawing a value cell teaches a state machine the recommended solution does not have.
- The correct scene omits the per-call forwarder goroutines. In Approach 1 every call runs `go func(){ ch <- compute(); close(ch) }()`. Only the first forwarder runs ComputeExpensive; each caller gets its own ch_i. The proposal's own risk list names this as the main trap, but the scenes draw one compute lane and short "returns ch_i" chips only.
- The lock caption is wrong. "mu held: map lookup only" ignores the insert of a new sync.OnceValue on a miss, which is also under the mutex. The true statement is: the lock covers the lookup and the insert, and never covers compute().
- "The channel does not exist for the caller" is imprecise. main.go:48 creates the channel before the compute. The channel exists, but the caller does not hold it until main.go:52. A teaching caption must say that.
- The counter wording mixes two units. "3 of 3 callers" and "1 of 3 callers" read as caller counts. CallCount counts ComputeExpensive runs (mockcompute.go:39-42). Say "ComputeExpensive ran 3 times for 3 callers".
- The 20-caller option cannot be drawn as lanes at 236-288px of content width. At 12px per lane, 20 lanes leave no room for labels. Drop it, or draw one stack glyph labelled x20 and keep the lane count at 3.
- Seven controls plus a reveal button do not fit the pane: Play, Pause, Step, Restart, scene selector, callers, keys, add-call. Cut to Play/Pause, Step, Restart, and the scene selector.
- The keyboard plan as written implies a page-level handler for Space and the arrow keys. Space scrolls the doc body, and Monaco uses the arrow keys. Bind keys only while a widget control has focus, and make Play, Pause, Step and Restart real buttons.
- The scaffold lane is not a failure: it returns the correct value, and TestFutureReturnsCorrectResult passes. Painting it with the --fail colour conflates slow with wrong. Use muted or hatched for the 150ms wait and keep --fail for the trap's never-served receivers. Pair every colour with a text label or a pattern, because colour alone fails accessibility.
- The design targets 320px, but the user can drag the doc pane to 260px (styles.css:506). Design for 260px and let 320px be the comfortable case.
- The clone counterpart is not delivered by the plan. Web-only interactive is the right answer, but then 17-future-promise/README.md needs the ASCII diagram. The readme-diagram rule lists 17 as backlog at tools/check/lint.go:46. Without that diagram the clone gets no visual at all.
- The "-race stays quiet" claim is only true when each map operation is individually synchronized, for example a sync.Map or a mutex released between load and store. A plain map read and write from two goroutines does trip -race. State the condition or the caption teaches a false rule.
- Effort M is low for the first widget. The frame model, the lanes renderer, keyboard and reduced-motion handling, and the DocPane work together cost about L. That cost amortizes only if other exercises follow.

**Why this tier.** The README names sync.OnceValue and the mutex map; the fix is the prose.

<a id="20-concurrent-map-reduce"></a>
#### 20 — Concurrent Map-Reduce: Parallel Word Count
`Tier D` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: **no — draw it first**

**Pattern.** Map-reduce: a fan-out map phase (one goroutine per chunk, each building a private map[string]int) followed by a sync.WaitGroup barrier and a single-goroutine, sequential reduce merge.

**Core idea.** Keep the map phase free of shared mutable state, wait at a barrier for every map goroutine, then merge once on one goroutine. Ten 30 ms sleeps overlap into about one 30 ms wait, and wg.Wait() is the happens-before edge that makes the lock-free reduce safe.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The scaffold is correct and never errors; its only defect is wall-clock time (300 ms vs ~30 ms), which is visible only under synctest's fake clock. The obvious fix (a goroutine per chunk writing one shared result map) passes plain `go test` yet is a data race that only `-race` reports, and the browser cannot run `-race`. So a learner cannot see the map-lane interleaving, the barrier that gates the reduce, or the shared-map collision.

**Design.** A single timeline from 0 to 300 ms on a fake clock. Ten chunk lanes stack vertically. In the default scene one lane is lit at a time: a 30 ms sleep bar, then a short tokenize bar that increments the one shared result map, then the next lane starts, ending at 300 ms with a stamp quoting the test's failure. In a second scene all ten sleep bars start at 0; at 30 ms ten arrows converge on one result map and two lanes collide on "fox", so a cell loses its update and a stamp reads "undefined behaviour — plain go test may pass; -race fails". In the map-reduce scene each lane fills its own small private map, with no arrow to the shared map; at 30 ms every lane meets a vertical barrier labelled "wg.Wait(): 10 → 0"; only then does a single reduce lane light up and fold the ten partial maps into one, matching the expected counts at ~31 ms. A second thin axis maps the chunk slider: the sequential total stretches linearly while the map-reduce total stays flat.

**Scenes.**
1. **Sequential baseline — the scaffold** — Ten lanes stacked on a 0–300 ms axis, only one lit at a time: a 30 ms sleep bar, a short tokenize bar that increments the single result map, then the next lane. The clock reaches 300 ms and a stamp quotes TestWordCountConcurrency: 'took 300ms, budget 100ms — chunks are processed one at a time.' The map is always correct; only the time is serial. This is the default scene.
2. **One goroutine per chunk, one shared map — the trap** — All ten lanes sleep from 0 to 30 ms together, then ten arrows converge on one shared result map; two lanes touch the same key ('fox') in the same instant. A cell loses an update and a stamp reads 'undefined behaviour — plain go test may pass; go test -race fails: WARNING: DATA RACE / fatal error: concurrent map writes'. The run is fast but wrong or unsafe.
3. **Map into private partials, then the barrier** — The same ten overlapping 30 ms lanes, but each fills its own private map during its window, with no arrow to the shared map. At 30 ms every lane reaches a vertical barrier labelled 'wg.Wait(): 10 → 0'; the reduce lane has been dimmed and blocked since t=0. When the counter hits zero, the barrier opens.
4. **Serial reduce — merge the partial maps** — One reduce lane lights at ~30 ms and folds the ten partial maps into one result, partial by partial. Duplicate words add up (fox 2+…=7, the 8) and the final map equals the expected counts. The clock stops near 31 ms, with the note 'one writer, so no mutex — wg.Wait() is the happens-before edge.'

**Controls.** Play, Pause, Step, Restart — real buttons, keyboard reachable, with Space/arrow-key bindings; Step advances one event and doubles as the prefers-reduced-motion mode.; Scenario: Sequential | Shared-map trap | Map-reduce — swaps the event schedule; defaults to Sequential so the scaffold's symptom shows first.; Chunks: 3 | 10 | 30 — the sequential total grows linearly while the map-reduce total stays flat; this teaches the scaling.; Slow one chunk (90 ms) — one lane runs long, so the barrier holds the reduce until the slowest lane finishes; total time becomes the max, not the sum.; Speed 0.5x–4x — visual pace only; the clock readout stays in fake-clock milliseconds.; Show expected counts — overlays the correct fox: 7 / the: 8 values so a lost update is visible as a short count..

**Code anchors.**
- 20-concurrent-map-reduce/main.go:46-50 — the scaffold's sequential `for _, chunk := range chunks { time.Sleep(ProcessDelay); … result[word]++ }`; scene 1 animates this exact loop.
- 20-concurrent-map-reduce/mockchunks.go:7 — `ProcessDelay = 30 * time.Millisecond`; every lane bar is 30 ms wide on the fake clock.
- 20-concurrent-map-reduce/check_test.go:109-131 — `TestWordCountConcurrency`; `sequentialTime = 10 * ProcessDelay` (122) and `budget = 100 * time.Millisecond` (123) are the timeline's 300 ms total and failed budget.
- 20-concurrent-map-reduce/check_test.go:139-152 — `TestWordCountRace`; scene 2's shared-map collision is the defect this test exists to catch under `-race`.
- solutions/20-concurrent-map-reduce.md:74-97 (Approach 1, banded lines 13 and 17 = doc lines 86 and 90) — `partials[i] = local` and `wg.Wait()`; scenes 3 and 4 render this behaviour without printing the code.
- solutions/20-concurrent-map-reduce.md:189-204 — the 'common mistake' shared-map variant (band {13} is the shared `result[word]++`); scene 2 visualises it code-free.

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- The timeline mixes two clocks. In `testing/synctest` only `time.Sleep` advances fake time; tokenize and merge cost zero fake time. `check_test.go:122-123` makes the sequential run exactly 300ms and a correct map-reduce run exactly 30ms. The proposed 'short tokenize bar' and 'clock stops near 31 ms' are therefore wrong on the synctest axis. Pick one axis and label it. Also, 'visible only under synctest's fake clock' is imprecise: a real-clock run would also exceed 100ms; synctest only makes the figure exact and fast.
- The trap scene draws 'two lanes touch fox in the same instant'. The site runs GOMAXPROCS=1 (web/README.md), so goroutines interleave, not run in parallel. Draw the interleaved read-modify-write that loses the update: c3 reads fox=2, c7 reads fox=2, c3 writes 3, c7 writes 3. That is also the honest reason a plain run may pass while `-race` reports it.
- The design does not fit 320 CSS px. Ten per-lane annotation strings, 30 lanes, and a second chunk-scaling axis cannot fit, and the hard constraints forbid hover-only labels. Replace them with one short legend (private map / shared map / barrier) plus one caption line per step; cap lanes at ten; drop the second axis.
- The Chunks control contradicts the test. The test is fixed at ten chunks with a 100ms budget (`check_test.go:122-123`). At three chunks the sequential run is 90ms and would pass. Without a 100ms budget line, the 'scaling' scene shows the scaffold being fine, which confuses the lesson. Fix chunks at ten and let the budget line carry the point, or explain the ten-chunk threshold in the caption.
- The 'Slow one chunk (90 ms)' control stays under the 100ms budget, so it cannot show a failure; it only shows max-not-sum. Use 120ms, or label the scene as deliberately under budget.
- Drift has no guard. The visual would duplicate `ProcessDelay`, the ten chunks, and the fox/the counts. `web/tools/gen/main.go:484-486` reads only .go, .md and go.mod, so the generator cannot emit or check visuals. Add a `tools/check` rule that compares the TS constants with `mockchunks.go:7` and `check_test.go:52-53,66`, or make the visual word-agnostic.
- A third tab is the wrong default. `.doc-tabs` is a 41px row (`web/src/styles.css:540-546`) that already holds two tabs, a spacer, a difficulty badge and a conditional pass badge (`DocPane.tsx:26-39`); at 320px a third tab overflows once the pass badge appears. A tab present on one exercise and absent on the other 42 is also inconsistent navigation.
- `spoiler_safe: true` is true only for this exercise, and only because the README already states the fix ('spawn one goroutine per chunk ... its own local map ... sync.WaitGroup ... a single reduce step'). As a general feature, an ungated Visual tab with a fix scenario is a spoiler bypass around the Solution gate. State that explicitly and gate fix scenes for any exercise whose README does not already reveal the shape.
- The proposal does not close the repo's own visual gap. `20-concurrent-map-reduce/README.md` still has no ASCII diagram; `tools/check/lint.go:47` keeps it on the `readme-diagram` backlog. A web visual does not satisfy that rule, so the offline path stays behind while the exception stays in the lint file.

Cut v1 to three scenes and one control row.
- Accessibility details are missing. Under `prefers-reduced-motion` auto-play must not start (`styles.css:1067` only disables transitions). A global Space binding collides with native button activation and page scroll. Use native buttons plus a focusable group with Arrow/Home keys, and add the visual's transitions to the reduced-motion block.

<a id="23-sharded-cache"></a>
#### 23 — Sharded Concurrent Cache: Reducing Lock Contention
`Tier D` · **nice** · lock and waiters · family `lock` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Sharding a lock (fine-grained locking): split one mutex-protected map into N per-shard mutex + map pairs, so unrelated keys never share a lock while the same key still serializes.

**Core idea.** A mutex is not the problem; its scope is. One lock held across the whole call to work makes a slow key block an unrelated fast key. Per-shard locks keep the same critical section but split it, so keys in different shards never queue behind each other, and keys in the same shard still do.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The scaffold is race-free, so -race passes and two of the three tests pass. The only defect is invisible waiting. The learner cannot see that (a) work() runs while the lock is held, so the lock hold equals the caller's work duration; (b) key "b" waits on the same mutex, not on key "a" or on the map; (c) after sharding, work still runs under a lock, and two keys can still collide in one shard because they share a first byte. The prose says "different keys should not block", but nothing in the tests or the editor shows the block itself.

**Design.** Two horizontal lanes enter a cache. Lane A calls Do("apple-key", work that sleeps 200ms). Lane B calls Do("banana-key", work that is instant) 20ms later. In the default state there is one lock at the cache door. A takes it, the lock turns solid red, and A's lane shows a work bar growing inside the held region. B's arrow runs to the same door and stops. A small queue forms behind B, and a clock beside B reads 180ms to completion, past a 50ms budget line. Behind the door, one map holds both keys. A formula chip shows shard("apple-key") = 1 and shard("banana-key") = 2, so the two keys share no data at all. The fix state keeps the same lanes and the same bars, but replaces the one door with a 4x4 grid of 16 locks and 16 maps. Shard 1 is red and holds A. Shard 2 is green, so B passes at 0ms and never crosses the budget line. A collision preset gives B the key "apricot-key" (first byte 'a' = 97), so both keys map to shard 1 and B waits again even with 16 shards.

**Scenes.**
1. **One lock, two keys (default)** — The scaffold. Lane A holds the single mutex for 200ms while work sleeps. Lane B arrives at 20ms, parks in the waiter queue, and completes at about 200ms. Its latency bar passes the 50ms budget line and turns red. Shard chips show key A in shard 1 and key B in shard 2, so they never touch the same data.
2. **Same key, many goroutines** — Fifty callers on "shared-key" queue at one lock and pass one at a time. The map cell steps 1, 2, 3 ... 50 with no lost update. The scene states that this serialization is required for correctness, and that sharding does not remove it.
3. **Sixteen locks (behind the reveal)** — The same two lanes and the same work bars. A 4x4 grid of shards replaces the single door. Shard 1 is held by apple-key. Shard 2 is free, so banana-key proceeds at 20ms. B's latency stays near 0ms while A's 200ms bar still runs. The end card shows the three tests passing.
4. **Collision: same first byte, same shard** — Caller B uses "apricot-key". The hash chip shows 97 % 16 = 1 for both keys. Both lanes aim at shard 1, B queues behind A, and the wait returns. Sharding groups keys by hash bucket, not by logical key.

**Controls.** Play / Pause (Space): run or freeze the scripted timeline; always focusable, no hover needed; Step (Right arrow or N): advance exactly one event (call, lock_wait, lock_acq, map_read, work, map_write, unlock, wake, done); Restart (R): reset to t=0 on the single-lock scaffold; Work duration: 50 / 200 / 400 ms, so the learner sees B's wait track A's lock hold exactly; Caller B's key: banana-key (shard 2) or apricot-key (shard 1); the second value switches on the collision lesson; Reveal the fix (16 shards): off by default; swaps the one lock for the shard grid, gated by one click like the Solution tab; Scene picker: One lock / Same key / Sixteen locks / Collision.

**Code anchors.**
- 23-sharded-cache/main.go:47-50 Cache{mu sync.Mutex; data map[string]int}: the one lock and one map the default scene draws
- 23-sharded-cache/main.go:62-70 Do: lock, read, work, write, unlock; the scene's held region is lines 63-68
- 23-sharded-cache/main.go:63-64 c.mu.Lock / defer c.mu.Unlock: the wait B is parked in
- 23-sharded-cache/main.go:67 next := work(cur): work runs inside the lock, so the hold lasts as long as the caller's work
- 23-sharded-cache/main.go:81-91 the demo's 200ms work on "a", 10ms gap, timed Do on "b"
- 23-sharded-cache/check_test.go:134-139 slowWork 200ms, startGap 20ms, budget 50ms: the scene's clock and budget line
- 23-sharded-cache/check_test.go:157-160 elapsed measurement and the failure message
- 23-sharded-cache/check_test.go:73-77 keyA "apple-key" and keyB "banana-key", chosen for shards 1 and 2

- web/src/components/DocPane.tsx:26-32 the tab row that would gain a third tab
- web/src/data.ts Exercise payload and loadExercise: per-exercise lazy fetch the visualisation should follow
- web/tools/gen/main.go reads only .go, .md and go.mod from HEAD, so scene data cannot come from the exercise folder
- tools/check/lint.go:154 layout rule: no new files in 23-sharded-cache/
- web/README.md GOMAXPROCS=1, no race detector: the browser cannot show true two-core parallelism

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Verdict is one tier too high. The visual adds a picture of the queue, not a hidden fact. The claim that the visual is 'the only place the symptom exists' is false.

An unconditional tab gives the other 42 exercises an empty tab. Add 'visualise' to Route.tab, routePath, DocPane (DocPane.tsx:11), and ExerciseView (ExerciseView.tsx:10). Keep the collision toggle, but label it 'hashing tradeoff, not a bug', or a learner reads the fixed solution as still broken.

<a id="25-graceful-multistage-shutdown"></a>
#### 25 — Graceful Multi-Stage Shutdown
`Tier D` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Worker-pool completion barrier: a sync.WaitGroup gates the close of a done channel, so the signal means "every process() call returned", not "Start() returned".

**Core idea.** A channel close is a promise only when a proven-finished event causes it. `done := make(chan struct{}); close(done)` promises nothing about the workers. The barrier must be the last `process(item)` return in every worker, and a closer goroutine converts the blocking `wg.Wait()` into that close so `Start` still returns at once.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The learner reads "done closes too early" and still cannot see the shape: at the instant `<-done` unblocks, 0 of 20 jobs are processed, the producer is blocked in its first send, and four workers are about to receive. Nor can the learner see the boundary between "the worker drained `jobs`" and "the worker's `process()` call returned" — the gap that `go process(item)` hides. Nor that the processed count at the barrier is scheduling-dependent (0, sometimes a few) while the wrongness is constant. The prose also hides the consequence: the caller is now free to close the sink while an in-flight `process()` is about to write into it.

**Design.** Five lanes share one time axis: a producer, four workers, and, in the fixed scenes, a closer. A jobs channel feeds the worker lanes from the top. A sink (the caller's file, DB connection or socket) sits at the bottom. A single vertical done barrier cuts the time axis. In the default scene the barrier sits at tick 0, before any worker lane leaves its start marker: the counter reads 0 of 20 processed, and 20 jobs wait in the channel. The lanes keep moving behind the barrier. The caller lane, unblocked by the close, prints "shutdown complete" and then closes the sink. From that tick on, every `process()` return writes into a closed sink, and the write is marked dropped. The fixed scene does one thing: it moves the barrier to the tick where the last worker leaves its lane. The visual is web-only. The clone-mode counterpart is the ASCII diagram already in the exercise README.

**Scenes.**
1. **Naive: done fires at tick 0** — Start spawns four worker lanes, creates and closes done, and returns, all before any jane lane starts. The barrier cuts at tick 0. Behind it the producer is blocked in its first send, 0 of 20 jobs are processed, and all 20 still wait on the channel. The caller's receive on the closed channel does not block, so 'shutdown complete' prints first. Counters: processed 0/20 at the barrier, queued 20, dropped 0.
2. **Naive: the caller tears down the sink** — The caller closes the sink right after the barrier. The producer and the workers keep running behind it. Every process() return after that tick writes into the closed sink and is marked dropped. The end counter shows 20 dropped writes. A reshuffle control moves the processed-at-barrier count between 0 and a few, so the learner sees the count is scheduling-dependent while the wrongness is not.
3. **Fixed: the barrier waits for every lane** — A closer lane blocks on wg.Wait(). Four wg.Done events land, one as each worker returns from range jobs after its last process() call. The counter reaches zero, then close(done) cuts the barrier. The caller lane's teardown starts after the last write. Counters at the barrier: 20/20 processed, 0 dropped.
4. **Near-miss: the loop drains before the work finishes** — The worker loops use go process(item), so range jobs empties while two process() calls are still blocked. The workers exit, wg.Wait returns, and the barrier lands with two jobs in flight. The caller's sink teardown then drops those two writes. This variant passes any timing-only test.

**Controls.** Play, pause, step and restart, each a button with a keyboard shortcut; no control depends on hover; Process latency: 0, 5, 10, 20 ms. At 0 the loss window closes and the bug leaves the screen, which teaches that the defect is a timing window; Teardown at done: on or off. Off shows the signal is wrong even when nobody closes a resource; Reshuffle schedule: reassigns items to workers and shifts tick offsets. The processed count at the barrier varies; the barrier position does not; Hand off with go process(item): swaps in the near-miss script; Show the fix: swaps in the WaitGroup script. This control sits behind the same reveal gate as the Solution tab.

**Code anchors.**
- 25-graceful-multistage-shutdown/main.go:81-82 — done := make(chan struct{}); close(done) is the barrier the default scene cuts at tick 0
- 25-graceful-multistage-shutdown/main.go:72-84 — Start returns an already-closed channel
- 25-graceful-multistage-shutdown/main.go:74-78 — each worker's range jobs / process(item) loop, the lanes the barrier ignores
- 25-graceful-multistage-shutdown/main.go:91-96 — the producer's send loop and close(jobs)
- 25-graceful-multistage-shutdown/main.go:98-107 — the caller's <-done and the 'shutdown complete' print that wins the race
- 25-graceful-multistage-shutdown/check_test.go TestStartProcessesEveryJob — asserts processed == 20 at the instant done fires; the default scene shows 0
- 25-graceful-multistage-shutdown/check_test.go TestDoneWaitsForInFlightProcessCall — the deterministic in-flight block; scene 4 is the variant it catches
- solutions/25-graceful-multistage-shutdown.md:71-89 — wg.Add(1) at :74, defer wg.Done() at :76, wg.Wait(); close(done) at :85-86
- solutions/25-graceful-multistage-shutdown.md:95-98 — the design notes the annotations repeat

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Wrong queue count on an unbuffered channel: 25-graceful-multistage-shutdown/main.go:89 is `jobs := make(chan int)` (capacity 0), yet the proposal says "queued 20", "20 jobs wait on the channel", and lists "a jobs channel (cap 0)" in the same data model. At the barrier exactly one send is pending; the other 19 job IDs are still in the producer's loop. A queue-depth gauge reading 20 is impossible and self-contradictory.
- The latency control teaches the opposite of the truth: main.go:81-82 closes done before any worker is scheduled, so process latency 0 does not close the loss window. Verified with a zero-latency probe under GOMAXPROCS=1: 0 of 400 job-trials were processed at the barrier. The defect is missing synchronization, not a timing window, so "at 0 the bug leaves the screen" is false.
- The reshuffle control is unsupported in the browser: web/README.md:43 fixes GOMAXPROCS=1, and under it the naive count at the barrier is always 0. check_test.go:82 printed "0/20 jobs actually processed" on every one of 5 runs. "Varies between 0 and a few" needs a multi-core machine, which the browser runner is not.
- The sink scene is invented: main.go:98-107 only sleeps and prints; no sink is ever closed. "20 dropped writes" asserts a consequence that no committed code and no test shows. The proposal's own risk 3 admits this, yet scene 2 keeps the number and calls it a counter.
- The near-miss caption is false: "This variant passes any timing-only test." I ran the `go process(item)` variant against the committed tests. TestStartProcessesEveryJob fails at check_test.go:82 with 0/20, and TestDoneWaitsForInFlightProcessCall fails at check_test.go:208. It passes no test in this suite.
- spoiler_safe is not honest as specified: the scenes list offers the fixed scene (scene 3) and the wrong-fix variant (scene 4) as ordinary scenes, not behind a reveal control. DocPane.tsx:17 keeps the reveal in local state, so "the same spoiler gate" cannot be reused across a new route without hoisting it into a shared component.
- The routing change is understated: router.ts:7 and router.ts:11-12 whitelist exactly "problem"/"solution", and router.ts:17 maps only solution. A third tab also changes DocPane.tsx:11, ExerciseView.tsx:10, and App.tsx:86. The claim "parseRoute already parameterises the tab" is not true today.

- Five lanes as five columns do not fit 320px: labels such as "producer" and "worker 0" need more than the roughly 55px per column that 320px leaves. Time must run down horizontal rows, or the lanes must be rows with a windowed time axis.
- Reduced motion is underspecified: "a step mode must exist" is not enough. The first frame must be static and playback off until the user presses play, or the component animates for users who asked it not to. styles.css:1067 shows the app already honours this preference elsewhere.
