<a id="26-pipeline-error-handling"></a>
#### 26 — Pipeline Error Handling: Result Values Instead of Aborting
`Tier D` · **nice** · pipeline graph · family `graph` · effort M · README diagram: **no — draw it first**

**Pattern.** Per-item error propagation in a multi-stage pipeline: failures travel as Result{Value, Err} values on the same channel, never as a stage-level return; the only legitimate early exit is cancellation via done.

**Core idea.** A bad item is data, not control flow. Each input must leave exactly one Result at the far end of the pipeline; a per-item failure must set that Result's Err and continue the loop, while only a closed done may end the stage goroutine. The two must never be the same `return`.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** Two things are invisible from the prose and a passing run. (1) There are two independent truncation walls: parse's `return` at main.go:98 closes parse's channel via `defer close(out)` at main.go:92, which ends reciprocal's `for res := range in` at main.go:119, so fixing only parse still loses the tail on the next "0" at reciprocal's `return` at main.go:124. (2) A parse-failure Result arrives with Value == 0, the very value reciprocal treats as invalid, so a Value-only guard overwrites the parse error with ErrDivideByZero and the caller can no longer tell the two causes apart. Neither is visible per-item; you only see counts (1 of 8) and a test message.

**Design.** Eight readings become eight rows in a ledger, with three stage columns: the raw string, parse, reciprocal, and a fourth column for the caller's returned slice. A cursor walks down the rows. Row 0 ("10") turns into Result{10} and lands in the caller. At row 1 ("abc") parse's goroutine calls Atoi, gets an error, and instead of building a Result it executes `return`; a wall drops across the parse column as its deferred close fires, the reciprocal column receives nothing more and its range loop ends, and rows 2 through 7 fade into dashed outlines labeled "never read". A counter above the ledger reads "1 Result for 8 readings". The same ledger then replays with the parse error removed: row 3 ("0") reaches reciprocal, its Value-only check fires `return`, and the tail from row 4 on goes dashed again -- proving the second wall. A final scene shows the collision: a Result carrying Value 0 and a parse Err enters reciprocal; a switch labeled "guard" flips between "Value only" (the parse error is stamped over with ErrDivideByZero) and "Err == nil && Value == 0" (the parse error passes through untouched). The goal scene runs all eight rows to the caller, each bad row carrying a small red Err tag, and a "close done" button then cancels every row at once to show that done stops everything while an error stops nothing.

**Scenes.**
1. **Scaffold: the first bad reading ends the batch** — Rows 0-7 in a ledger. Row 0 passes. At row 1 ("abc") parse's Atoi error hits the early return at main.go:98; the deferred close paints a wall across the parse column, reciprocal's range ends, and rows 2-7 stay dashed as 'never read'. Counter: 1 Result for 8 readings.
2. **Scaffold, second wall: reciprocal ends the batch on 0** — A control swaps the batch for one with no parse errors: "10","5","0","-3","7". Rows 0-1 pass; at "0" reciprocal's Value-only check returns at main.go:124 and rows 3-4 become dashed. Teaches that fixing only parse leaves a second truncation point.
3. **The collision: Value 0 means two different things** — A parse-failure Result {Value: 0, Err: invalid reading "abc"} enters reciprocal. A guard switch shows 'Value only' stamping ErrDivideByZero over the parse error (the label flips and errors.Is(res.Err, ErrDivideByZero) wrongly becomes true) versus 'Err == nil && Value == 0' letting it pass unchanged.
4. **Goal: one Result per reading, errors as values** — All eight rows reach the caller, good rows green with their integer, bad rows carrying a short Err tag (parse error at 1 and 5, ErrDivideByZero at 3 and 6). Counter: 8 Results for 8 readings. A 'close done' button then cancels every remaining row at once, contrasting intentional shutdown with a per-item failure.

**Controls.** Play / pause the scripted run; Step forward one event (and step back); Restart scene; Batch picker: 8 mixed / no parse error / all valid - switches which scaffold wall is visible; Guard switch: Value only vs Err == nil && Value == 0 - shows the error collision; Caller: drain vs pause - pausing makes both sends read 'blocked on send'; Close done now - cancels the whole pipeline, contrast with a bad item; Scene switcher with 4 labeled scenes; all controls keyboard reachable (Space, ArrowRight, R).

**Code anchors.**
- 26-pipeline-error-handling/main.go:95-99 - parse's `if err != nil { return }`, the first wall
- 26-pipeline-error-handling/main.go:92 and 118 - `defer close(out)`, the mechanism that turns a stage return into a truncated downstream range
- 26-pipeline-error-handling/main.go:119 - `for res := range in` ends when parse closes its channel
- 26-pipeline-error-handling/main.go:120-124 - reciprocal's `if res.Value == 0 { return }`, the second wall
- 26-pipeline-error-handling/main.go:101-105 and 127-131 - the select with the `case <-done` arm, the only legitimate early exit
- 26-pipeline-error-handling/main.go:140-147 - ProcessReadings drains into a slice; its length is the counter
- solutions/26-pipeline-error-handling.md:109-114 - the fixed parse builds Result{Value: 0, Err: ...} instead of returning
- solutions/26-pipeline-error-handling.md:135 - `if res.Err == nil && res.Value == 0`, the guard that prevents the error collision
- solutions/26-pipeline-error-handling.md:152-159 - one Result per reading, always
- 26-pipeline-error-handling/check_test.go:136 TestProcessingContinuesPastBadReadings - indices 2, 4, 7 come after bad readings and are exactly the ones a truncating stage loses

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- The stated blind spot is refuted by the visible prose. The scaffold's header comment names both walls at 26-pipeline-error-handling/main.go:14-18 (parse returns on Atoi failure; reciprocal returns on Value 0), and the README repeats both at 26-pipeline-error-handling/README.md:10-16. So 'two independent truncation walls ... invisible from the prose' is false. Only the interaction 'fixing parse alone still leaves a wall' is non-obvious, and solutions/26-pipeline-error-handling.md:182 states it plainly.
- The 'collision' is also visible to the learner. README.md:38-47 says reciprocal must 'never overwrite an existing error, never even look at a Value that was never valid'. check_test.go:108-113 asserts errors.Is(res.Err, ErrDivideByZero) for parse failures and prints the exact rule in the failure text ('a failed parse's Value defaults to 0 which must NOT be re-validated as a divide-by-zero'). A learner who writes the Value-only guard is taught the lesson by the test message.
- Scene 3 misstates the scaffold. reciprocal in HEAD never stamps ErrDivideByZero: it returns at main.go:120-125. The proposed data_model event set-err(i, ErrDivideByZero) and the 'Value only stamps the parse error' frame describe a hypothetical wrong fix, not code that exists at HEAD. As written, the 'deterministic explainer tied to code' shows a state the program cannot reach.
- The 'fixing only parse still loses the tail on the next 0' wording is imprecise. With the shipped readings, the parse-failure Result at index 1 has Value 0, so reciprocal's Value-only check at main.go:120 fires on that item immediately; it never reaches a later '0'. Scene 2's swap to a parse-error-free batch does isolate the second wall, so the scene is right but the stated rationale is wrong.
- spoiler_safe: true is not honest. Scene 3 displays the correct guard `res.Err == nil && res.Value == 0`, and scene 4 shows the completed 8-result run. Both are reachable from a visible scene switcher and guard control without the Solution tab's reveal (web/src/components/DocPane.tsx:57-68). Labelling them 'goal' is not a gate. Scene 2 also teaches part of the fix. Only scene 1 is spoiler-safe.
- A third tab is the wrong structure for 43 exercises. DocPane.tsx:26-32 hardcodes two tabs; router.ts:6 accepts only 'problem' | 'solution'; ExerciseView.tsx:10 and App.tsx:86 repeat that union. A third tab needs edits in four files plus a dead or empty state on the other 42 exercises. The cleaner answer to the user's question is 'existing tabs': symptom figure in Problem, fix scenes in Solution after the existing reveal. That removes the empty state and the spoiler gate together.
- 'Reuses the existing lazy per-exercise payload' is false. The site lazy-loads JSON data (web/src/data.ts:43-59), not code. web/tools/gen/main.go:485 admits only .go, .md and go.mod from git archive HEAD, and the payload struct (main.go:66-70) has no visual field, so scene data cannot ride the existing JSON. The widget needs new files under web/src (allowed: tools/check/lint.go:154 forbids them only inside exercise folders) plus a dynamic-import registry. No `lazy(` or `import(` exists in web/src today, so this is new infrastructure, not reuse.
- The repo's mandatory artifact is missing from the proposal. tools/check/lint.go:256-257 requires every README to hold an ASCII 'today-vs-goal' diagram, and 26 is an exception with reason 'backlog' (lint.go:48). An ASCII diagram works in clone mode and the web site and clears the exception. A web-only ledger does not, and leaves clone mode with no diagram at all. This is the strongest reason to downgrade: the ASCII diagram is the required deliverable, and the interactive widget is optional on top.
- The design is over-scoped. It carries four scenes and six or more controls (play/pause, step forward and back, restart, batch picker, guard switch, caller drain/pause, close done, scene switcher) for one rule: a bad item is data, only done stops the pipeline. The 'caller drain vs pause' control teaches backpressure and done semantics, which check_test.go:177-220 already covers and which is not the per-item-error lesson. The lead analyst's own risk 6 concedes the ledger is a table unless blocking is shown; adding blocking is what forces the extra control.
- 320 CSS px is tight for four stage columns plus a scene switcher plus six controls in a pane whose default width is 40% of the viewport (styles.css:506) and which also scrolls. Annotations such as 'defer close(out) - the stage goroutine is gone' cannot fit a ~69px cell and must become a second line under the current row, which the proposal says but does not design for the narrow case.
- Motion and keyboard policy is unspecified. The proposal says needs_motion: false but offers Play, which implies auto-advance; it must be disabled or degraded to Step under prefers-reduced-motion (styles.css:1067 only disables sidebar and chip transitions today). Global Space and ArrowRight handlers will also fight the scrollable doc body and Space-activated buttons; handlers must be scoped to a focusable widget container.
- No automated coverage is designed, only listed as a risk. The repo already runs TS directly with Node (node scripts/smoke-test.ts), so a pure scene-data module and a pure stateAt reducer can be tested with node:test and no new dependency. The corrected design should make that a requirement, because the scene list must not drift from the solution doc.
- Concurrency fidelity: the 'one send pairs with the next receive in the same tick' model hides that parse computes item i+1 while reciprocal may be blocked sending item i to a paused caller. That blocked-send state is only reachable by pausing the caller, a state check_test.go:193-199 creates for the done test, not for the per-item-error story. If the widget never shows a blocked send in its default story, the analyst's own risk 6 applies: it is a table.

**Why this tier.** The README states both truncation walls; a ledger is a table. Draw the missing ASCII diagram.

<a id="34-prime-sieve"></a>
#### 34 — Concurrent Prime Sieve: A Growing Pipeline That Must Learn to Shut Down
`Tier D` · **nice** · pipeline graph · family `graph` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Growing pipeline with a shared done channel (or-done cancellation) — and a goroutine leak hidden behind a correct return value

**Core idea.** A pipeline that grows one stage per prime must be given one real done channel, and that channel must be closed once. Closing broadcasts to every stage at the same instant, so the whole chain stops when the reader stops reading. Passing nil defeats the guard silently, and the values stay correct while every stage leaks.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** Nothing in the output shows the failure. Primes returns the right primes. TestPrimesReturnsCorrectPrimes passes. But after Primes returns, each of the n+1 goroutines sits blocked forever on `out <- i`, with its next candidate held in the send. The blocked send is invisible in the terminal, and a static diagram cannot show that every stage is stuck at the same time. Two more things are invisible: `case <-done:` with done == nil can never fire, so the or-done code in generate and filter is already dead; and the tempting fix — wait for the chain to drain, then close — deadlocks because every stage is blocked on send and nobody reads.

**Design.** A narrow left-to-right chain of small stage boxes in the doc pane: generate at the left, then filter(2), filter(3), filter(5), and so on. Each box is one goroutine with an in-edge and an out-edge. Integer tokens travel rightward along the edges. A filter drops its multiples with a short discard flick. As the reader pulls values from the right end, a new filter box slides in per prime. A primes tray and a goroutine counter sit above the chain. In the default scene the reader leaves: the tray disappears, and the next candidate in every stage stops at its out-edge with a "blocked: send" badge while the counter stays high. A second Primes call stacks a second frozen chain below, and the counter doubles. A thin done rail runs under all boxes; in the fix scene, closing it lights every stage's "case <-done" tag in the same frame, all boxes exit together, and the counter falls to baseline. The graph uses the existing CSS variables for light and dark, wraps or scales to the pane, and turns token travel into discrete steps under prefers-reduced-motion. No state depends on hover.

**Scenes.**
1. **Splice: the chain grows** — generate emits 2, 3, 4, 5…; Primes receives 2 and splices filter(2), then 3 gives filter(3), then 5 gives filter(5). Each filter discards its multiples. The learner sees that depth is dynamic and equals primes found + 1, and that Primes itself builds one goroutine per prime.
2. **Return and abandon (default, scaffold)** — Primes takes its n-th prime and returns. The tray disappears. In every stage the next candidate hangs on its out-edge with a 'blocked: send' badge. The goroutine counter stays at n+1 and never falls. Pressing 'Call Primes again' stacks another complete frozen chain, so the count climbs by n+1 per call. This is the scene a learner lands on.
3. **One done, one close (fix, gated)** — Primes makes one done channel and gives the same one to generate and every filter. After the last prime, defer close(done) fires. The done rail turns solid, every '<-done' case becomes ready in the same frame, all boxes exit together, and the counter drops to baseline. No stage needs to know the chain length or shut down in order.
4. **The wrong fix that hangs** — A drain-first fix waits for the chain to finish. Every stage is still blocked on its out-edge and nobody reads. A 2 s deadline badge, matching primesTimeout, fires and the test line turns red with 'did not return'. The learner sees why teardown cannot wait for a pipeline that can never send.

**Controls.** Play / Pause; Step (one event at a time; the default under prefers-reduced-motion); Restart; Next prime — splice one more filter stage; Let Primes return — the reader walks away; Call Primes again — repeat the call up to 5 times to accumulate the leak; Close done — fix scene only, disabled until the Solution tab is revealed; Try the drain-first fix — opens the deadlock scene.

**Code anchors.**
- 34-prime-sieve/main.go:97 — ch := generate(nil): the nil that disables every stage's done case
- 34-prime-sieve/main.go:103 — ch = filter(nil, ch, prime): the same nil passed to every spliced stage
- 34-prime-sieve/main.go:48-52 and 73-77 — the or-done selects that are correct but can never fire
- 34-prime-sieve/main.go:101 — prime := <-ch, the receive that stops when Primes returns
- 34-prime-sieve/main.go:106 — return primes while n+1 stages still run
- solutions/34-prime-sieve.md:78-79 — done := make(chan struct{}); defer close(done)
- solutions/34-prime-sieve.md:81 and 87 — generate(done) and filter(done, ch, prime), one channel shared by the whole chain
- solutions/34-prime-sieve.md:43-45 — nil channel in a select is never ready
- 34-prime-sieve/check_test.go:71, 89-108 — numGoroutinesToSettle and TestPrimesDoesNotLeakGoroutines, the counter the visualisation mirrors
- 34-prime-sieve/check_test.go:24, 28-42 — primesTimeout (2 s) and primesWithTimeout, the deadline the deadlock scene shows

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Spoiler safety is not honest as designed. The placement is problem-inline, but scene 3 is the fix and the controls are labelled 'Close done' and 'Try the drain-first fix'; even disabled, those labels hand the learner the answer. DocPane's `revealed` flag is local state (web/src/components/DocPane.tsx:17), and ExerciseView is keyed only by exercise id (web/src/App.tsx:77), so `revealed` survives a switch from Solution back to Problem. A gate wired to it would unlock the fix scene inside the Problem tab. A structural split by tab is the only safe gate.
- The horizontal chain does not fit the pane. styles.css:506 lets the doc pane shrink to 260px, and `.doc-body` pads 22px on each side (styles.css:544), leaving about 216px of content. generate + filter(2) + filter(3) + filter(5) + reader at readable text size is wider than that. The proposal's 'wrap or scale' either breaks the left-to-right metaphor or makes labels illegible. Use a vertical stage stack: one stage per row, done rail on the left.
- The deadlock scene conflates two different wrong fixes. 'Every stage is still blocked on its out-edge and nobody reads' fits only a WaitGroup-style wait for the stages to exit without closing done. A real drain loop does read, so the stages are not blocked; it hangs because generate is infinite (34-prime-sieve/main.go:47, `for i := 2; ; i++`). Pick one mechanism and label it precisely; as written the scene is internally inconsistent.
- The 'Close done' control teaches the wrong call shape. The solution uses `defer close(done)` (solutions/34-prime-sieve.md:79), which fires when Primes returns, not a manual close. Show a 'Let Primes return' step in the fix scene, or learners may add a bare close() instead of the defer.
- It cannot replace the README diagrams. tools/check/lint.go:257 requires an ASCII diagram, and 34 is not in the exceptions map (lint.go:39-56). Removing README.md:7-9 or 28-39 would fail lint. The widget is additive; the two ASCII diagrams stay as the clone/GitHub equivalent.
- The goroutine counter must be labelled as scripted, not measured. runtime.NumGoroutine() works in the browser runner, and the HEAD scaffold's failing test already prints the real delta (verified by running HEAD's files: 13 to 64 after Primes(50); 64 to 219 after five calls). A hard-coded '51' impersonates a measurement. Show 'chain goroutines: k of n+1' and say the number is scripted.
- No n is pinned, so timeline length and the counter disagree with the README. n=50 produces too many events to step through and a counter of 51; a small n produces a different counter. Pin n (7 or 10), collapse the tail as '... xk more', and annotate 'n=50 gives 51' in the caption.
- Effort is understated. A shared timeline engine, four scenes, tab-scoped gating, a vertical responsive layout, reduced motion, keyboard controls, a lazy chunk, and a drift guard is L, not M. This would also be the first visualisation in web/src; a one-off becomes a permanent maintenance cost across 43 exercises.
- Seven controls plus a scene selector in a 216px column will wrap to several rows above the README and push the exercise text down. Use Play/Pause, Step, Restart, and a two-scene selector scoped to the current tab.

**Why this tier.** Easy; two ASCII diagrams exist and the browser prints the goroutine delta.

<a id="38-settle-once"></a>
#### 38 — Settle Once: CompareAndSwap Between a Timeout and a Completion
`Tier D` · **nice** · goroutine lanes · family `lanes` · effort M · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Atomic check-and-flip: exactly-once settle between two independent writers, with CompareAndSwap and no lock or retry loop.

**Core idea.** Two goroutines can both read a shared flag before either writes it, so a plain check-then-set lets both call notify. One CompareAndSwapInt32 makes "am I first?" and "install settled" a single indivisible decision, so exactly one caller wins and the loser returns false at once.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The interleaving itself: no test message prints the two-step read-then-write window, and no run shows the losing goroutine's timing. The blocking difference that motivates CAS over sync.Once is also invisible: a losing Do() waits on Once's mutex until the winner's notify returns, while a failed CAS returns immediately. The browser runner hides both further: GOMAXPROCS=1, and -race is unavailable.

**Design.** A narrow scene with two lanes, Complete and Timeout, feeding one shared Responder card. The card holds a single bit, settled, and shows pending (0) or settled (1); below it a notification strip lists every notify call. A tick line runs left to right. In the scaffold run, each lane emits two separate tokens, a read of settled and later a write of settled = true, with a shaded gap between them. When the ticks interleave, both reads return false, both writes flip the card, and the strip collects "completed: result" and "timed out". In the CAS run, each lane's read and write are one solid block, CompareAndSwapInt32(&settled, 0, 1), that cannot be split; the first block to reach the card flips it, and the second fails, stops at a red "return false" marker, and never touches the strip. A third mode slows the winner's notify into a 300 ms bar: with sync.Once the losing lane piles up at a gate and crosses only when the bar empties, while with CAS it crosses at once. Use the site's tokens: --accent for the winning lane, --fail for the double notify, --fg-muted for the unchanged state, --border for lane rails. At 320 px the lanes stack vertically, the card sits between them, and the notification strip stays a single scrolling line.

**Scenes.**
1. **Scaffold: read, read, write, write** — Two lanes against one shared bool. Complete and Timeout both read settled as false, then both write true and call notify. The notification strip shows two outcomes for one request and both lanes return true. The shaded read-to-write gap marks the race window.
2. **CAS: one indivisible step** — The same two lanes, but each check-and-flip is one block. The lane that reaches the card first swaps 0 to 1 and notifies; the second fails the swap, returns false immediately, and never touches the strip, which holds exactly one outcome. A caption says there is no retry loop because the new value is always 1.
3. **Why not sync.Once: the loser waits** — The winner's notify is a slow 300 ms bar. In sync.Once mode the losing Timeout call sits at Once's gate until the bar empties, then returns false late; in CAS mode the same call returns false at once. A timer beside each losing call makes the wait difference explicit.
4. **Why the demo prints 0/30** — The contender count switches from 2 to 200 against one Responder, all released together. With two, the interleaving often misses and the strip stays at one entry; with 200, many contenders enter the window and the strip overflows. The scene says that one clean run proves nothing and that the test uses 200 contenders per round for this reason.

**Controls.** Interleave: force both lanes to read before either writes, versus run them one after the other. Shows the bug needs an order, not just a shared variable.; Implementation: plain bool / sync.Once / CompareAndSwap. Makes the three answers comparable in one scene.; Slow notify (300 ms): stretches the winner's side effect so the sync.Once loser's wait becomes visible.; Contenders: 2 / 200, released together. Shows how the test widens a nanosecond window into a reliable failure.; Play / Pause / Step / Restart, keyboard-operable. Step is the reduced-motion default and the only control that needs no timing..

**Code anchors.**
- 38-settle-once/main.go:80-83: Responder's settled bool field
- 38-settle-once/main.go:95-101: Complete, with the read at line 96, the write at 99, and notify at 100
- 38-settle-once/main.go:108-114: Timeout, with the same three steps at lines 109, 112, 113
- 38-settle-once/main.go:140-141: the demo's one Complete goroutine and one Timeout goroutine per request
- 38-settle-once/main.go:145-148: the notifications != 1 check that counts double notifies
- 38-settle-once/check_test.go:TestSettleRaceExactlyOneWinner: 200 contenders per round released from a closed start channel, 500 rounds
- 38-settle-once/check_test.go:TestLoserDoesNotBlockOnWinner: 500 ms winner notify against a 150 ms loser deadline
- solutions/38-settle-once.md:60-80: settled int32 and atomic.CompareAndSwapInt32(&r.settled, 0, 1), on the banded lines 67 and 75

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Verdict overstates: the README already draws the exact read-read-write-write interleaving as an ASCII diagram at 38-settle-once/README.md:22-26, and the scaffold comment already prescribes the answer ('CompareAndSwap idiom ... won := CAS(&settled, 0, 1)') at 38-settle-once/main.go:41-48 and explains sync.Once's blocking loser at main.go:50-56. Every idea in the four scenes already exists in text; the visualisation adds interactivity, not missing information. 'high' -> 'nice'.
- Empirical correction that also breaks scene 4: I ran `node web/scripts/smoke-test.ts --only 38` on HEAD data (confirmed byte-identical to git HEAD). TestSettleRaceExactlyOneWinner PASSES in the browser runner even on the scaffold, because GOMAXPROCS=1 serialises the contenders; the only scaffold failure is the static AST check TestMainUsesCompareAndSwap (check_test.go:242). So the proposed scene 4 ('with 200, many contenders enter the window and the strip overflows') would contradict the learner's own test output. Rework it to explain why the bug is invisible here, or drop it.
- Scene 4 is infeasible as drawn at 320 px: 200 contender lanes cannot be rendered. The design says 'the strip overflows' with no aggregation strategy. Needed: a crowd counter or a handful of representative tokens, not 200 lanes.
- Lazy-loading is asserted, not designed. The hard constraint requires per-exercise lazy loading, but no code-splitting mechanism is given. Needed: a static registry id -> () => import(...) plus React.lazy/Suspense, loaded only when the tab is opened.
- The third tab must be conditional. DocPane.tsx:26-32 renders a fixed two-tab list and router.ts:9-18 only knows problem|solution. Showing a third tab for all 43 exercises is wrong. Use a static map in web/src keyed by exercise id (avoid tools/gen/main.go, which only ships .go/.md/go.mod) and render the tab only when an entry exists; parseRoute must map an unknown segment back to problem.
- Annotation line anchors will rot. The design hard-codes main.go:96,99,100 and 109,112,113 plus solution lines 67,75. Keep labels keyed to event kinds/symbols in one module, not spread as literals. Also correct the solution reference: the banded lines {7,15} in solutions/38-settle-once.md resolve to the two CompareAndSwapInt32 calls (file lines 67 and 75), not the `settled int32` field (line 63).
- Accessibility: --accent/--fail alone to encode win/fail is color-only. Pair it with text or icons. In the scaffold scene BOTH lanes win, so styling one lane as 'the winning lane' with --accent is misleading; reserve the win/fail distinction for the CAS scene. Ensure play/pause/step/restart are real <button>s with a visible focus ring (existing pattern at styles.css:213) and that the notify strip does not need hover.
- Reduced motion is under-specified. The 300 ms bar and any autoplay need a matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)') gate in JS; the existing reduce block (styles.css:1067) only disables two unrelated transitions. Step must fully replace autoplay, and the sync.Once timing scene must render as static bars in that mode.
- Width: the doc pane minimum is 260px (styles.css:506) and at <=900px it becomes full width (styles.css:986+). Target a 320px viewport, which leaves ~288px of content after doc-body padding. Stack the two lanes vertically and wrap the notify strip; no horizontal scroll.
- The sync.Once scene must not imply every losing Do takes the internal mutex: Once's fast path is an atomic load, and only the first loser takes doSlow. Keep that caveat in the caption, as the proposal's risks note.
- check_test.go's own comment says '100 simultaneous contenders' (line 69) while the constant is 200 (line 76). If the widget quotes a number, use 200 (and README says 'hundreds').

**Why this tier.** The scaffold comment gives CAS; the browser passes the race test. A caption, not an animation.

<a id="39-load-shedding"></a>
#### 39 — Load Shedding: Reject Fast Instead of Blocking the Hot Path
`Tier D` · **nice** · capacity gauge · family `gauge` · effort S · README diagram: yes

**Pattern.** Bounded worker pool with non-blocking admission control: a fixed-size job channel where Submit uses select+default to shed load instead of blocking on a full buffer.

**Core idea.** A bounded queue has a hard ceiling: capacity slots plus the jobs currently running in workers. At that ceiling the caller must choose to wait or to reject. On a hot path it must reject in the same instant and let the caller drop the work, because every millisecond spent waiting for a metric slot is stolen from the real request.

**What prose and tests cannot show.** The failure is a latency stall, not a wrong answer or a deadlock. The workers keep draining, so the naive send does return eventually, and every test except the deadline test passes. The learner cannot see the capacity ceiling (capacity plus one job in flight per worker, main.go:101 NewQueue(2, 4)), which Submit attempt crosses it (the test's own comment calls it racy), or that the wait is unbounded and comes out of the caller's request time. Nothing in the prose shows the instant of saturation or the deliberate contrast with exercise 18, where blocking is the correct answer.

**Design.** Show a horizontal hot path: a request (the real work) with a caller goroutine attached, calling Submit at a gate; behind the gate a bounded channel drawn as N discrete slots, then a worker lane with one lane per worker running one job at a time, and a Result channel back to the caller. The default mode is the scaffold. Under light load the gate opens instantly, a slot fills, a worker picks the job up, runs it, sends the result, and the caller resumes, all quick and quiet. Then raise the load: the slots fill, each worker is shown holding a job, the gauge reads full. The next Submit reaches the gate and gets stuck mid-send: a pulsing "blocked" marker with an elapsed-time counter climbing, and the request behind it visibly frozen. The workers keep draining (one slot frees, one drained job leaves) to make clear this is not a deadlock, only an unbounded wait the caller cannot budget for. In the second mode the same saturated frame makes Submit snap back at t=0 with ErrOverloaded, the caller records "dropped" and proceeds, and the request is never slowed. A final comparison frame puts 18's backpressure beside 39: same bounded channel and pool, but in 18 the producer is supposed to wait, in 39 the caller has somewhere better to go. Everything is scripted and deterministic; time is a synthetic clock, labelled as compressed.

**Scenes.**
1. **Room to spare** — Light load. Submit puts a job into a free slot and returns at once; a worker dequeues it, runs it, sends the result, and the caller resumes. Both the scaffold and the fixed version behave the same here, because the channel is not full, so this scene is spoiler-free and establishes the normal flow.
2. **Hot path stalls (default)** — Saturation. All capacity slots are full and every worker is holding a job. The next Submit sits mid-send with a pulsing blocked marker and a rising elapsed-time counter; the request behind it is frozen. Workers keep draining to show it is a stall, not a deadlock: something does free up, but the caller cannot know how long.
3. **Fail fast, keep serving** — The same saturated frame in fail-fast mode: Submit returns ErrOverloaded in the same instant (t=0), the caller logs the dropped job and the request proceeds unslowed. Reachable only after an explicit reveal, so the default view never shows the fix.
4. **Same shape, opposite answer** — Exercise 18 beside 39. Both are a bounded channel feeding a fixed worker pool. In 18 the producer is the pipeline and waiting is the intended backpressure; in 39 the caller is unrelated request work and waiting is the bug. A toggle switches which policy the same gauge exercises, so the learner sees that the mechanism is identical and only the caller's intent differs.

**Controls.** Play / Pause / Step / Restart, all keyboard operable, with a visible step counter so reduced-motion users get the same lesson one event at a time; Policy toggle: Blocking (scaffold) vs Fail fast (fixed), placed behind the same reveal as the solution so the default run is the scaffold; Workers stepper (1-3) and Capacity stepper (0-3): shows the ceiling is capacity plus in-flight workers, and that the scaffold only fails once both are full; Job duration (fast / slow): a slow Run makes saturation reachable and makes the stall visibly long; Load: number of concurrent callers, or submit rate, to reach saturation on demand; An 'elapsed wait' readout on the blocked caller, to quantify the cost a hot path cannot afford; 'Compare with 18 (backpressure)' toggle that reuses the same gauge under the opposite policy.

**Code anchors.**
- main.go:96 q.jobs <- job (the scaffold's blocking send; the stall)
- main.go:95-98 Submit's signature and doc comment must never block the caller
- main.go:58 var ErrOverloaded
- main.go:69-71 Queue struct { jobs chan Job }
- main.go:75-83 NewQueue: make(chan Job, capacity) sets the slot count; loop spawns workers
- main.go:85-89 worker: for job := range q.jobs { job.Result <- job.Run() } (drains, proving not a deadlock)
- main.go:101 NewQueue(2, 4) and main.go:104-118 the 20 concurrent callers
- main.go:119-121 err != nil -> fmt.Printf("job %d dropped")
- solution: select { case q.jobs <- job: return nil; default: return ErrOverloaded }
- check_test.go TestSubmitFailsFastWhenQueueIsFull: workers=1, capacity=2, 200ms per-call deadline, and its comment that the ceiling is capacity plus one in flight per worker
- README.md today/goal fenced diagram (the static version this replaces in the browser)

**Verifier corrections — read before building.**
- Verdict tier is overstated. The proposal's own reason concedes that the scaffold is easy, that the failing test names the symptom, and that the README gives the idiom. check_test.go:98-99 names select/default in its failure message, and README.md:57-70 hands over the hint. The visual adds timing, not a new fact. 'nice' fits; 'high' does not.
- The proposal says the test's own comment calls the rejected Submit racy (learner_blind_spot, risk 3). check_test.go:87-90 says the opposite: 'Generous margin over the true ceiling ... so this doesn't depend on pinning that exact bookkeeping.' Do not attribute a racy-submit warning to the test.
- The proposal calls the README diagram 'the static version this replaces in the browser'. Nothing replaces it. DocPane.tsx:53-54 renders README.md from HEAD, and the readme-diagram lint rule (AGENTS.md) requires an ASCII diagram. The visual is additive, so it must add something the diagram lacks, not redraw the decision flow.
- Almost all of the proposed content is already in the ungated Problem tab. README.md:10-21 draws today vs goal, README.md:23-29 states the stall and 'not a deadlock', and README.md:46-55 contains the full 18-vs-39 contrast. The proposed spoiler gate therefore protects little; the only genuinely new content is the elapsed-wait experience.
- A third tab needs shell changes. router.ts:9-18 maps only 'problem' and 'solution'; DocPane.tsx:26-32 renders two tab links; ExerciseView.tsx:10 and App.tsx:86 carry the same union type. At 320 CSS px the doc-tabs row already holds Problem, Solution, a spacer, a difficulty badge, and an optional 'passed' badge (DocPane.tsx:27-39). .doc-tabs (styles.css:540-555) does not wrap.
- A third tab is not behind the spoiler. DocPane.tsx:17 keeps `revealed` as local state. A shared /<id>/visual URL mounts with revealed=false, so the proposal's 'same reveal as the solution' needs the state lifted and the route made reveal-aware. Direct shares of the visual URL would otherwise depend on component defaults for spoiler safety.
- spoiler_safe is not true as drafted. The annotations list includes `default: return ErrOverloaded` and the 18 comparison. If annotations render before the reveal, the fix is visible. The proposal says it never renders the solved source, but annotation 2 is the solved source.
- The proposal says the widget 'reads from git HEAD via the generator'. It cannot. web/tools/gen/main.go:484-487 copies only .go, .md and go.mod, and no visualization hook exists. A hand-written TS module under web/src is not derived from HEAD and can drift from main.go:101 `NewQueue(2, 4)` and check_test.go:66-68 (workers=1, capacity=2). Keep the widget parameter-driven and do not print the exercise's exact numbers as if derived.
- The width floor is 260px, not 320px. styles.css:506 sets `minmax(260px, var(--doc-frac, 40%))`. The proposed caller lanes plus gauge plus worker lanes will crowd at 260-320px. The proposal lists this only as a risk; it is a layout requirement.
- Scene 4 misstates exercise 18. 18's scaffold is a slice (18-bounded-pipeline-backpressure/main.go:54-63), not a bounded channel. 'Both are a bounded channel feeding a fixed pool' is true only of 18's solved state. The scene must label it '18 once fixed', or it teaches a false premise.
- Effort 'S' is too low. Four scenes, seven controls, a deterministic event script, keyboard operability, reduced motion, theme variables, and a lazy chunk are M work. The low estimate helps justify the overstated 'high' verdict.
- The 'unbounded wait' is bounded by the job duration in any concrete run. A fixed job duration makes the stall look measurable. If the lesson is 'the caller cannot budget for it', the widget must state that the job duration is unknown to the caller, not show a fixed number.

**Why this tier.** Easy; the README gives select/default; the visual adds a millisecond stall.
